# CHAPTER 4 — ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS

## Introduction: From Lists to Systems

Root-cause analysis of KP's insecurity too often degenerates into lists: poverty, madrasas, the Afghan war, bad governance, Pashtun marginalization, "foreign hands." Lists are analytically useless because they cannot discriminate among drivers that (a) *create* conflict, (b) *trigger* it, (c) *accelerate* it, and (d) merely *correlate* with it. This chapter advances a four-layer model — **structural conditions, proximate triggers, accelerants, and sustaining mechanisms** — and maps the familiar catalogue of causes into it. The model's analytical payoff appears in the discussion of Kurram, Swat and the NMDs, where the same nominal cause (sectarianism, militancy, tribal conservatism) plays different roles in each layer.

The chapter's central argument is that KP's conflict is best explained by **institutional exclusion operating on an armed, networked periphery, within a geopolitically open borderland**. Grievance provides the *recruitment narrative*; sanctuary and weapons supply the *capability*; war economy and factional politics supply the *duration*; and the absence of a just, resourced, locally legitimate governance offer supplies the *recurrence*.

## 4.1 Political Marginalization and Governance Deficits

**The long exclusion.** For seventy years, FATA's residents were excluded from ordinary courts, taxation-as-citizenship, provincial legislation, police protection, and local government. The 25th Amendment ended the legal exclusion; the *practical* exclusion persists in measurable forms: the merged districts have the province's lowest human development indicators (SDPI 2025), the weakest road connectivity, the thinnest health infrastructure, and a judicial presence that remains skeletal (Chapters 5 and 10). Meanwhile, the settled districts of KP have experienced their own variant of exclusion: provincial governments have had limited control over security policy; federal agencies — the military, the Interior Ministry, NACTA — dominate counter-terrorism decision-making; and fiscal autonomy is constrained by the province's dependence on federal transfers and by withheld payments (the war-on-terror dues dispute is a running grievance; CM Afridi publicly cited delays in releasing constitutional dues in 2025–26) (Wikipedia 2026e).

**Measuring the governance deficit.** Several indicators triangulate. (i) *Local government*: KP held local elections in two phases in 2021–22, but the devolution of powers, funds and functions under the KP Local Government Act has been repeatedly delayed; in the NMDs, local councils remain largely absent, and the promised "transition from Political Agents to Deputy Commissioners" produced an administration with formal authority but no functional capacity to deliver services. (ii) *Justice*: district courts in the NMDs were established belatedly and unevenly; the Peshawar High Court has repeatedly intervened on the non-implementation of merged-district reforms. (iii) *Service delivery*: school closures lasting months in Kurram, Bajaur, Khyber, Mohmand and North Waziristan have been documented; health facilities and their staff are frequently attacked or withdrawn (EUAA 2026). (iv) *Citizen experience*: surveys and journalistic accounts consistently describe a state experienced as predatory (checkpoints, night raids, collective fines) rather than protective — a perception that militants exploit ("the state takes your boys; we give them dignity and a purpose").

**The mechanism.** Political marginalization does not produce violence directly. It produces *low-cost recruitment, low-information environments, and high legitimacy costs for the state*. Where citizens cannot seek justice, they seek protection; where they cannot seek protection, they accept the protection offered by armed actors, even at a cost. ICG's 2026 report captures the reciprocal dynamic: many KP residents "reject jihadism but also resent the military's heavy-handed tactics," creating a legitimacy space that counter-insurgency cannot enlist and militancy can inhabit (ICG 2026).

## 4.2 Socio-Economic Deprivation: Poverty, Unemployment and Inequality

**The macro trend.** KP's poverty rate rose from 28.7 per cent to 35.3 per cent in the FY2024-25 estimates — the second-highest provincial rate after Balochistan's 47 per cent, and well above Punjab's 23.3 per cent (Planning Ministry 2026; Economic Survey 2025-26). National poverty reached 28.9 per cent, and inequality rose to its highest level in 27 years (Gini-equivalent inequality index 32.7 nationally, 29.4 in KP). The drivers identified by the Planning Ministry include IMF-programme fiscal consolidation, energy tariff adjustments, inflation, disaster losses and weak growth — a policy mix that, as the World Bank's provincial estimates also suggest, pressed hardest on the poorer provinces (KP Youth Policy 2026).

**Labour market.** National unemployment reached 7.1 per cent (5.9 million people) in the FY2024-25 estimates, the highest in 21 years; youth unemployment (15–24) is substantially higher, and KP's educated youth are disproportionately affected: PIDE's March 2025 assessment found 31 per cent of educated youth jobless, with women constituting 51 per cent of the unemployed (KP Youth Policy 2026). The scale of the jobs deficit is illustrated by a single statistic: in mid-2025, 866,000 candidates applied for 16,454 teaching vacancies in KP — more than 52 applicants per post. In the NMDs, the combined effect of displacement, destroyed enterprises, and missing banking and market infrastructure means formal employment is rare and informal work is fragile.

**Inequality and spatial disparity.** KP's internal inequality is as important as its position relative to other provinces. The Hazara and Peshawar divisions concentrate industry, services and education; the southern districts and NMDs have lower literacy, worse health outcomes and thinner infrastructure. SDPI's district-level Human Development Index series shows KP's overall HDI falling from 0.650 to 0.623 (2019–2023), with only two districts improving on score while 27 declined (SDPI 2025).

**Mechanism and caution.** The mechanism connecting deprivation to militancy is *relative*, not absolute: it is the combination of rising expectations, unemployable education, and visible inequality — not merely poverty — that produces the "nothing to lose but dignity to gain" recruitment pool. The caution is equally important: **poverty does not make people violent**. Most poor residents of KP never join armed groups; many resist them. The empirical claim this study supports is narrower and defensible: socio-economic deprivation *lowers the cost* of recruitment and *raises the value* of the material benefits (salary, status, protection) that armed groups can offer, particularly for young men in the NMDs and southern belt.

## 4.3 Educational Deficiencies and the Madrasa Ecosystem

**Formal education.** KP's literacy rate stands at 51 per cent (with a wide gender gap — 37 per cent female versus 65 per cent male), below the national 60 per cent and Punjab's 66 per cent (Economic Survey 2024-25; KP Government 2024). The province has the second-highest number of out-of-school children, and learning outcomes are weak even among enrolled students. In the NMDs the disparities are stark: only 37 per cent of girls attend primary school and ~5 per cent reach secondary level (KP Youth Policy 2026). School infrastructure gaps, teacher absenteeism, insecurity-driven closures, and cultural constraints on girls' education interact.

**The madrasa ecosystem.** Pakistan's madrasa sector is estimated at roughly 30,000–35,000 institutions (with counts varying by registration method), teaching between two and four million students, with the largest concentrations in Punjab, KP and the former FATA. The sector is organized into five umbrella *wafaq* boards (Wafaq-ul-Madaris Al-Arabia — Deobandi; Tanzeem-ul-Madaris — Barelvi; Wafaq-ul-Madaris Al-Salafia — Ahl-e-Hadith; Rabita-tul-Madaris — Shia; and Ittehad Tanzeemat-ul-Madaris — a coordinating body) plus thousands of unaffiliated institutions. In KP and the tribal belt, madrasas have historically performed three functions: (i) free education and boarding for poor families (a genuine welfare function); (ii) ideological formation, including for jihadist networks; and (iii) social mobility where state education is absent or weak.

**The regulation saga.** Pakistan's attempts to regulate the sector have a specific and illuminating history: the 2019 agreement under which madrasas registered with the Ministry of Education (with roughly 18,000 registering) was reversed in October 2024, when the JUI-F secured legislation (the Societies Registration (Amendment) Act) allowing madrasas to register as charitable societies under a colonial-era law with minimal oversight of curricula, funding or foreign students (NYT 2025; Adaamir 2024). President Zardari raised international-compliance concerns (FATF); the government proposed re-registration under the education ministry; and the Ittehad Tanzeemat-e-Madaris has demanded gazette notification of the 2024 law. In early 2026, Punjab reached an agreement with the wafaq boards on *online* registration with the provincial charity commission — a model KP has not replicated at scale (The Nation 2026b). The practical result: after two decades of declared intent, a substantial share of madrasas in KP operate outside effective oversight of funding, curricula or foreign students.

**Assessment.** The evidence does not support the crude claim that madrasas are terrorist nurseries. It supports three more precise claims: (a) a subset of madrasas has historically provided recruitment, ideological socialization and logistics for armed groups (the Haqqania and Kandahar-circle lineages being the best-known examples, as well as the TNSM network in Malakand); (b) unregulated madrasas receiving foreign funding and enrolling foreign students present specific risks (Chapter 9); and (c) the *opportunity cost* of an education system that produces neither employable skills nor critical literacy is a structural driver of vulnerability to radicalization, regardless of institution type. Regulation without financing and quality reform will remain a paper exercise; conversely, a serious reform offer — curriculum modernization plus teacher training plus a transition path for graduates plus facility funding — is one of the highest-leverage interventions available (Chapters 8, 15).

## 4.4 Tribal Social Structures, Honour Codes and Conflict Resolution Traditions

**Social organization.** Pashtun social structure in the frontier belt — *qawm* (descent-and-solidarity group), *jirga* (assembly), *melmastia* (hospitality), *nanawatai* (asylum/refuge-seeking), *badal* (reciprocal justice/vendetta), *ghairat* (honour), *tor* (customary law applied through the jirga) — supplied both the governance system that the colonial state chose to work through and the conflict-resolution system that communities relied on when the state was absent (Ahmed 1983; Barth 1959; Banerjee 2000).

**The double-edged legacies.** These institutions are neither inherently violent nor inherently peaceful; they are conflict-management technologies with known pathologies. On the constructive side: jirgas have repeatedly ended violent cycles (the 2011 Kurram truce after ~2,000 deaths; the 2025 14-point agreement; Bajaur's tribal jirgas disavowing shelter to militants in 2025; the Tirah tribal committees negotiating return corridors). On the destructive side: (a) customary *riwaj* (custom) has been used to legitimize collective punishment, forced marriage and the exclusion of women from inheritance; (b) the honour/vendetta complex provides escalation structures that sectarian and militant entrepreneurs can *capture* — the Kurram conflict is the paradigmatic case; (c) jirga verdicts cannot be enforced without state backing and therefore fail against armed actors; and (d) the merger's dismantling of the malik system removed intermediaries who, whatever their faults, had brokered between state and society.

**The Malik and intermediaries: decline and consequences.** The malik system — state-supported tribal notables — was systematically destroyed by the militants between 2005 and 2014 (thousands of maliks and elders were assassinated or driven out; the 2008–2013 period saw the killing of prominent tribal leaders across the NMDs, including attacks on jirga meetings that killed dozens) and then sidelined by the merger's abolition of the Political Agent's patronage structures. The result is an *intermediary vacuum*: old intermediaries discredited or dead; elected local representatives (where they exist) without fiscal or police authority; and new "peace committee" figures who are vulnerable and often unelected. The vacuum is filled by the militants' own intermediaries (the local commanders, "spokesmen", and tax collectors) and by vigilante figures. Rebuilding legitimate intermediaries — through elected local government with real budgets, plus credible, protected elder representation in dispute resolution — is a core requirement of normalization that policy has not yet embraced (Chapter 15).

## 4.5 Religious Extremism and Ideological Radicalization

**Theological-political lineages.** KP and the tribal belt host several distinct radical traditions that are often conflated in policy discourse: (i) *Deobandi-jihadist* networks (the Haqqania/Peshawar *jihadi* milieu; TNSM; TTP's ideological core), which draw on a scholarly tradition that has also produced anti-militant clerics; (ii) *Salafi-jihadist* currents (ISKP and its local sympathizers, historically concentrated in Bajaur, Mohmand and parts of Kurram), ideologically hostile to Deobandis and Sufis; (iii) the *TNSM/Barelvi-jihadi* hybrid of Malakand, whose moral-reform program captured Swat's local grievances; (iv) *sectarian anti-Shia* networks (LeJ/SSP lineages), which overlap with both (i) and (ii); and (v) *apolitical Deobandi and Barelvi conservatism*, which supplies the ecosystem of seminaries, mosques, and moral policing in which radicalization incubates — often without active support for violence.

**What actually radicalizes?** The KP evidence suggests a *pathway model* with four interacting elements: (a) *grievance narratives* (state violence, humiliation at checkpoints, drone casualties, prison abuse, anti-Muslim foreign policy); (b) *identity and honour* (defence of community, protection of honour, revenge for kin); (c) *network recruitment* (family, madrasa classmates, village commanders, online contacts); and (d) *material incentives* (wages, status, protection, escape from unemployment). Radicalization is socially embedded: recruits generally join networks they already trust. Preventing terrorism therefore depends as much on disrupting *networks* and offering *alternatives* as on defeating ideas in the abstract.

**The state's own role.** Pakistan's religious policy has been contradictory: it has used religious discourse for state legitimation (laws on blasphemy, the constitutional declaration of Islam as the state religion, and the state's historic sponsorship of jihad in Afghanistan and Kashmir) while periodically attempting to roll back extremism. Critics, including within Pakistani scholarly and human rights communities, argue this inconsistency — employing extremists tactically, then fighting them later — produced the Frankenstein dynamic repeatedly invoked in the literature. This study treats that critique as a *hypothesis about incentives*, supported by specific historical episodes (Kashmir-focused groups' differential treatment; the 2004–2008 deals; Hafiz Gul Bahadur's earlier status as a state-aligned militia), while noting that it does not fully explain the current insurgency's scale and autonomy.

## 4.6 Historical Grievances and the Legacy of Colonial Frontier Policies

Three colonial legacies are structurally causal.

**First, legal dualism.** The FCR created two classes of Pakistani citizenship in effect for seven decades. Its structures — collective responsibility, no appeal, punitive demolition, and the Political Agent's fiat — are remembered; the 2024–2026 persistence of FCR-style practices (reported detentions under "40 FCR" joint-liability logic) shows the legacy is not merely memory (Haq 2024; GRR Journal 2024).

**Second, the Durand Line's non-recognition.** Afghanistan's refusal to accept the boundary (and successive Afghan governments' support, at least rhetorically, for Pashtun self-determination) supplied both a diplomatic irritant and a sanctuary framework. Pakistan's counter-moves — strategic depth, support to Afghan Islamists, and cross-border intervention — embedded KP in the conflict dynamics of a neighbouring state (Chapter 9).

**Third, the "good tribal" bargains.** Purchased loyalties (allowances, local militias, and tolerance of smuggling) created a frontier political economy whose collapse — when allowances were suspended or when the state tried to disarm militias after 2001 — destabilized precisely the actors the state had relied on. The trajectory of Hafiz Gul Bahadur (from a state-tolerated North Waziristan commander to a principal insurgent) illustrates the pattern of client-to-antagonist conversion.

## 4.7 Geopolitical Exploitation: Proxy Wars and External Interference

**The layers of geopolitics.** (i) *The Afghan layer*: the sanctuary granted to the TTP by factions within the Afghan Taliban (and the Taliban's denial of control); the ISKP sanctuary in Afghanistan; and the period 2021–2026 in which Pakistan's leverage over Kabul collapsed (Chapters 2, 9). (ii) *The India layer*: Pakistan's longstanding allegation that India's intelligence services support Baloch and (occasionally) TTP-linked militancy; India's denial; the issue recurs in Pakistani crisis discourse (and was invoked during the 2025 escalation). Independent verification is rare, and this study treats the claim as an allegation contested by the party accused rather than an established fact. (iii) *The Iran layer*: Iranian interests in Shia protection in Kurram and its regional competition with Pakistan over Afghanistan; Zainebiyoun-linked networks in the border belt. (iv) *The China layer*: CPEC's security risk premium; Chinese pressure on Pakistan (and, conversely, Chinese mediation between Islamabad and Kabul in 2026); Chinese interest in mineral development in the NMDs. (v) *The US/global layer*: residual counter-terrorism interests, the post-withdrawal loss of intelligence and ISR support that Pakistan had enjoyed during the Zarb-e-Azb era, the Afghanistan-specific visa and resettlement pipeline, and FATF-linked regulatory pressure.

**Mechanism: opportunity, not instruction.** The analytically defensible position is that geopolitics chiefly supplies *opportunity structures* — sanctuaries, weapons, funding, diplomatic cover — rather than directly *commanding* KP's conflict. Pakistan's own history of using non-state actors in Afghanistan and Kashmir establishes that the instrument has been used by multiple states in the region; the current insurgency is supplied, however, by a genuinely autonomous domestic-ideological core and by the Afghan sanctuary, not solely by foreign instruction.

## 4.8 Weak State Writ and Institutional Fragility

**The composite problem.** A state's ability to provide security depends on the interaction of force, intelligence, justice, revenue and legitimacy. In KP each element is deficient in a distinct way: force is concentrated in the military with police overloaded; intelligence is fragmented (military intelligence; ISI; IB; CTD; district police; Special Branch) and the KP CTD suffers resource and personnel gaps (19 per cent vacancy in sanctioned posts in 2026; equipment shortfalls acknowledged even in official data); justice is slow (ATCs are clogged; 2,273 anti-terror cases pending as of November 2024; 657 death sentences awarded between 2019 and 2022 with no executions during that period, per the NAP review literature); revenue is hostage to federal transfers; and legitimacy is contested by both the PTM-aligned rights movement and religious-political parties (Chapters 7, 11).

**Policing the periphery: the NMD's unresolved model.** The absorption of the Khasadar and Levies forces into the KP Police (2019 legislation; a force of roughly 28,000 personnel split between ~15,600 Levies and ~12,000–15,000 Khasadars, with figures reported variously) produced an institution with three incompatible inheritances: the Levies' light border-policing doctrine; the Khasadar's communal, unpaid, and locally embedded model; and the KP Police's urban-focused, standardized force structure. The result is documented: inadequate training and equipment; personnel "posted" to the NMDs from the settled districts who are unaccountable to local communities; and local officers without the authority or protection to act. This is the single most specific institutional gap in KP's security architecture (Chapter 7).

**The civil–military disconnect.** ICG's 2026 report (94 interviews, Sept 2025–Feb 2026) documents that the military conducts counter-insurgency with limited coordination with provincial police; that army commanders shape internal-security policy while the police bear the losses; and that provincial authorities have criticized both the conduct of operations and the withheld resources (ICG 2026; Dawn 2024a). This disconnect is not an accident of personalities; it is structural — the provincial police depend on the province for budget and on the centre for strategy, with no joint command-and-accountability mechanism.

## 4.9 Ungoverned Spaces and Geographic Terrain

**The terrain issue.** KP's mountainous border zones — Tirah's high pastures, the Shawal and Razmak ranges, the Bajaur and Mohmand hills, and the Kurram's passes — are not "uninhabited"; they are *thinly governed and seasonally transited*. Militants use escarpment sanctuaries, caves, seasonal pasture routes and cross-border valleys; state forces face mobility, visibility and communications constraints; and the climate (snow, fog) determines the tempo of violence (winter lulls; spring escalations — hence the TTP's "spring offensive" framing).

**Ungoverned space is relational.** It is more useful to think in terms of a *governance gradient* than a binary. In KP, space grades from fully governed (Peshawar's urban core) to partially governed (southern settled districts, where state presence is contested at night) to thinly governed (Tirah's upper valleys, south Waziristan's border strips) to non-governed (sanctuary pockets in Afghanistan). Policy must therefore specify the mechanism by which each gradient segment is to be moved — and must not assume that "clearing" a district translates into a governance outcome (Chapter 6).

## 4.10 The Drug Economy and War-Economy Dynamics

**Structure of the war economy.** KP's conflict economy has six revenue streams: (i) smuggling and trade diversion (fuel, consumer goods, vehicles, livestock, currency); (ii) extortion and "taxation" of commerce and transport; (iii) narcotics transit and local retail (with links to the broader Afghan opiate and synthetic-drug economy); (iv) mining and natural resource rents (marble, chromite, coal, antimony, and sand/gravel), including illegal mining and the coercion of contractors; (v) kidnapping for ransom and, occasionally, hostage-taking; and (vi) "project rents" — the capture of public works contracts, employment and NGO/government projects in conflict districts. A seventh, less-discussed stream is the revenue from *security contracting itself*: private armed escorts for transport, mining, and development work, and informal taxation by militias and police checkpoints alike.

**Greed, grievance and the war economy's true role.** The Collier–Hoeffler framework helps explain why the conflict has lasted, not why it began. KP's insurgency did not arise from lootable resources, and its fighters were not principally profit-seekers. But once armed actors are established, the war economy: (a) funds operations more reliably than donations; (b) generates private constituencies for conflict (smugglers, contractors, checkpoint officials, militia commanders) whose interests exist independently of ideology; (c) corrupts the state institutions and private actors with which it interacts (documented in multiple cases of police and customs complicity in smuggling routes); and (d) makes *peace* costly for specific actors — a fact any settlement must confront by providing transition pathways (Chapter 15).

**The state's smuggling crackdowns.** The 2025–26 anti-smuggling drives (and the creation of provincial and federal task forces; the curbs on "non-customs paid" vehicles and fuel) coincided with — and were partly justified by — the TTP's reliance on smuggling rents. But they also deepened the economic crisis of the border communities, including the tens of thousands who depend on the trade. Normalization policy must be explicit about this trade-off: convert the border economy rather than merely repress it (border markets, legal crossings, tariff-based trade, and employment substitution).

## 4.11 Identity Politics, Pashtun Nationalism and the PTM Movement

**The Pashtun question.** Pashtun political identity has been mobilized in three distinct registers since 1947: the *nationalist* register (Pakhtunistan, the ANP and allied parties); the *religious-political* register (JUI-F and Deobandi political networks); and, since 2014, the *rights/human-rights* register of the Pashtun Tahafuz Movement (PTM), which began as the Mahsud Tahafuz Movement (May 2014) demanding de-mining and compensation after Waziristan's operations, and grew into a national movement after its 2018 renaming. The PTM's demands are specific and documented: de-mining; freedom of movement and removal of checkpoints; accountability for extrajudicial killings and enforced disappearances; a truth and reconciliation process; and the release of detainees (PTM 2018; ICG 2022; Aaj 2024).

**The ban and its consequences.** On 6 October 2024 — days before a scheduled "Pashtun National Jirga" — the federal government proscribed the PTM under Section 11B of the Anti-Terrorism Act, citing "activities prejudicial to peace and security," "anti-national narratives," and "anarchy" (The News 2024; The Hindu 2024; Business Recorder 2024). The KP government then froze its activities; Section 144 was imposed to block a Khyber district gathering; and clashes between police and PTM supporters followed (October 2024). Since then, human rights organizations have documented continuing restrictions on Pashtun civil society, including detentions of PTM-affiliated activists without charge (HRW/Amnesty/HRCP reporting, cited in Countercurrents 2026). Simultaneously, the movement's leadership has faced repeated prosecutions (Ali Wazir, Manzoor Pashteen) and bans on entry (Balochistan's 90-day prohibition in August 2024).

**Analytical assessment.** The PTM ban is a case study in the counterproductive dynamics of securitization. The movement has, in fact, been non-violent (its signature tactic is the sit-in and the "peace march" preceded by a peace jirga), and its demands — de-mining, accountability for disappearances, safe mobility — overlap with the practical requirements of any normalization strategy. By criminalizing it under the anti-terrorism law, the state: (i) removed a channel for non-violent grievance articulation precisely as the insurgency escalated; (ii) conflated peaceful critics with armed actors, weakening the state's claim to distinguish between them; (iii) alienated parts of the anti-militant constituency whose cooperation the counter-insurgency needs; and (iv) shifted the movement's surviving activism to informal, less accountable forms. The counter-argument — that the movement's rhetoric is inflammatory, that some figures have made irresponsible statements, and that its mobilization intersects with ethnocentric narratives that the TTP also seeks to exploit — has some basis but does not justify terrorism-law proscription of a non-violent movement.

## 4.12 Intersectionality of Root Causes: A Systems-Thinking Perspective

**The four-layer model.** Layering the causes produces the study's diagnostic framework:

**(1) Structural conditions** (make the system vulnerable; change slowly): legal exceptionalism and its legacy; geographic proximity to a collapsing borderland; the absence of industrial employment and formal education quality; sectarian-Muslim communal geography; low state legitimacy; and Pakistan's own use of non-state actors as strategic instruments.
**(2) Proximate triggers** (set episodes in motion): operations and their displacement effects; drone strikes; assassinations of elders; land disputes (Kurram); electoral and representational shocks (FATA's representation and its subsequent disappointment); negotiated truces that fail; and the 2021 Afghan regime change.
**(3) Accelerants** (increase the scale and speed of violence): the Afghanistan sanctuary; weapons surplus; drone and IED technology diffusion; the Islamic State's provocation and sectarian competition; factional splits (JuA 2026) and umbrella competition; online radicalization; and fiscal shocks (poverty rising 28.7→35.3 per cent in KP).
**(4) Sustaining mechanisms** (keep violence going even when causes persist elsewhere): war economy; weak justice; police–military bifurcation; displaced-person grievance; the intermediary vacuum; and the credibility deficit of state promises (merger funding, compensation, return guarantees).

**Intersectionality in practice: three mini-cases.** In *Kurram*, a structurally old sectarian-land conflict was triggered by a convoy attack, accelerated by armed-group competition and cross-border sanctuary, and sustained by a weak justice system and closed roads. In *Swat*, structural factors were comparatively benign (high literacy, tourism economy), which is why the 2007–2009 insurgency required the TNSM's ideology plus state collapse of presence; the 2025–26 resurgence is driven primarily by the *accelerants* (sanctuary, factional expansion, technology) rather than by revived local structural causes (NSBRRI 2026). In *North Waziristan*, all four layers are active simultaneously, which explains why it has been the single most violent district almost continuously since 2004 — and why a military operation alone (Zarb-e-Azb) could not end violence there without the other layers being addressed.

**Policy implication.** Interventions targeted at a single layer fail predictably. Kinetic operations address triggers but not sustaining mechanisms; development projects address structural conditions but not triggers; deradicalization addresses accelerants in part; and only the simultaneous treatment of all four layers — sequenced by what each can realistically achieve in what time — produces movement toward the normalization thresholds defined in Chapter 1. This is the analytical foundation of the roadmap in Chapter 15.

---

| Structural | Madrasa/education deficits | ~18,000 registered pre-2024; reversal post-2024 | Medium-High |

# CHAPTER 5 — THE FATA MERGER AND ITS SECURITY IMPLICATIONS

## 5.1 Historical Context of FATA's Special Constitutional Status

The Federally Administered Tribal Areas comprised, on the eve of the merger: seven agencies (Bajaur, Mohmand, Khyber, Orakzai, Kurram, North Waziristan and South Waziristan) and six Frontier Regions (FR Peshawar, FR Kohat, FR Bannu, FR D.I. Khan, FR Lakki Marwat and FR Tank), covering 27,220 km² with a population of approximately five million (2017 census). FATA's constitutional status was sui generis: governed directly by the federal executive through the Governor of KP as agent to the President; administered by Political Agents; regulated by the Frontier Crimes Regulations 1901 (Article 247 of the 1973 Constitution preserved the arrangement); represented in the National Assembly and Senate but not in the provincial assembly; and excluded from the jurisdiction of the superior judiciary, from ordinary criminal and civil law, and from the federal taxation and development framework in any normal sense.

**The institutions of exception.** Four institutions defined FATA's political life. (i) *The Political Agent* — combining executive, judicial, revenue and intelligence functions, accountable to the federal government and largely unaccountable to residents. (ii) *The FCR* — permitting collective punishment (including the notorious "40 FCR" joint-liability clause under which entire communities could be fined, blockaded, or have property seized for individual offences); excluding appeal and lawyers' representation; and empowering the Political Agent to order detention and demolition. (iii) *The maliki system* — the network of state-recognized tribal notables receiving allowances and performing brokerage functions (including raising lashkars and providing intelligence), whose fate under militancy would shape the post-merger order. (iv) *The jirga* — recognized as a dispute-resolution and, in practice, criminal-adjudication mechanism, with the state's authority mediated through it. Alongside these sat the *Khasadar* (paid tribal constabulary, locally recruited, lightly armed) and *Levies* (a more regularized frontier force) — forces of the frontier, not of the ordinary citizen.

**Consequences of the exception.** Empirical assessments of FATA's condition in 2018 were unambiguous. The region had: the lowest literacy rates in Pakistan (with female literacy in the low single digits in parts of the belt); the worst health indicators (few functioning hospitals; high maternal and infant mortality); the fewest kilometres of paved road per capita; minimal electricity; no banking in most areas; and no systems for property registration, which would later bedevil the merger's land and mineral governance. The FCR also produced specific human rights pathologies documented over decades: arbitrary detention, collective punishment, exclusion of women's rights under customary practice, and, in the post-2001 period, the use of FATA as the primary theatre of war (Chapter 6). It is against this baseline that the merger's promises — and its shortfalls — must be judged.

## 5.2 The FATA Reforms Committee and the Road to the 25th Amendment

**The committee.** The FATA Reforms Committee, constituted in 2015 and chaired by the Adviser to the Prime Minister on Foreign Affairs, Sartaj Aziz, was mandated to propose a durable governance solution. Its 2016 report presented options — (a) full merger with KP; (b) maintaining the status quo with reform; (c) creating a separate province/administrative unit; (d) phased integration — and recommended: **full merger with KP**, to be implemented over time; the **discontinuation of the FCR**; the extension of the superior judiciary and of provincial services; a **transitional "FATA Development Authority"**; and — decisively for fiscal politics — a **3 per cent share of the National Finance Commission divisible pool** for ten years to bring the region to parity. The committee reported having consulted in depth with more than 3,000 local elders/jirga representatives, with a clear majority favoring merger (FATA Reforms Committee 2016; Dawn 2016).

**Political contestation.** The reform process was contested across three axes. First, *within the tribal areas*: some elders opposed merger as the loss of a distinctive identity and of the maliki entitlements; youth and reformist networks (later heavily associated with the PTM) supported it as the end of civic exclusion. Second, *between the centre and KP*: the federal government, having administered FATA directly, would transfer both responsibility and cost to Peshawar; KP's government (then PTI-led) supported merger as a historic expansion of the province's territory and representation. Third, *among political parties*: JUI-F, whose networks were strongest in FATA, contested the terms; ANP and PTI competed for credit; and the Pashtun-nationalist current was itself divided.

**The 25th Amendment (May 2018).** On the committee's recommendation, Parliament enacted the 25th Constitutional Amendment, merging FATA and the Frontier Regions into Khyber Pakhtunkhwa; repealing Article 247; extending the constitution, the superior courts' jurisdiction and provincial law to the merged districts; providing 23 general seats in the KP Assembly (bringing it to 145, of which 16 are reserved seats and 4 for non-Muslims); and guaranteeing representation in the National Assembly (12 NA seats) — a step that also increased KP's share of national legislative seats. The amendment passed with overwhelming cross-party support — a rarity in Pakistan's fractious politics — and was widely celebrated as the most significant constitutional reform since the 18th Amendment.

**The fiscal promise.** Attached to the merger through parliamentary resolutions and committee recommendations was the commitment to allocate 3 per cent of the divisible pool — approximately Rs 90–90+ billion annually by some early estimates, rising as revenues grew, and amounting, by later accounting, to a cumulative expectation of roughly Rs 700 billion over the first seven years (The News 2026). This commitment was framed as the *quid pro quo* for integration: without it, the merged districts would remain the poorest in the federation and merger would be a transfer of legal labels rather than of resources.

## 5.3 The Merger Process: Legal, Administrative and Security Dimensions

**Legal transition.** The legal extension required: (i) the replacement of the FCR with the ordinary criminal and civil law of KP; (ii) the creation of district courts and prosecution services in the seven districts; (iii) the extension of the KP Police Act 2017 and the absorption of the Levies and Khasadar forces; (iv) the application of provincial revenue, excise, education, health and local government law; and (v) the dismantling of the Political Agent system in favor of Deputy Commissioners — the administrative emblem of the merger.

**Administrative transition.** Deputy Commissioners replaced Political Agents; "Tribal District" administrations were established; and provincial departments extended (schools, health, agriculture, forests, roads). In practice, transition occurred on a spectrum: Khyber, Bajaur, Mohmand and Kurram continued to host large military/FC deployments and the writ of civilian authority remained thin; the Frontier Regions were absorbed into adjacent settled districts (Peshawar, Kohat, Bannu, D.I. Khan, Lakki Marwat, Tank), creating a patchwork of district boundaries; and the province's *administrative* capacity to supervise seven new districts was limited by finance and by the security environment.

**Security transition.** The most consequential and least resolved dimension. In June 2019 the KP government promulgated Levies and Khasadar ordinances (subsequently legislated in September 2019), bringing approximately 28,000 personnel of the two forces under police command: the Levies (about 15,673 personnel against 16,053 sanctioned posts before transition) as an "essential service," and the Khasadars — whose legal basis had been executive orders, not statute — brought under police powers. The KP government committed to recruiting about 6,000 additional police for the merged districts (in phases; the first tranche of several hundred posts proposed in 2019), and to establishing district police structures. The transition, however, produced a hybrid: large numbers of locally recruited, lightly trained personnel integrated into a force whose doctrine, discipline and accountability systems were built for settled districts; command from distant district police officers; and local legitimacy deficits because the "new" police were sometimes seen as the old Khasadars under a new label — or, worse, as outsiders. The 2019 legislation itself passed amid opposition protest and Levies/Khasadar demonstrations outside the assembly, an early indicator that personnel were being managed as a *fiscal and employment* problem rather than a security-capability problem.

**Representative transition.** The first provincial elections in the merged districts (2019, in the 16 NA/23 PK seats) enfranchised the tribal population in provincial politics for the first time. PTI and independent candidates performed strongly; subsequent 2024 general elections confirmed PTI's organizational dominance in the belt. But representation without fiscal authority or local government produced a "seats without services" outcome: MPs and MPAs could advocate, but the administrative and financial machinery to implement remained absent.

## 5.4 Governance Challenges Post-Merger: Institutional Vacuum and Capacity Gaps

**The vacuum.** Independent assessments five to eight years after the merger converge on a set of findings. The GRR Journal's 2024 study of post-merger "administrative chaos" concluded that the transformation had been implemented hastily, without adequate planning or capacity-building; that the promised administrative structures remained incomplete; and that "the merger's promises have not been fulfilled, and the new administrative structure still struggles to gain public trust." The ASPIRE-KP symposium (2025–26) found deficient funding and unpreparedness; the failure to post capable personnel; weak coordination between federal and provincial governments; incomplete devolution to local government; and the resurgence of TTP violence aggravating all of the above (GRR Journal 2024; ASPIRE-KP 2025).

**Specific governance deficits.**
1. **Local government**: The merger's implicit promise of local self-government for the tribal districts was not implemented at scale; the KP Local Government Act's application to the NMDs is partial, and provincial governments have repeatedly deferred local elections and (where elected) local budgets and functions.
2. **Land and minerals**: FATA's customary land tenure and the absence of cadastral records created a legal vacuum; the province's efforts to regularize mining (marble, chromite, coal) generated disputes over who owns and who benefits — with reports of provincial-federal contestation and of interest from foreign investors, including Chinese and (reportedly) US firms, over critical minerals, amid unresolved ownership rules (ResearchGate/GRR assessments 2025–26).
3. **Civil service capacity**: The merged districts are not attractive postings; allowances are limited; security constraints reduce mobility; and provincial departments have filled only a fraction of sanctioned posts.
4. **Revenue**: With no tax base and limited provincial fiscal space, the districts depend on transfers — which are delayed, contested, and shrinking as a share of need.
5. **Coordination**: The federal government retains significant roles (SAFRON; federal PSDP projects; the FC's security role), while the province holds administrative responsibility — producing the "two masters" problem noted in the literature.

**The FCR's afterlife.** Perhaps the most damaging finding of the post-merger assessments is that FCR-style practices survived the FCR. Reports documented detentions and punishments under the logic of collective responsibility (the "40 FCR" clause) even after the regulation's repeal; and there are credible accounts of punitive demolitions, curfews, and collective fines. This is not legal formalism: a community that experiences the "new" law as the old exception under a different name learns to distrust the merger itself.

## 5.5 Security Force Transition: From Frontier Corps to Police and Levies

**Structure of forces in the merged districts (2026).** Four security actors operate: (1) the **Pakistan Army and Frontier Corps (FC North)**, holding border positions, conducting operations, and providing the *de facto* security backbone; (2) the **KP Police**, with district structures, CTD presence and the absorbed Levies/Khasadar personnel; (3) **local militias/peace committees/lashkars**, partially armed and paid, operating with state encouragement in some districts; and (4) **Federal Constabulary**-type units deployed for specific tasks (protests, campaigns, patrols), which suffered 124 killed and 244 injured in 2025 according to the KP CTD's own data.

**Capability assessment.** Independent and parliamentary assessments identify nine recurring problems: (i) personnel shortfalls against sanctioned strength (with the CTD at roughly 81 per cent of sanctioned posts province-wide in 2026 and the NMD forces worse); (ii) equipment deficits (the official claim that the entire CTD has only nine M4 rifles and eleven night-vision devices — contested, but indicative of the debate over resourcing — was made by a federal coordinator in September 2026); (iii) insufficient training for counter-insurgency roles (police are trained for law-and-order and investigation, not for platoon-level combat); (iv) absence of protected mobility and counter-IED capacity (routine APC losses); (v) delayed salaries/compensation and inadequate life insurance, which undermines morale; (vi) command and control fragmentation (DIGs, DPOs, FC, army sectors); (vii) intelligence-sharing gaps between the military and police (ICG 2026); (viii) the double burden of counter-terrorism and ordinary policing; and (ix) "insider" risk from locally recruited personnel coerced by militants.

**The core dilemma.** Two models compete. *Model A* (police-led): a well-resourced provincial police, trained, equipped and accountable, assuming primacy in the merged districts with federal paramilitary support at the border — the model implied by the merger's logic and demanded by provincial politicians. *Model B* (military-led): continued army/FC primacy in counter-insurgency, with police relegated to static protection and investigation — the de facto status quo (ICG 2026). Each has a coherent rationale; what fails is the *combination* — military primacy without police protection and without a transfer-of-authority pathway. The study's assessment (Chapter 7, Chapter 15) is that a *sequenced* Model A, beginning with protection and intelligence fusion and progressing to police primacy in districts as capability thresholds are met, offers the only pathway consistent with both the merger's promises and counter-insurgency doctrine — but it requires resources currently not committed.

## 5.6 Judicial System Extension to Merged Districts

**What was supposed to happen.** The 25th Amendment extended the jurisdiction of the Peshawar High Court and the Supreme Court to the merged districts, abolished the FCR's appellate exclusions, and contemplated the establishment of district courts, prosecution services, and the extension of the KP judicial system.

**What happened.** The record indicates partial, slow and shallow extension. District and sessions courts were established in the seven districts, but with deficiencies in: physical infrastructure (court buildings, residential accommodation, secure transport); judicial officers (shortfalls, and reluctance to serve in insecure areas); prosecution capacity; police investigation quality (the binding constraint on conviction); legal aid; and family/consumer/anti-corruption jurisdiction. The Anti-Terrorism Act applies, but ATC capacity is province-wide and congested (Chapter 7). Two further complications are specific to the merged districts: (a) the persistence of informal justice (*jirga*, *riwaj*) both as a service that communities prefer (speed, accessibility, local legitimacy) and as a system that departs from constitutional rights (collective punishment, exclusion of women, coercive enforcement); and (b) the coexistence of *qisas/diyat* and other Islamic-law provisions with statutory law in a context where communities expect the *jirga*'s restorative logic. Independent scholarship documents the "skeletal" state of the formal system and the continued functioning of hybrid orders (Haq 2024; GRR Journal 2024).

**Why it matters for security.** In security terms, the justice gap has three consequences: (i) *impunity* — militants and their facilitators are rarely convicted, so the coercive message of law is weak; (ii) *grievance* — citizens detained under security laws without trial, or fined collectively, feel the state's coercion without its protection; and (iii) *substitution* — communities turn to armed actors or tribal militias for dispute resolution, which entrenches the very structures that sustain conflict (Chapter 4). The Peshawar High Court's sustained attention to merged-district rights litigation (including the NFC shortfall and Sehat Card cases) is one of the few institutional counterweights documented in 2025–26.

## 5.7 Development and Rehabilitation in Merged Areas

**The promise and the balance sheet.** At merger, financing for the merged districts was to come through three channels: (i) the **3 per cent divisible-pool commitment** (via the NFC framework), approximately Rs 700 billion cumulatively over the first seven years; (ii) the **Accelerated Implementation Programme (AIP)**; and (iii) annual federal and provincial PSDP allocations. The actual delivery, as documented in a July 2026 account by a former KP finance secretary and in PHC proceedings: **Rs 132 billion delivered against Rs 700 billion expected — a Rs 568 billion shortfall**; no new NFC award since 2010 (the 7th NFC Award predates the merger); a promised National Assembly resolution endorsement (September 2021) of a 3 per cent allocation (which then-CM and others estimated at up to nearly Rs 1 trillion annually in future terms) never implemented; and no provision in the FY2026-27 budget for the roughly Rs 192 billion a year owed. The commitment expires in 2028 (The News 2026; Tribune 2025d; PHC reporting 2025–26).

**Consequences for services.** The shortfall is directly visible: incomplete health facilities and closures; teacher shortages and school closures (compounded by insecurity-driven closures in Kurram, Bajaur, Khyber, Mohmand and North Waziristan); the suspension of Sehat Card health entitlement coverage for tribal districts at one point (litigated); stalled road and electricity projects; and the inability to fund the ~6,000 new police posts promised. Provincial fiscal stress is compounded by the KP government's claim to withheld federal dues.

**The political economy of the shortfall.** Two structural causes stand out. First, the **NFC structure**: provincial shares are determined by an award that requires consensus of all provinces, and no province has agreed to fund the merged districts' 3 per cent out of its own share. The commitment therefore functions as an unfunded federal mandate. Second, **budgetary politics**: the federal government's fiscal consolidation under the IMF programme (FY2026-27) constrained discretionary development spending; and in 2026 all provinces reportedly agreed that FBR collection above the previous year's base would accrue to the federation — a formula that further squeezes the merged districts.

**Assessment.** The fiscal shortfall is the single most damaging failure of the merger, because it converts a promised *peace dividend* into its opposite: a demonstration that state promises are unreliable. It is also the most fixable: the instruments exist (a presidential order under Article 160; a dedicated statutory fund; ring-fenced AIP allocations; a legislated floor for merged-district spending). What is required is political will — and this study treats the fiscal settlement of the merged districts as the central near-term test of normalization seriousness (Chapter 15).

## 5.8 Community Perceptions and Political Dynamics Post-Merger

**Perceptions.** The available evidence — academic studies, civil society consultations, and journalistic accounts — indicates a trajectory from *hope* (2018) to *disappointment* (2020–22) to *anger and fear* (2023–26). Survey research in the immediate post-merger period found overwhelming support for merger (one study reported 92 per cent support among educated respondents) alongside pessimism about implementation. Later assessments describe the prevailing view as: legal integration is welcome; economic integration has failed; security has deteriorated; and the state's promises (compensation, jobs, return guarantees) are not credible. ASPIRE-KP's civil-society forum recorded particular frustration with delayed fund disbursement, and the "neglect by the KP government" in ensuring robust governance mechanisms — a criticism that cuts across party lines.

**Political dynamics.** Three trends are visible. First, PTI's dominance of the merged districts (2019, 2024 elections) gives the belt a distinct political voice — but also places it at the centre of the federal-PTI conflict, with consequences for service delivery and for the tone of security policy. Second, the *malik system's* collapse left the old patronage politics without an institutional successor; the elected representatives have no local fiscal apparatus; and new intermediary figures (peace-committee chairs, contractors, militia commanders) have emerged in an unregulated fashion. Third, the *youth* cohort (the majority in the NMDs) is simultaneously the most integrationist and the most alienated: it demands rights, mobility, and jobs, and it is the segment most receptive both to rights movements (including the proscribed PTM's earlier mobilizations) and, at the margins, to militancy's offers. The 2026 Tirah displacement episode — 33,800 families registered, return windows negotiated for October 2026, and provincial pushback against federal characterizations of the displacement — illustrates the volatility of the politics of return (Countercurrents 2026; EUAA 2026).

## 5.9 Cross-Border Security Management Along the Durand Line

**The fence and the crossings.** Pakistan's border-management program along the KP-facing section of the Durand Line produced: a fence along the great majority of the KP/NMD frontier (largely completed by 2021–23); formal crossing terminals at Torkham, Ghulam Khan and Angoor Adda; and border forts and surveillance systems. The program has documented effects: reduced large-scale infiltration; greater state capacity to detect movement; and increased friction with Afghanistan (whose government rejects the fence and has fired on its construction and on border posts).

**Recurrent closure as policy.** Border closures — including the prolonged closure of Torkham in 2025 over a border-post dispute, and repeated closures during the 2025–26 hostilities — have become a default instrument. Their costs are borne by border communities: loss of trade and labour income, price spikes, stranded patients and students, and separation of kin. Their security benefits are episodic: the CRSS data showed a measurable decline in attacks after the October 2025 closure, followed by resumption (CRSS 2025b). Closures thus function as a *policy of harm to the population to influence the state*, and their repeated use erodes the border communities' stake in the state.

**Assessment.** Border management along the Durand Line requires three elements that Pakistan has not yet assembled: (a) *legality and predictability* — a border regime (permits, trade facilities, movement corridors) that is stable and lawful rather than reactive; (b) *intelligence-led interception* rather than blanket closure; and (c) *diplomatic framework* — a modus vivendi with Kabul on border management, acknowledging that Afghanistan's refusal to recognize the line is a durable condition that must be managed, not wished away (Chapter 9).

## 5.10 Assessment: Has the Merger Contributed to or Complicated Security Normalization?

**Verdict.** The merger has been *constitutionally successful, administratively partial, fiscally failing and—on security—ambiguous*. Six findings support this assessment.

**First, juridical integration is real.** Four million-plus citizens gained the protections (in principle), rights and representation they lacked; the superior courts' jurisdiction is a genuine remedy; and the 23-seat provincial allocation altered KP's politics. The merger's counterfactual — the continuation of the FCR under 2026 conditions — is worse.

**Second, fiscal non-delivery has converted a promise into a grievance.** The Rs 568 billion shortfall is not an accounting detail; it is the difference between an integration narrative and a betrayal narrative in the districts where the insurgency recruits.

[[FIG:fig4_fiscal.png|Figure 4. The merger's fiscal shortfall against annual conflict losses. Sources: The News (July 2026); Business Recorder (June 2026); Chapter 10 estimates. See Statistical Annex, Table D.1.]]

**Third, security transition without capability transfer has left the police exposed and unreformed.** The merged districts remain *de facto* military-led, while the police absorb casualties without the means to protect themselves — a combination that satisfies neither doctrine nor legitimacy.

**Fourth, the justice vacuum has preserved the very practices (collective responsibility, informal enforcement) the merger was meant to end.**

**Fifth, the merger has *complicated* the security picture in three specific ways**: (i) it created new expectations and new political actors with claims on the state, precisely as violence resurged; (ii) it transferred administrative responsibility without transferring resources; and (iii) it coincided with — and is sometimes blamed for — the TTP's re-infiltration, even though the causal driver was the Afghan sanctuary (Chapter 9).

**Sixth, and most importantly for policy, the merger remains the correct framework.** The failures are failures of implementation, not of the design principle. The alternative — reversion to exception, or partial reversion under security cover — would repeat the historical error that produced the insurgency.

**Consequent design requirements** (developed in Chapter 15): a statutory, ring-fenced merged districts fund with a mandated floor and audit; completion of district court and prosecution capacity with a reform of informal justice that preserves legitimacy; a staged police-capability transfer with protection-first sequencing; local government with budgets; land and mineral regularization with benefit-sharing; and a public, measurable *Normalization Compact* against which progress is reported annually.


# CHAPTER 6 — MILITARY OPERATIONS AND COUNTER-TERRORISM CAMPAIGNS

## 6.1 Overview of Major Military Operations in KPK and FATA (Comprehensive Timeline)

Pakistan's counter-insurgency in KP and the tribal areas has been conducted through an unusually large number of named operations — a fact of analytical significance in itself, since each name implies a distinct campaign and each has often been followed by another within a few years. The following timeline distinguishes *major manoeuvre operations* (division-scale, sustained, displacing populations) from *sector operations* (brigade/battalion-scale, district-specific) and from *standing campaigns* (national frameworks under which operations and intelligence-based raids continue).

| Operation | Period | Area | Stated objective | Displacement (est.) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Al Mizan | 2002–2006 | Waziristan | Clear foreign fighters; dismantle al-Qaeda infrastructure | Localized |
| Zalzala | 2008 | South Waziristan (Mehsud areas) | Pressurize Baitullah Mehsud | Tens of thousands |
| Sherdil | Aug 2008 onward | Bajaur | Break militant control of Bajaur | ~200,000–300,000 |
| Rah-e-Haq I & II | Nov 2007; 2008 | Swat/Malakand | Clear TNSM/TTP presence | Hundreds of thousands |
| Rah-e-Rast | May–Jun 2009 | Swat, Buner, Dir, Shangla | Defeat insurgency; restore state authority | 2–2.5 million |
| Rah-e-Nijat | Oct–Dec 2009 | South Waziristan | Destroy TTP's main base | ~300,000+ |
| Brekhna | 2011–12 | Mohmand | Clear border sanctuaries | Tens of thousands |
| Khyber I–III | 2014–15 | Khyber (Bara, Tirah) | Clear LEI/TTP infrastructure | Hundreds of thousands |
| Zarb-e-Azb | Jun 2014–Jul 2017 | North Waziristan (later others) | Eliminate militant sanctuaries across FATA | ~1 million |
| Khyber IV | Jul–Aug 2017 | Rajgal Valley (Khyber) | Seal high-altitude crossing routes | Localized |
| Radd-ul-Fasaad | Feb 2017–present | Nationwide | Consolidate; dismantle residual networks | Localized |
| Azm-e-Istehkam | Jun 2024–present | Nationwide (KP/Balochistan focus) | "Reinvigorated" CT campaign; kinetic + diplomatic | Anti-displacement framing |
| Sarbakaf | Jul–Aug 2025 | Bajaur (Lowi Mamund) | Clear re-established TTP presence | ~100,000 (relief agencies) |
| Ghazab-lil-Haq | Feb 2026–present | Afghanistan (cross-border) | Punish/coerce Afghan Taliban over TTP sanctuary | Cross-border refugee flows |

Sources: ISPR statements as reported (Dawn, The News, Tribune 2008–2026); Wikipedia operational summaries (2026a, 2026d); Paradigm Shift 2025; CRSS 2025b; UN/OCHA displacement figures as cited.

**Analytical observation.** The timeline reveals a repeating structural pattern: operations are launched when violence becomes politically intolerable; they achieve temporary clearing; the state consolidates with *some* development spending; violence returns within 4–8 years; and a new operation is named. The pattern is not evidence that force fails, but that force *alone* is a depreciating asset.

## 6.2 Operation Rah-e-Haq (Swat, 2007–2009): Objectives, Execution and Outcomes

**Context.** The Swat insurgency developed from the TNSM's moral-reform campaign (2007) into an armed movement controlling much of the valley by 2008, with Maulana Fazlullah's FM broadcasts, attacks on barber shops and music shops, the destruction or closure of hundreds of schools (with a documented campaign against girls' education), beheadings of opponents, and attacks on police. Two earlier operations (Rah-e-Haq I in November 2007 and a follow-on in 2008) failed to restore state control: the forces were too few, the political will fluctuated, and each operation was followed by negotiation — including the February 2009 Nizam-e-Adl Regulation, which conceded Sharia-based adjudication in Malakand in return for peace, and collapsed within weeks as militants expanded into Buner and Dir.

**Significance.** Rah-e-Haq's failure is instructive for three reasons. First, it demonstrated that *partial operations followed by concessions* signal weakness and accelerate mobilization — the militants' expansion after the February 2009 deal was rapid. Second, it established the pattern of "operation–peace deal–collapse" that recurs in KP's history (Chapter 2). Third, it created the political conditions — the flight of 2 million people, the Buner crisis, the collapse of public tolerance for the TTP — that made a decisive operation possible in May 2009.

## 6.3 Operation Rah-e-Rast (Swat, 2009): The Liberation of Swat Valley

**Execution.** Launched in early May 2009 after the collapse of the Nizam-e-Adl arrangement, Rah-e-Rast employed approximately 20,000+ troops (with FC and police support) using artillery, helicopter gunships and airstrikes; the operation cleared Mingora (the valley's capital) within two weeks and continued for months into the valley's periphery and adjoining districts. Approximately 2–2.5 million people fled — the largest displacement in Pakistan since 1947 — into camps in Mardan, Swabi, Charsadda and Peshawar, and into host communities.

**Outcomes (assessed).** *Military*: the operation broke the TNSM/TTP's territorial control; Swat's insurgency as an organized force was defeated, and many commanders fled to Afghanistan (Kunar/Nuristan), where they remained. *Political*: the operation was accompanied by a consensus parliamentary resolution and was broadly popular in KP — a critical legitimacy asset that enabled subsequent operations. *Human cost*: hundreds of civilians were killed; displacement produced a humanitarian crisis; and there were credible reports of extrajudicial killings and disappearances. *Reconstruction*: the Malakand recovery, though slow at first, eventually produced rebuilt bazaars, roads, and a remarkable restoration of tourism (Chapter 10), and the "Swat model" became internationally studied as a case of counter-insurgency that combined force with governance, albeit imperfectly.

**Assessment against doctrine.** Rah-e-Rast came closest of any Pakistani operation to population-centric counter-insurgency (Kilcullen 2009; the Walden case study of the campaign finds that governance was deliberately integrated with military action). Its weaknesses were in the *sequencing* of the post-conflict phase: compensation and reconstruction lagged by years; the displaced returned to destroyed infrastructure; and the state did not build institutions capable of preventing re-infiltration across the thirteen years that followed — a failure made manifest in August 2026, when a TTP suicide bomber killed 17 people at a police station in Swat's Kabal area and militants re-entered the valley (Chapter 3).

## 6.4 Operation Rah-e-Nijat (South Waziristan, 2009): Strategy and Impact

**Execution.** Following the Swat operation, the army turned to South Waziristan, launching Rah-e-Nijat in mid-October 2009 with roughly 28,000 troops against the Mehsud heartland, supported by FC and air power. The operation dislodged the TTP's central leadership structure from its base area (Baitullah Mehsud had been killed in August 2009; Hakimullah Mehsud relocated), destroyed infrastructure, and displaced several hundred thousand people (Bannu, D.I. Khan and Tank hosting most).

**Impact and limits.** The military achieved clearing but not holding, in the doctrinal sense: TTP units dispersed into North Waziristan, Orakzai, Kurram, Khyber and Afghanistan; and the pattern of "clear, withdraw, re-infiltrate" was set. The operation also began the construction of the "military-first" model in the tribal areas: FC and army presence in populated areas; curfews and checkpoints; and a dependent local economy — a model whose legitimacy costs would compound over time (ICG 2026).

## 6.5 Operation Zarb-e-Azb (North Waziristan, 2014): The Game-Changer?

**Execution.** Launched in June 2014 after the collapse of the peace process with the TTP and the Karachi airport attack, Zarb-e-Azb targeted North Waziristan's sanctuaries — including the Haqqani network's space (a point of significant US–Pakistan friction, since the operation had been demanded for years and its timing was partly a response to US pressure and to the collapse of negotiations) and those of the TTP and foreign fighters. The operation was notable for: pre-operation warnings to civilians; an announced displacement and de-radicalization plan; sustained duration (formally concluded in July 2017 but effectively continuous); and the scale of forces committed.

**Outcomes.** By official accounts, more than 3,500 militants were killed and hundreds of hideouts destroyed; approximately one million people were displaced (many to Bannu's camps); and the TTP's ability to organize mass-casualty attacks from North Waziristan was substantially degraded. Violence across Pakistan fell markedly in 2015–2018, with 2018–2020 producing the lowest terrorism tolls in over a decade, coinciding with the National Action Plan's instruments (military courts, intelligence operations, counter-financing) and Radd-ul-Fasaad. Militarily, Zarb-e-Azb plus Khyber I–IV was the most consequential campaign in Pakistan's counter-insurgency history.

**Limits and critiques.** Five are documented. First, the operation *pushed* rather than *captured* much of the adversary: many TTP and allied fighters crossed into Afghanistan (Kunar, Nangarhar, Khost), where a sanctuary was available, a fact of decisive importance after 2021 (Chapter 9). Second, *the presence of the Haqqani network* was not decisively addressed, sustaining the argument that Pakistan's counter-terrorism remained selective. Third, *displacement and reconstruction* were poorly sequenced: TDP returns began in 2015 but housing and compensation continued for years; many never fully recovered. Fourth, *the de-radicalization provision* (the promised centres) was never implemented at scale. Fifth, *the operation had no political component*: no negotiations, no political settlement, no institution-building plan for the districts after clearance — precisely the gap Galula's theory identifies as fatal to counter-insurgency.

**Was it a game-changer?** For a period of roughly six years (2015–2021), yes: Pakistan experienced its lowest violence in a decade. But the sustainability test — do the gains hold when pressure is released? — was failed: within four years of the American withdrawal, Pakistan had returned to 2015-level violence, with KP absorbing the majority of it. Zarb-e-Azb changed the *scale* of the threat, not its *structure*.

## 6.6 Operation Radd-ul-Fasaad (2017–Present): Consolidation and Nationwide Approach

**Design.** Launched on 22 February 2017, Radd-ul-Fasaad ("elimination of discord") was announced as a countrywide operation to consolidate Zarb-e-Azb's gains and eliminate the residual terrorist infrastructure, with an explicit "whole-of-nation" framing: military operations, intelligence-based operations, police and Rangers actions, counter-financing, and a call for public cooperation. Unlike previous campaigns, Radd-ul-Fasaad was not a manoeuvre operation with a defined area and timeline but a standing framework under which operations and IBOs continue.

**Outcomes and critiques.** The framework's first years coincided with declining violence. However, from 2021 onward the campaign's limits became visible: it had no capacity to address the sanctuary in Afghanistan, no political mechanism for the FATA merger's fiscal delivery, and no answer to the provincial-federal estrangement that followed the 2022 political crisis. Independent assessments conclude that Radd-ul-Fasaad's document-based "whole-of-nation" design was implemented as a *security-forces-first* program, with negligible investment in the soft components (rehabilitation, education, political inclusion) — the mirror image of its design intention.

## 6.7 Intelligence-Based Operations (IBOs): Precision Strikes and Targeted Operations

**What they are.** IBOs are small-unit, intelligence-driven raids and strikes against specific individuals, cells and infrastructure, conducted by the military (including special forces and aviation), the FC, and provincial CTDs, often in coordination. Since 2017 they have been the dominant modality of counter-terrorism, and in 2025–26 they constitute the bulk of operational reporting: dozens of engagements per month across the NMDs and southern districts, killing hundreds of militants annually (CRSS data record 392 security operations in 2025 killing 2,060 "outlaws" — the highest since 2015, and 80 operations in Q2 2026 alone killing 453).

**Strengths.** IBOs: (a) minimize displacement relative to manoeuvre operations; (b) exploit human and technical intelligence; (c) generate a continuous attrition pressure against commanders; and (d) are politically less costly than announced operations.

**Weaknesses.** (a) *Attrition without endgame*: as 2026 data confirm, killing hundreds of militants per quarter has not reduced attack frequency; the CTD recorded 137 attacks in 2025 despite 320 retaliatory operations and a 50 per cent increase in registered cases; and the third quarter of 2026 saw militant fatalities rise 103 per cent while civilian and security-force deaths rose as well (CRSS 2026c). (b) *Human rights risk*: IBOs conducted with incomplete verification of identity and without judicial process have been associated with civilian casualties, including airstrikes (notably the reported 22 September 2025 Tirah Valley airstrike that killed 30+ civilians, including women and children, and the 13 August 2025 Bajaur mortar incident that killed a woman and her two children). (c) *Local intelligence degradation*: each civilian-harm incident reduces the flow of local intelligence, which IBOs depend upon — a self-limiting dynamic. (d) *Recruitment*: killed militants' funerals and grievances become recruitment events, especially where compensation is absent.

**Assessment.** IBOs are a necessary instrument, not a strategy. Their effectiveness is conditional on (i) high-fidelity intelligence; (ii) avoidance of civilian harm (both for ethics and for effectiveness); (iii) follow-through in investigation/prosecution; and (iv) political accompaniment. Pakistan's IBO record in 2025–26 satisfies (i) partially, (ii) unevenly, (iii) weakly and (iv) not at all.

## 6.8 Assessment of Military Effectiveness: Kinetic Success versus Political and Social Outcomes

**A seven-criteria framework.** The study assesses operations against criteria drawn from counter-insurgency theory and evaluation practice: (1) *Violence reduction* (immediate); (2) *Durability* (measured after 3–5 years); (3) *Territorial control transfer* (to civilian/police authority); (4) *Displacement and recovery*; (5) *Civilian harm and legitimacy*; (6) *Political settlement* (a settlement that addresses the conflict's participants); (7) *Institutional legacy* (security, justice, governance capacity).

| Operation | Violence ↓ | Durability | Transfer | Recovery | Legitimacy | Political settlement | Institutional legacy |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sherdil (2008) | Partial | Low | No | Poor | Mixed | No | Limited |
| Rah-e-Rast (2009) | High | Medium (13 yrs) | Partial | Medium–high (Swat's recovery) | Medium | No | Some (police, tourism) |
| Rah-e-Nijat (2009) | Medium | Low | Low | Poor | Low–medium | No | Limited |
| Khyber I–III (2014–15) | High | Low–medium | Low | Poor | Medium | No | Limited |
| Zarb-e-Azb (2014–17) | High | Medium (6 yrs) | Partial | Medium | Medium | No | Limited–medium |
| Radd-ul-Fasaad (2017–) | Medium | Low | Partial | n/a | Medium | No | Limited |
| Sarbakaf (2025) | Local | Not yet assessable | Low | Poor | Low (protests) | No | Negative (grievance) |
| Azm-e-Istehkam (2024–) | Mixed | Not yet assessable | Low | n/a | Low (KP opposition) | No | Unknown |
| Ghazab-lil-Haq (2026–) | Negative (retaliation) | n/a | n/a | n/a | Contested | No | Interstate escalation |

**Findings.** First, *the correlation between immediate violence reduction and durability is weak*: the operations with the largest immediate effects (Zarb-e-Azb; Rah-e-Nijat) decayed within a half-decade; the one with the best durability (Rah-e-Rast) benefited from an accompanying governance effort and was anyway overtaken by cross-border re-infiltration. Second, *no operation produced a political settlement* — a striking uniformity that reflects the absence of a negotiating framework with the insurgency that is compatible with the state's constitutional order. Third, *civilian harm and legitimacy were inversely related to outcome quality*. Fourth, *no operation transferred security primacy to a capable civilian institution*, meaning that military gains had no custodian.

**Doctrinal reading.** Against Galula: Pakistan's campaigns inverted the political-military ratio, treating politics as an announcement rather than a process. Against Kilcullen: the "accidental guerrilla" mechanism — operations converting neutrals into enemies — is visible in the Kurram convy attack aftermath, Sarbakaf's protests, and the post-2018 deterioration in local intelligence in parts of the NMDs. Against Trinquier: Pakistan has repeatedly practiced a "warfare of reprisal" (collective fines, curfews, demolitions) whose costs fall on the civilians the campaign is meant to protect.

## 6.9 Civilian Casualties, Human Rights Concerns and Accountability

**Data problem.** There is no independent, systematic casualty-tracking mechanism for state operations in KP. Available estimates are partial and contested, including: (a) the *South Asia Terrorism Portal*'s civilian fatality counts for terrorism (not state operations); (b) *Amnesty International*'s statements (June 2025) criticizing rising drone strikes in KP and the failure to protect civilians; (c) *UNAMA*'s Afghanistan-side counts for cross-border strikes (764 civilian casualties between 26 January and 31 March 2026; more than 1,100 civilian deaths/injuries by September 2026, including at least 10 killed where most were children in the 1 October 2026 Kunar/Helmand strikes); (d) *Human Rights Watch, Amnesty and HRCP* documentation of enforced disappearances, extrajudicial killings, and restrictions on civil society in KP, including PTM-affiliated activists held without charge in 2025–26; (e) specific incidents documented by local and international media: the 13 August 2025 Bajaur mortar strike (three civilians killed), the 22 September 2025 Tirah airstrike (30+ civilians reported killed), the killing of civilians in the Orakzai/Arandu operations, and allegations surrounding several IBOs. The *absence* of an official casualty registry is itself a policy finding: accountability mechanisms cannot function without data.

**Legal and normative issues raised by documented practice.** These include: collective punishment (fines on villages; "40 FCR" style measures) contrary to international humanitarian law and Pakistan's own constitutional guarantees post-merger; demolitions of homes and businesses (the November 2025 Surkhab camp demolitions in Balochistan illustrate the practice); prolonged detention without charge; trial of civilians by military courts (the 21st Amendment's military courts and the continuing use of FGCMs for protest-related offences, including against KP residents for the May 2023 episodes, with documented complaints that defendants received no charge sheets or verdict documents and were convicted after appeal deadlines — matters litigated in the Peshawar High Court in 2025); and restrictions on movement and protest. The 27th Amendment (November 2025) further insulated military decision-making from judicial scrutiny (Chapter 11), which will make independent accountability harder.

**The strategic cost of unaccountability.** Counter-insurgency's most valuable non-lethal currency is intelligence from the population. Accountability failures — and their perception — are therefore not merely normative lapses; they are *operational losses*. This is the empirical content of the ICG's finding that KP residents "reject jihadism but resent the military's heavy-handed tactics" (ICG 2026).

## 6.10 The Civil–Military Relations Dynamic in Counter-Terrorism

**The division of labour (de facto).** The military (Army and FC) leads internal-security strategy in the provinces most affected — the National Security Committee, the Army GHQ, corps commands, and the ISPR set policy and conduct operations; the provinces fund police; and the police implement static protection, investigations and casualties. ICG's 2026 report documents three pathologies: (i) *coordination deficits* between military operations and police, including intelligence-sharing gaps that cost lives; (ii) *political externalities*: provincial governments, particularly after 2022, have opposed operations (Azm-e-Istehkam; Sarbakaf), creating a public rift while the police absorb the risk; and (iii) *narrative competition*: federal/military communiqués frame the problem as externally sponsored militancy requiring more force, while provincial statements frame it as the failure of federal policy and the withholding of resources (Wikipedia 2026e; Dawn 2024a; PARADIGM Shift 2025).
