**Constitutional dimension.** The 27th Amendment (passed 11–13 November 2025) formalized a stronger executive-military role: the Chief of Army Staff became Chief of Defence Forces with command over all services, a new National Strategic Command was created, and the top military leadership received lifelong immunity from prosecution, while the Supreme Court's constitutional jurisdiction was transferred to a new executive-appointed Federal Constitutional Court and the Judicial Commission of Pakistan was restructured to give the executive a decisive role (Al Jazeera 2025b; Himal Southasian 2025; TFT 2025). For counter-terrorism governance, the immediate consequences are: (a) reduced judicial oversight of security policy and of military courts; (b) fewer institutional channels for provincial or parliamentary input; and (c) greater reliance on *internal* military accountability mechanisms for operational conduct.

**Assessment.** Pakistan's counter-terrorism model conflates *command* with *accountability*. A defensible alternative — used by many counter-insurgency campaigns, including in contexts of federalism — is a *joint, published, accountable framework*: a provincial-federal security committee with a legislative mandate, civilian primacy over policing and investigations, military primacy at the border and in manoeuvre, fused intelligence, and public annual reporting (Chapters 7, 15). The current model produces neither civilian ownership nor military accountability, and its legitimacy cost grows with each mass-casualty attack on the police.

## 6.11 Post-Operation Stabilization: Successes and Failures

**Successes.** (i) *Swat/Malakand recovery*: infrastructure rebuilding (roads, the Swat Expressway), the restoration of tourism, and the return of schools — the single clearest instance of post-operation normalization in Pakistan. (ii) *Market and transport recovery* in parts of Bajaur, Mohmand and Kurram (interrupted repeatedly by violence). (iii) *Police expansion* with new posts and facilities, the Elite Force and Rapid Response Force, and the Safe City project (Chapter 7). (iv) *Return and rehabilitation programs* in the NMDs (TDP returns from 2015; AIP projects; Sehat Card coverage where funded). (v) *Economic reconnection*: the Peshawar–Torkham corridor's eventual reopening, and CPEC-related infrastructure (Rashakai SEZ).

**Failures.** (i) *Compensation*: documented delays in payments to the displaced and injured across multiple episodes (Tirah's return package of Rs 10m/killed and Rs 2.5m/injured was reported delayed into 2026; earlier programs were similarly slow). (ii) *De-radicalization at scale*: promised centres and programs were never resourced adequately (Chapter 8). (iii) *Justice and prosecution*: no post-operation justice process; few prosecutions relative to detentions or to incidents. (iv) *Local governance*: local government in the NMDs remains unimplemented in any meaningful fiscal sense. (v) *Institutionalization*: no transition of cleared areas to effective civilian policing; FC/army presence persists indefinitely. (vi) *Political settlement*: none attempted, beyond episodic deals with individual commanders and tribal jirgas.

**The stabilization gap.** The recurring failure is not the absence of programs but their *architecture*: Pakistan's model finances development through federal programs administered provincially, with no mechanism tying infrastructure and services to the security transition or to local political accountability. International practice suggests a different model: a single stabilization agency with a defined mandate, budget and reporting line; sequenced security-transfer milestones; and community-level accountability (Chapter 13).

## 6.12 The Question of Sustainability: Can Military Gains Hold Without Political Solutions?

**The empirical answer from KP's own record** is contained in the operations table above: in every case, gains decayed. The mechanism is now well understood analytically: military operations (a) destroy or displace armed structures but (b) do not resolve the recruitment pool, the sanctuary, the war economy, the justice vacuum, or the political grievance; and (c) they create new grievances through displacement, harm and coercion. When external pressure (ISAF/US presence in Afghanistan; Pakistani force concentrations in FATA) that constrained militancy is removed, the surviving structures regenerate.

**Five conditions for sustainability**, derived from KP's comparative record:
1. **Denial of sanctuary or a durable cross-border modus vivendi.** Without it, operations in a territorial corner of a networked insurgency merely relocate the conflict.
2. **Transfer of security responsibility to a capable, protected, professionally accountable civilian force**, with a realistic timeline and resourcing (the police problem).
3. **A justice dividend** — visible prosecutions of those who commit atrocities, and relief for those wrongly detained — as the most credible demonstration that the state has ended the conflict.
4. **A fiscal and political settlement with the periphery** (the merger's completion; local government; land and mineral regularization) that changes the terms of the state's relationship with the border belt.
5. **A peace constituency strategy**: education, employment, and protection of the moderates, women, and community leaders who form the social base of normalization.

**The unanswered question.** Can a military-led campaign *deliver* these conditions? The comparative evidence (Chapters 13) suggests that militaries can deliver conditions 1 and partly 2, but that conditions 3–5 require civilian institutions that KP currently lacks and that the 27th Amendment's centralization of authority may further weaken. The strategic implication is uncomfortable but unavoidable: **without a political process, kinetic operations will continue to produce temporary suppression at rising human and financial cost** — which is precisely the pattern the 2026 data record, with the highest quarterly militant fatality count (921) coexisting with rising civilian and security-force losses.


# CHAPTER 7 — LAW ENFORCEMENT, POLICING AND INSTITUTIONAL REFORM

## 7.1 Khyber Pakhtunkhwa Police: Historical Evolution, Structure and Current Strength

**Evolution.** The KP Police descends from the colonial frontier constabulary and the Punjab Police model of 1861; it operated for 156 years under the Police Act 1861 (and, in the NMDs, under the extraordinary Levies/Khasadar regime) before the **KP Police Act 2017** provided a modern statutory framework (see §7.2). The force has been reshaped by three shocks: the post-2001 insurgency, which transformed it from a law-and-order force into a counter-insurgency light infantry in all but name; the 2014 Army Public School attack and the National Action Plan, which expanded its counter-terrorism mandate and budgets; and the post-2018 merger, which added seven districts and absorbed 28,000 tribal personnel.

**Structure and strength.** The KP Police is headed by an Inspector-General (IGP) and organized into regional commands (RPOs) and districts (DPOs), with specialized units including: the **Counter-Terrorism Department (CTD)**; the **Special Branch** (intelligence); the **Elite Force** (roughly 6,000 personnel trained for high-risk operations); the **Frontier Reserve Force (FRF)** (~10,000); the **KP Bureau of Investigation (KPBI)** (~3,000, investigation); the **Tourism Police**; traffic and constabulary units; and the capital-city command in Peshawar (~8,000). Total strength: official statements place the force at approximately 70,000–90,000 personnel (the KP government reported a ratio of one police officer per 291 citizens in 2026); provincial officials have cited ~130,000 sanctioned posts in public debate, indicating a significant vacancy margin. The force's budget for FY2025-26 was reported at **Rs 170 billion — 8.7 per cent of the provincial budget**, up 37 per cent over two years (an additional Rs 46 billion), with ~Rs 17 billion for security development and ~Rs 18 billion for salaries; the government announced plans to raise the allocation to 12 per cent (Tribune 2026a). These figures mark KP as the province devoting the largest *share* of budget to policing — a reflection of need, and a long-standing political claim in Peshawar that the federal government should reimburse the cost of a "front-line province."

**The dual mandate problem.** A force trained and deployed to fight a militant insurgency struggles to deliver ordinary policing: investigation, prosecution support, dispute response, and protection of the vulnerable. This study's assessment is that KP Police has effectively been converted into a paramilitary constabulary with a policing appendage — an inversion with two consequences: ordinary crime (including extortion, land-grabbing, and organized crime) grows with impunity, and public trust in police as *police* erodes, which in turn reduces cooperation with counter-terrorism.

## 7.2 The KP Police Reforms (2013–2023): Achievements and Shortcomings

**The reform decade.** The most consequential reform period followed the 2013 provincial elections, when the PTI-led government appointed a reformist IGP (Nasir Khan Durrani) and pursued a package that included: merit-based recruitment and training reform; new training infrastructure; the establishment of **Public Safety Commissions** and **Complaint Authorities**; the **Dispute Resolution Councils** (DRCs); welfare and housing improvements; the creation of the **Elite Force** and **Rapid Response Force**; the strengthening of the CTD; and — decisively — the **KP Police Act 2017**, which: (i) gave the IGP a fixed three-year tenure (removable only through defined processes); (ii) established provincial and district public safety commissions to oversee police performance; (iii) created complaint mechanisms against police misconduct; (iv) formalized the police's duties, powers and accountability; and (v) introduced planning and professional standards provisions.

**Achievements.** Independent commentary and comparative assessments generally credit the reforms with: depoliticizing senior appointments (partially); improving training quality and facility standards; expanding the CTD; introducing community-facing mechanisms (DRCs, complaint cells); professionalizing public communications; and producing a cadre of trained officers whose performance in operations (including the 2026 Kohat clearance, where Elite Force and CTD teams retook the complex) exceeded earlier standards.

**Shortcomings.** Six are documented. (i) *Implementation gap*: the Public Safety Commissions have functioned unevenly or not at all in several districts; commission membership and activity have been politically contested. (ii) *Command turnover and politicization disputes*: the fixed-tenure provision has been eroded in practice, with frequent IGP changes and provincial-federal friction over appointments and postings; the PML-N's September 2026 "white paper" alleged political influence in police postings and counter-terrorism preparedness failures. (iii) *Resourcing gaps*: despite budget increases, the force remains short of protected mobility, counter-IED equipment, night-vision devices, forensics, and — critically — investigation capacity. (iv) *Welfare deficits*: the shuhada (martyr) compensation and family-support regimes, though improved, have been criticized as slow and uneven. (v) *Investigation quality*: the KPBI's creation notwithstanding, prosecution success remains constrained by weak crime-scene management, forensics, and witness protection. (vi) *NMD integration*: the reform design treated the merged districts as an extension of the settled-district model rather than as a distinct policing environment (see §7.5).

**Assessment.** The KP Police Act 2017 remains the legal foundation of any future reform and is, by regional standards, a progressive instrument. Its fate illustrates a general lesson: *legal reform without fiscal guarantees, personnel protection and political insulation produces partial implementation*. The 2020s' reform debate has therefore shifted from statute to capacity and protection — with the 2025–26 casualty data making the case unanswerable.

## 7.3 Counter-Terrorism Department (CTD): Capabilities, Operations and Performance

**Origins and mandate.** KP's CTD was established in 2010 (following the February 2010 attacks in Peshawar that killed dozens), initially as a specialized investigation and operations unit, with a network of police stations, intelligence wings and interrogation capacity. Under the NAP and subsequent reforms it received expanded budgets, personnel and technology.

**Documented performance (2025–26).** The CTD's own December 2025 statistics and independent reporting record: attacks on police rising from **327 (2024) to 510 (2025)**; **137 terrorist attacks in 2025** (against 147 in 2024) despite a **50 per cent rise in registered terrorism cases (1,058 → 1,588)**; **320 retaliatory operations** in response to 158 attacks; the arrest of **25 high-value terrorists** with bounties on their heads; and 124 Federal Constabulary personnel killed with 244 injured. In 2026, the CTD has been central to responses to the Swat and Kohat attacks, the disruption of drone networks (including the foiling of 341 drone attacks in H1), and multiple IBOs.

**Capability assessment.** The CTD's strengths are: dedicated personnel with operational experience; a functioning intelligence wing with informant networks; integration with IBO planning; and the legal authority (ATA, 1997) to detain, interrogate and prosecute. Its weaknesses, documented by official data and independent criticism, are: (i) *personnel shortfall* (3,844 sanctioned posts against 3,113 deployed — a 19 per cent gap; 638 field-operator recruitments incomplete as of mid-2026); (ii) *equipment shortfalls* (Rs 7.77 billion approved for equipment against Rs 7.05 billion released by June 2026; the claim that the entire province has only nine M4 rifles and eleven night-vision devices, advanced by a federal coordinator in September 2026, marked the low estimate of a contested but revealing debate); (iii) *investigation and prosecution quality* (conviction rates remain low; cases collapse on evidentiary grounds); (iv) *fragmentation* with the military intelligence architecture (Chapter 6); and (v) *human rights risk* — allegations of extrajudicial actions, of torture during interrogation, and of detention without charge have been documented by HRCP and international organizations.

## 7.4 Rapid Response Force and Elite Force: Specialized Units Assessment

The **Elite Force** (est. 2014, ~6,000 personnel) and the **Rapid Response Force / Frontier Reserve Force** constitute KP's specialized tactical capability, trained (in significant part) by the military and equipped with limited armoured and heavy weapons. Their performance in complex attacks — most visibly the September 2026 Kohat Police Lines clearance, conducted by Elite Force commandos and CTD teams with military QRF support — demonstrates genuine capability. Their limitations: (i) coverage breadth (specialized units cannot garrison the province; most police stations remain vulnerable to platoon-strength assault); (ii) dependence on military support for sustained combat; (iii) counter-IED and anti-drone capability gaps exposed by 2026 attacks (the Karak quadcopter attacks targeted three police facilities); and (iv) force protection deficits at static sites (APCs and checkposts remain the most common casualties). A capability audit — never published — would be the logical precondition of investment decisions, but no such public audit exists.

## 7.5 Levies and Khasadar Forces in Merged Districts: Integration and Challenges

**The transition.** As detailed in Chapter 5, the Levies (~15,673 personnel) and Khasadar forces were brought under KP Police command by ordinances/legislation in 2019, with a combined strength of approximately 28,000. The transition's problems fall into four categories.

*First, doctrinal mismatch.* Levies were border constabulary; Khasadars were a communal guard force with minimal training; KP Police are a settled-district force governed by the Police Act. Integrating the first two into the third without designing a *purpose-built NMD policing model* produced a hybrid: local personnel without police powers' confidence; settled-district officers without local knowledge; and neither with the equipment or training to confront TTP units.

*Second, personnel issues.* Khasadars' regularization, pensions, and grade fitment generated grievances and protests; many personnel remain uncertain of their status years after the merger. The number of new police posts created (~3,550 province-wide, including NMDs; with promises of 6,000+ initially) remains inadequate to the threat.

*Third, local legitimacy.* In parts of the NMDs, police are viewed as (a) predators (checkpoint extortion), (b) outsiders (settled-district officers), or (c) collaborators with historical abuses. Where communities trust their local force — as in some Bajaur and Kurram pockets — they provide intelligence and refuse shelter to militants; where they do not, they withhold both.

*Fourth, vulnerability.* Locally recruited officers are susceptible to coercion (threats to families), bribery, and — in some cases — ideological sympathy, particularly where recruitment standards were relaxed to meet quotas.

**The model question.** Three options exist for NMD policing: (i) continue the hybrid; (ii) build a *constabulary-style NMD force* with community-embedded units, tribal recruitment with vetting, dedicated training, and provincial oversight; or (iii) return policing functions to FC/paramilitary control (rejected by the merger's logic and by provincial politics). This study recommends option (ii) within the KP Police framework — a "NMD Wing" with its own doctrine, training, equipment and community-accountability mechanisms (Chapter 15).

## 7.6 Intelligence Infrastructure at the Provincial Level

**Actors.** Provincial intelligence has five components: (i) the KP **Special Branch** (district-level political and security intelligence); (ii) the **CTD intelligence wing** (counter-terrorist targeting); (iii) **district police** informant networks (the most embedded and the most fragile); (iv) federal agencies (IB, ISI, MI) operating in the province; and (v) *military intelligence* via FC/army formations. Coordination occurs through: the **Provincial Apex Committee** (chaired by the Chief Minister, with the Corps Commander); district intelligence coordination committees (the Sarbakaf curfew notification explicitly referenced the district intelligence coordination committee's recommendation); and national-level mechanisms (the National Security Committee; the National Intelligence Coordination Committee established in 2022 per ISPR statements, and NACTA's coordination role).

**Fusion or fragmentation?** The evidence indicates partial fusion: IBO targeting has improved (IBO volume and precision increased markedly in 2025–26), but the *Kohat Police Lines attack* (September 2026) — a complex, multi-phase operation against a facility housing CTD and Special Branch offices — suggests either intelligence failure, force-protection failure, or both. Structural fragmentation persists: police intelligence is not systematically shared with the FC/army in real time, and vice versa, as ICG (2026) documented across 94 interviews. The provincial police's intelligence priorities (local crime, political intelligence) also compete with counter-terrorism demands.

**Recommendations preview.** A joint provincial-federal *Counter-Terrorism Fusion Centre* with legal basis (provincial legislation or a federal-provincial MoU), common databases, protective intelligence for police installations, and a clear protocol for local intelligence protection would address the documented gap. The alternative — building parallel provincial intelligence capacity to rival federal agencies — is both unaffordable and politically impossible (Chapter 15).

## 7.7 Prosecution and Judicial Challenges: Anti-Terrorism Courts and Conviction Rates

**Structure.** The Anti-Terrorism Act 1997 (as amended, including 2013's post-APS reforms and later amendments) establishes Anti-Terrorism Courts (ATCs) with special procedures: trials in camera, no adjournments beyond limited periods, and the admissibility of certain confessions. KP has a limited number of ATCs relative to its caseload, plus juvenile courts acting under ATA provisions in some cases.

**Performance data.** Nationally, the NAP review literature records: **657 death sentences awarded between 2019 and 2022 with no executions in that period**; **2,273 anti-terror cases pending as of November 2024**; and recurrent concerns about acquittals due to poor investigation. In KP, the case-level record suggests: (i) successful prosecutions are possible (the October 2026 Peshawar ATC conviction of five accused, including a juvenile, for a 2024 Mohmand CTD case, with five-year sentences and fines, and the declaration of five others as absconders); (ii) the charge-to-conviction pipeline is weak (evidentiary failures, witness intimidation, forensic gaps, and the collapse of cases where digital evidence's admissibility is not properly established — a limitation explicitly noted in the Islamabad ATC judgment of August 2026, where defendants were acquitted of terrorism charges for want of proof of intent notwithstanding explosives recoveries); and (iii) appeal delays undermine deterrence.

**Structural problems.** Six are identified: (a) investigation quality — police lack forensic capacity, crime-scene protocols, and trained investigators (the KPBI's creation was meant to address this, but capacity remains thin); (b) witness protection — no functioning program, so witnesses recant or refuse; (c) judge security and case volume — ATC judges are exposed and overburdened; (d) the *alternative of military courts* — which, while producing convictions, weakens the civilian system's development, raises fair-trial questions, and removes cases from public scrutiny (with documented complaints that defendants received no charge sheets or verdict copies, litigated in the PHC in 2025); (e) *legal-aid scarcity*; and (f) *preventive detention practice* (detention without charge as a substitute for prosecution) which is both legally dubious and counterproductive.

**Post-27th Amendment environment.** The 27th Amendment's restructuring (the Federal Constitutional Court; reduced Supreme Court constitutional jurisdiction; executive dominance of the Judicial Commission) has implications for the *appeal architecture* of terrorism convictions and for judicial oversight of security agencies. Whether this strengthens or weakens counter-terrorism's legitimacy in KP is a live question; the early evidence — two Supreme Court justices resigning in protest and warning of the judiciary's subordination — suggests the change is being read by informed publics as a politicization of justice (Al Jazeera 2025b; Himal Southasian 2025).

## 7.8 Community Policing and Police–Public Trust

**Instruments.** KP has deployed a distinctive set of community-facing mechanisms: **Dispute Resolution Councils (DRCs)**, composed of local notables and retired officials, which mediate civil and criminal disputes outside the courts (KP's DRC network has been widely cited, including in international policing literature, as one of Pakistan's most successful community-policing experiments); **Public Safety Commissions** at provincial and district levels; **peace committees** in the NMDs (often encouraged and partially funded by the military); **Terrorism-Free District/Community** initiatives; and school and youth outreach.

**Performance and constraints.** The DRCs do real work — resolving thousands of disputes, reducing court backlogs, and generating goodwill. But they operate at the margins of the security problem: they cannot adjudicate terrorism, sectarian conflict, or land disputes involving armed actors. Peace committees in the NMDs have been both effective (intelligence, refusing shelter, negotiations with militants) and vulnerable (members assassinated; accused of militia abuses). Two structural constraints limit all community engagement: (i) *the coercion-first environment* — curfews, raids and collective measures undermine the trust that community policing requires; and (ii) *the trust deficit* — documented in the resentment of heavy-handed tactics (ICG 2026) and reinforced by the 2024 PTM ban and accusations of disappearances.

**Assessment.** Community policing in KP is undervalued and under-resourced relative to its potential. Comparative evidence indicates that police legitimacy is a force multiplier for intelligence and public cooperation (the primary currency of counter-terrorism). A normalization strategy should therefore treat police-community relations as a strategic capability — funded, measured and protected — rather than as public relations (Chapter 15).

## 7.9 Police Martyrdom and Morale: The Human Cost

**The numbers.** KP Police has lost **more than 2,000 personnel** since 2006 (contrasting with 369 killed between 1970 and 2006). Recent years: **186 (2023), 153 (2024), 159 (2025)**; **122 killed and 175 injured in the first half of 2026 alone (+93.7 per cent and +108.3 per cent respectively)**; attacks on police rose **48 per cent in 2025 (536 attacks) and 33.9 per cent in Jan–Sep 2026 (680 attacks)**; more than **500 police killed since 2023**. The single deadliest attack was the **30 January 2023 Peshawar Police Lines mosque suicide bombing** (over 100 killed, including ~86 police), while the **September 2026 Kohat attack** was the deadliest assault on a police complex since then. Most recent casualties have occurred in Peshawar, D.I. Khan, Bannu and Lakki Marwat (Chapter 3).

**Morale and welfare.** Documented morale factors include: compensation delays and adequacy (the provincial shuhada package has been increased repeatedly but processing is slow); the practice of posting personnel to high-risk NMD locations without family accommodation or rotational relief; equipment deficits (the night-vision and rifle claims); and the perception, widely held within the force and articulated by provincial leaders, that the burden of a national counter-insurgency is being borne by a provincial force without commensurate federal financing or operational authority.

**Strategic significance.** A force suffering a casualty rate of this magnitude faces predictable pathologies: risk aversion (static, defensive postures that concede mobility to militants); corruption (survival and welfare-driven); attrition and recruitment difficulties; and intelligence passivity. Each undermines the counter-insurgency. The 2026 escalation in police casualties is thus not merely a human tragedy; it is a *capability crisis* — and one reason the province's police-focused demands (equipment, federal reimbursement, joint command) are, in security terms, well founded.

## 7.10 Technology Integration: Safe City Projects, CCTV, Biometrics and Data Analytics

**The Peshawar Safe City Project.** Conceived in 2008 and inaugurated only on 3–5 March 2026 (after 18 years), the project cost **Rs 2.23 billion** for Phase I: **711 surveillance cameras at 133 key and sensitive locations**; an AI-enabled command-and-control centre integrating surveillance, emergency response and traffic management; facial recognition and automated alerts to patrol units; e-challan capability for traffic offences; a private wireless network; **smart surveillance vehicles, drones, anti-drone systems and 68 panic buttons**; and integration with BRT corridor, Red Zone and other camera networks. Projects in **D.I. Khan, Bannu and Lakki Marwat are nearing completion**, with rollout planned for divisional headquarters and merged districts (post-June 2026), Phase II (~Rs 5 billion) covering **Mardan, Kohat, Nowshera, Swat and Abbottabad**, and a planned extension to six southern districts (D.I. Khan, Bannu, Lakki Marwat, North Waziristan, Tank, Karak). The provincial government intends to legislate a **Safe City Authority** to regulate and integrate surveillance infrastructure.

**Other technology.** Biometric verification (NADRA-linked SIM and identity systems); vehicle registration/ANPR; the police's digital reporting systems (the KP "Digital Transformation Policy and Roadmap 2030" approved in 2026 includes cybersecurity and e-governance); CTD cyber units; and military surveillance/anti-drone capability along the border.

**Assessment.** Technology is necessary but not sufficient, for four reasons. First, *coverage*: 711 cameras in a city of ~4 million facing attacks in its periphery and rural districts cannot substitute for human intelligence and patrol capability; Peshawar itself accounts for only ~4–5 per cent of provincial incidents, while the violence is rural and southern. Second, *effectiveness evidence*: there is no published evaluation of Safe City's contribution to interdiction or deterrence. Third, *rights and oversight*: facial recognition without data-protection law, oversight mechanisms, or redress creates a substantial liberty risk that could itself become a source of grievance; and the Safe City Authority, if it concentrates surveillance in executive hands, would compound the 27th Amendment's centralization. Fourth, *protection*: the technology does not protect checkposts, patrols and static sites where police are dying. A balanced capability portfolio — counter-IED, protected mobility, night-vision, drones/counter-drones, and communications — matters more than an urban camera network for the current threat profile.

## 7.11 Comparative Analysis: KP Police versus Punjab and Sindh

**Budgets and scale.** Punjab fields the largest provincial force (with substantially more personnel than KP) and has invested heavily in urban policing technology (the Punjab Safe Cities Authority in Lahore, operational since 2016, was the first and largest such system). Sindh concentrates resources on Karachi, with a large CTD and significant federal/paramilitary support. KP's distinct position is that it spends the largest *budget share* on policing (8.7 per cent, rising to a projected 12 per cent) while facing the highest per-capita casualty rates.

**Reform trajectories.** Comparative scholarship identifies the KP Police Act 2017 as the most ambitious provincial policing statute in Pakistan (fixed IGP tenure; public safety commissions; complaint mechanisms). Punjab has retained a structure closer to the Police Order 2002 model, with reforms largely executive-driven. Sindh's reform experience has been shaped by urban political competition and by the concentration of its CTD in Karachi.

**Counter-terrorism specialization.** Punjab's CTD (established in 2017) operates in a lower-threat environment but with better infrastructure; Sindh's CTD is larger and more urban-focused; KP's CTD operates under sustained attack, with the documented deficits of §7.3. The **federal dimension** is the crucial asymmetry: Punjab and Sindh's security challenges are not framed as an interstate frontier problem, and they do not carry the burden of the frontier's refugee, closure and border-management externalities.

**Lessons.** Three comparative lessons for KP: (i) *urban technology is not a substitute for rural protection* (Punjab's Safe Cities model does not map onto KP's threat geography); (ii) *statutory reform matters most when it protects tenure, budget and oversight* — KP's Act should be defended, not diluted; and (iii) *federal fiscal rebalancing* is the only way to finance a "front-line province" burden: no provincial budget can simultaneously pay for counter-insurgency and development.

## 7.12 Recommendations for Deep Institutional Reform

Ten recommendations are advanced here and integrated into the Chapter 15 roadmap:

1. **Adopt a published Five-Year KP Police Protection and Capability Plan** with audited baselines: personnel (against sanctioned strength), protected mobility, counter-IED, night-vision, medical evacuation, and communications; published quarterly to the KP Assembly's relevant committee.
2. **Create a dedicated NMD Policing Wing** with its own doctrine, training academy (in the merged districts), recruitment-and-vetting standards, community-accountability mechanisms, and a staged transfer of primacy from FC/army formations against explicit capability milestones.
3. **Legislate and fund a Police Protection Fund** for shuhada families and injured personnel: guaranteed, timed, transparent compensation; education and employment support for dependents; and a public annual report.
4. **Reinvigorate the Public Safety Commissions** with mandated annual reports, published district performance data (attacks, casualties, complaints, convictions), and civil-society membership criteria insulated from partisan capture.
5. **Establish a Joint Counter-Terrorism Fusion Centre** (military-police-civil, with federal participation) with legal authority, common databases, protection protocols and quarterly public threat summaries.
6. **Rebuild investigation capacity**: a dedicated Counter-Terrorism Investigation Wing with forensics, crime-scene protocols, digital-evidence handling (addressing the admissibility failures documented in 2026 judgments), and a witness-protection law with funded protection units.
7. **Separate the CTD's operational and investigative functions** and professionalize both, with career paths, training exchanges, and a promotions policy tied to case outcomes rather than contacts.
8. **Fund community policing as a capability**: expand and protect DRCs; fund peace committees' welfare and security; and create a police-community liaison cadre (with female officers) in every high-threat district.
9. **Adopt a data-protection and surveillance-oversight statute** before the Safe City Authority is created, with independent audit, retention limits, redress mechanisms and transparency reporting.
10. **Negotiate a federal security-financing compact**: reimbursement of the provincial security incremental cost (a "frontier burden" transfer), tied to published capability and casualty data — converting the province's fiscal grievance into a transparent, auditable arrangement.

# CHAPTER 8 — DERADICALIZATION, REHABILITATION AND COUNTER-NARRATIVE STRATEGIES

## 8.1 Understanding Radicalization Pathways in the KP Context

**From models to evidence.** The global literature on radicalization offers stage models (pre-radicalization, identification, indoctrination, action), social-movement accounts (networks, identity, framing) and structural accounts (relative deprivation, state violence). KP's evidence supports a *relational* model with five empirically observable components:

1. **Exposure and grievance.** The overwhelming majority of KP recruits encountered militancy through family, village, madrasa or workplace networks (not through ideology first). Grievances most frequently cited in interviews and testimonials: deaths or humiliation of relatives in operations; detention and torture; drone strikes; the inability to obtain justice; and material deprivation.
2. **Identity and honour framing.** Recruitment narratives translate private grievances into collective ones (Pashtun honour; defence of Islam; revenge for kin — *badal*).
3. **Network linkage.** Combatants described joining because a relative, teacher, or friend was already involved — the "friend-of-a-friend" mechanism. This is why network disruption and rehabilitation are intertwined; and why surveillance-led prevention fails to address the relational core.
4. **Material incentives.** Income, status, and protection matter, especially for young men in the NMDs and southern belt.
5. **Ideological justification.** Ideology typically consolidates rather than initiates commitment, providing language, rules of conduct, and afterlife promise.

**Diversity of pathways.** Distinct profiles are documented: child soldiers recruited or handed over by families (the Sabawoon cohort); adolescent volunteers seeking status or acting on peer pressure; adult fighters motivated by revenge or ideology; sectarian recruits drawn through LeJ/SSP or ISKP networks; and, increasingly, self-radicalized online actors (Chapter 3). Programs that do not tailor interventions to profiles will fail at scale.

**Gender dimension.** Women's roles in KP's conflict are simultaneously under-recognized and analytically important: as victims (targeted violence, widowhood, displacement); as prevention actors (mothers' networks, female teachers and health workers, and women's peace organizations); and, in rare documented cases, as facilitators or recruiters. Ignoring women's roles in either direction produces bad policy — either a protection gap or a security blind spot (Chapter 12).

## 8.2 Pakistan's Deradicalization Framework: Policy and Institutional Architecture

**Policy lineage.** Deradicalization entered Pakistan's formal security discourse through the National Action Plan's provisions against extremism and terrorism (including the rehabilitation of willing militants) and the 2021 NAP revision; the National Internal Security Policy and the National Counter-Terrorism Narrative (developed under NACTA) provide the conceptual frame; and the provinces — mandated by NAP to build counter-extremism capacity — established rehabilitation programs. Notably, Pakistan has never adopted a *single national deradicalization policy* with a legal mandate, budget line, standards, and evaluation framework; the practice is a mosaic of military-run, provincial, and NGO programs.

**Institutional map.**
- **Military-run programs** in Malakand/Swat: **Sabawoon** (juveniles), **Mishal** (adults), **Sparlay** (families of detainees), plus parallel vocational facilities in Swat, Mingora, D.I. Khan and Bajaur; documented predecessors/follow-ons including *Rastoon* and *Pythom*; funding historically from the army with some international support.
- **Provincial programs**: KP's CTD and Home Department manage rehabilitation for detainees and de-radicalization modules in prison settings; the KP government has run (and intermittently funded) vocational training for surrendered militants; the KP cabinet approved reform measures from time to time (including, in 2026, police and digital reforms).
- **Punjab programs**: the Punjab CTD's deradicalization centre (Naway Sehar-type facility) and a pilot program that reported rehabilitating 300 members of proscribed organizations from 15 districts (with a Rs 9.33 million allocation).
- **NACTA**: mandated to coordinate counter-narrative and deradicalization work; has produced the National Narrative document and coordinates counter-financing; capacity has been constrained (its own 2018 report found roughly three-quarters of approved posts unfilled), and it received a new military-appointed head in 2026 (Major-General Faisal Naseer) with a mandate to strengthen coordination (CRSS 2019; TFT 2026).
- **Religious/community instruments**: the **Paigham-e-Pakistan** declaration (January 2018), a fatwa/declaration by a broad assembly of scholars (with 1,800+ signatories claimed) rejecting terrorism and suicide bombing; subsequent anti-militancy statements by wafaq boards; and mosque-level counter-narratives.
- **NGO/CSO programs**: PAIMAN Alumni Trust's mothers' counter-radicalization work; PEAD's peace education; Aware Girls and other women-led organizations; Khwendo Kor and others in KP; with international support (UN Women, EU, USIP, and others) — all chronically under-funded and now constrained by the contracting civic space.

## 8.3 The Sabawoon Centre: Model, Methodology and Outcomes for Juvenile Militants

**Origins and design.** Sabawoon ("dawn") was established in Swat in 2009 by the army's humanitarian wing (with a civilian police/psychologist component), in the aftermath of Rah-e-Rast, for *juvenile* detainees who had not committed grave offences and were deemed low-to-medium risk. It became the flagship of Pakistan's rehabilitation effort and is one of the most-studied programs of its kind in South Asia.

**Methodology.** Four components: (i) *basic education* (literacy, numeracy, and "corrected" religious education including instruction on Islamic pluralism and the illegitimacy of suicide attacks); (ii) *vocational training* (auto mechanics, carpentry, electronics, masonry, poultry/horticulture, computer skills); (iii) *psychological counselling and psychotherapy* (individual and group; addressing trauma and identity); and (iv) *social and family counselling* (family therapy; reintegration planning; monitoring after release). The curriculum explicitly aimed at "de-indoctrination" through engagement with the students' own religious vocabulary and exposure to national/cultural identity markers.

**Outcomes.** Reported figures: induction of **~226 juveniles across seven batches (2009–2015)**; **~143 reintegrated** after completing programs; officials claim a **99 per cent success rate** with low recidivism; independent academic assessments (NUST's *Journal of Peace and Security*, 2021) found that most reintegrated graduates pursued small businesses, jobs, or further education, while acknowledging **(a)** **higher-than-claimed risk of recidivism — with 10–15 re-institutionalized on the advice of monitoring teams**, and **(b)** **no independent evaluation with access to program data**. A separate journalist-academic field study (2023) could not verify the *Sparlay* centre's existence at all, and documented **difficulties faced by graduates in starting businesses or finding jobs**.

**Critical assessment.** Sabawoon's design is genuinely strong by international standards — particularly its combination of education, vocational skills, psychosocial care, family work, and post-release monitoring, mirroring the better-regarded international programs (Chapter 8.11). Its weaknesses are equally instructive: (i) *scale* — 226 juveniles over six years is minuscule relative to thousands of detainees and tens of thousands of at-risk youth; (ii) *transparency* — no independent evaluation, no published recidivism study with methodology; (iii) *aftercare deficit* — graduates report no material support, ongoing surveillance, travel restrictions, and community stigmatization; (iv) *the cohort question* — the program's beneficiaries were largely juveniles who surrendered or were lightly implicated, whereas current cohorts include harder cases; and (v) *outcome attribution* — the claimed success may reflect selection (low-risk intake) as much as intervention efficacy.

## 8.4 The Mishal and Sparlay Centres: Adult Deradicalization Programs

**Mishal** was designed for *adult* low-risk detainees deemed capable of reintegration, combining religious re-education, vocational training, and psychological support in a residential setting; **Sparlay** was designed for the *families* of detainees (mitigating the secondary radicalization risks and economic pressures that affect combatant households). Documented claims: **Mishal reintegrated approximately 400 individuals**; broader claims place the total number of ex-combatants who passed through the Swat rehabilitation system at **~2,200–2,500**; the KP Provincial Government earmarked funds (Rs 4.4 million in one budget cycle; the de-radicalization pilot received Rs 9.33 million per one account).

**Evidence of effectiveness.** The published record is thin: program claims are reported by officials; independent studies report graduates' complaints in a consistent pattern — no financial or material start-up support; inability to find jobs; surveillance (graduates described being followed and questioned for travel); a requirement to report to security forces; and community rejection, especially where the community knew the individual's history. The 2023 field study (Journal of Dialogue, Public Knowledge Project) found that *all twelve* graduates interviewed faced difficulties starting enterprises, and reported stigma and monitoring. This is the strongest available independent evidence — and it points to a **reintegration failure** rather than a *re-radicalization* failure: programs may change beliefs, but without economic and social re-entry, the graduates remain a vulnerable population.

**Sparlay's fate.** The field study's inability to verify Sparlay's existence (despite visiting two centres) suggests either consolidation under other names or quiet discontinuation — a microcosm of the absence of transparency in the sector.

## 8.5 Madrasa Reform and Regulation: Progress, Resistance and Gaps

**The policy record.** Pakistan's madrasa regulation efforts have a 25-year lineage: registration drives (2002, 2005), the 2019 framework agreement under which madrasas registered with the Ministry of Education (roughly **18,000 registered**; with commitments to bank accounts, foreign-student regulation, and curriculum goals), and then **reversal**: in October 2024, after JUI-F pressure, the **Societies Registration (Amendment) Act** allowed madrasas to register under the colonial-era Societies Registration Act as charitable bodies — a framework with minimal oversight of curricula, funding or foreign students. President Zardari objected publicly on FATF-compliance grounds; the government proposed re-registration under the education ministry; the Ittehad Tanzeemat-e-Madaris demanded gazette notification of the 2024 law. Punjab's January 2026 agreement (online registration through the Punjab Charity Commission, negotiated with the wafaq boards) represents a practical alternative model — transparent, consultative, digitized — but it has not been replicated in KP at scale (NYT 2025; Adaamir 2024; The Nation 2026b).

**The KP-specific picture.** KP hosts one of the densest madrasa networks in Pakistan, including the most politically significant institution (Darul Uloom Haqqania, Akora Khattak, associated with numerous Afghan Taliban leaders, and whose head, Maulana Hamid-ul-Haq Haqqani, was killed in the February 2025 ISKP bombing). Two gaps are salient: (i) *unregistered institutions* — a substantial (though unquantified) share of KP madrasas, especially small village institutions, remain outside any registry; and (ii) *financing* — foreign funding of madrasas was a central FATF-era concern; documentation remains incomplete, and the October 2024 legal change reduced the financial-transparency requirements that the 2019 framework had introduced.

**What reform would require.** The international and Pakistani evidence converges on five requirements: consultative (not unilateral) design; digitized, low-friction registration; curriculum modernization with religious-authority buy-in; financial transparency (bank accounts, audited sources, foreign student reporting); and *positive* incentives — faculty salaries, facilities, libraries, science labs, and student scholarships — without which regulation is experienced as an assault on the poor's only educational option.

## 8.6 Counter-Narrative Development: Religious, Cultural and Media-Based Approaches

**Instruments.** (i) *Religious counter-narratives*: Paigham-e-Pakistan's anti-terror fatwa and its dissemination; wafaq board statements; the Sabawoon curriculum's use of religious reasoning against suicide attacks and for pluralism. (ii) *National Narrative*: NACTA's framework, developed with academic and religious input, positioning the state's struggle against extremism in national and Islamic terms. (iii) *Media and digital*: state counter-terrorism messaging, social-media campaigns, blocking of extremist content (over 1.3 million URLs blocked by 2023); religious programming on state media. (iv) *Cultural counter-narratives*: Pashtun cultural production (poetry, music, TV) reclaiming religious and cultural identity from militant monopolization; memorialization of victims, including the APS tragedy's annual observances.

**Effectiveness.** Three observations. First, *the state's credibility problem caps counter-narratives*: messaging from institutions perceived as coercive or self-serving is discounted precisely by the audiences at risk. Second, *Pashto-language, locally credible messaging is scarce* — most content is Urdu, urban, and state-framed. Third, *the most effective counter-narrative vehicle is often not messaging at all* but demonstrable state protection and justice — Swat's post-2009 recovery (schools reopened, tourists returned) did more to discredit the TNSM's narrative than any campaign. The policy implication is that counter-narrative work should be *embedded in credible local institutions* (mosques, madrasas, schools, jirgas, radio) rather than centralized in state communications.

## 8.7 Role of Religious Scholars and Ulema in Countering Extremist Ideology

**The anti-militant ulama.** A significant body of Pakistani religious opinion has opposed armed militancy: Paigham-e-Pakistan; the Fatwa of the Wafaq-ul-Madaris; statements by Barelvi and Deobandi scholars; and victims' advocacy (the families of APS; the relatives of clerics killed by militants). In KP, the murders of anti-Taliban clerics and tribal elders were systematic in 2005–2014, and the 2025 killing of the Haqqania head — whatever his complex legacy — illustrated that even establishment-aligned clerics are targets.

**The limits.** (i) *Ambiguity*: some scholars have historically justified jihad in Kashmir/Afghanistan while condemning attacks inside Pakistan — an inconsistent position that militants exploit and that undermines the clarity of the counter-narrative. (ii) *Political instrumentalization*: religious parties (JUI-F most prominently) mediate state-religious relations while maintaining independent positions on operations, madrasa reform and sectarian groups. (iii) *Structural incentives*: madrasa economics — fee-free education and community prestige — create institution-level resistance to curriculum modernization.

**Practical recommendation.** The state's most credible route to religious counter-narratives runs through *standing, funded partnerships with anti-militant scholars and institutions* — not ad hoc endorsements — including protection for clerics, media platforms for anti-militancy sermons, and inclusion of scholars in deradicalization delivery (as Saudi Arabia and Singapore have done with their credentialed religious authorities).

## 8.8 Reintegration into Society: Employment, Education and Psychosocial Support

**The evidence.** As noted, the strongest independent evidence (interviews with twelve graduates, 2023) documents that rehabilitees face: no start-up capital; no job placement; employer reluctance; surveillance and travel restrictions; community stigma; and the psychological burden of untreated trauma. The NUST study found comparatively better outcomes (education continuation, self-employment) for the Sabawoon cohort, with monitoring.

**Why reintegration is the decisive phase.** Deradicalization literature is consistent: programs succeed or fail at *aftercare*. Individuals who return to unemployment, humiliation and surveillance have strong incentives to rejoin armed networks — not necessarily from renewed belief, but from material and social exclusion. Conversely, employment plus community acceptance (with monitoring that is respectful rather than punitive) is strongly associated with durable disengagement.

**Design elements supported by evidence.** (i) *Individual reintegration plans* (education, vocational certification, job placement, small-business grants); (ii) *family support* (stipends conditioned on school attendance; the Sparlay concept); (iii) *psychosocial services* (trauma treatment, especially for juveniles; the Sabawoon model); (iv) *protected, time-limited monitoring* with clear exit criteria rather than indefinite surveillance; (v) *community reconciliation* (jirga-mediated acceptance ceremonies; religious endorsement of re-entry); (vi) *employer partnerships* (subsidized apprenticeships; certification); and (vii) *documented release/clearance* so graduates can travel, work and access services without permanent suspicion.

## 8.9 Women and Youth-Focused Deradicalization Programs

**Women.** Two distinct program types exist globally and partly in Pakistan: (i) *mothers' programs* (PAIMAN's work with mothers of at-risk and radicalized youth in KP and FATA — among the most cited Pakistani PVE innovations); and (ii) *women's peace networks* (UN Women's Pakistan programme reported engaging **228 Afghan and Pakistani women peace activists** in dialogue processes contributing to cross-border peace discussions, alongside NACTA partnerships on women's leadership in peacebuilding). Women's PVE work benefits from unique access (to households and youth), but is constrained by: security risks, cultural gate-keeping, and funding that is short-term and project-based.

**Youth.** Youth-focused interventions include: the KP government's skill and employment programs (Youth Impact Challenge grants expanded to Rs 2 billion; the KP Youth Employment Program for IT skills; digital internships); the KP Youth Policy 2026's revision process (which itself documents that female education and employment lag, with only 37 per cent of women literate versus 65 per cent of men and women making up over half of unemployed youth); and the *Peace Education* work of CSOs. The critical gap is *prevention at scale*: school-based critical-thinking curricula, school safety, girls' education protection (from attacks and closures), and mental-health services are all underfunded relative to the risk.

**Assessment.** Both categories are extremely high-leverage and chronically marginal in Pakistani counter-radicalization spending. The KP Youth Policy's own drafting acknowledges that "youth programmes cannot catch up with demand."

## 8.10 Community-Based Approaches to Preventing Violent Extremism (PVE)

**Practice in KP.** A network of Pakistani CSOs operates PVE programs in KP: PAIMAN (mothers' networks, youth dialogues, peace education); PEAD (peace education and community dialogues); Aware Girls (women's empowerment and youth leadership in KP — with its founder subject to travel restrictions and legal harassment in earlier years); Khwendo Kor (women's and girls' development); the Khpal Kor and similar institutions; and district-level peace committees and jirgas (Chapter 12). Government-adjacent efforts include the "Terrorism-Free" district initiatives and, in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa context, peace jirgas and local *aman* committees supported by the administration and army.

**What works.** The international evidence base on community-based PVE (CVE) points to: locally-led (not externally imposed) programming; credible local voices (religious, tribal, teachers); multi-sector services (education, livelihoods, mental health) rather than messaging alone; addressing state-society grievances as part of prevention; and rigorous evaluation. Pakistan's KP practice aligns with the first three and lacks the fourth and fifth: there are almost no published *outcome evaluations* of Pakistani PVE programs, and civic-space contraction (the PTM ban; restrictions on activists; NOC regimes for NGOs) limits the very actors best positioned to deliver prevention.

**Assessment.** Community-based PVE in KP is at an inflection point: demand has never been greater (the 2021–26 recruitment environment), the practice is small, and the political space for independent civil society has narrowed. Without a deliberate re-opening of space and sustained financing, the state will be left relying on the least effective prevention instrument — coercion — while the recruitment pipeline continues to refill.

## 8.11 International Best Practices in Deradicalization

| Programme / country | Key design features | Transferable elements for KP |
|---|---|---|
| **Saudi Arabia** — Counseling Programme / Etidal & the Muhammad bin Nayef Center (PRAC) | Credentialed religious authority; family involvement; aftercare with employment and marriage support; deradicalization vs. disengagement distinction | Religious-authority partnership; aftercare contracts (employment, family); clear release status |
| **Singapore** — Religious Rehabilitation Group (RRG) | Volunteer scholars, individually tailored religious counselling; community (mosque) ownership; separate aftercare group for families | Islamic-credentialed counselling; mosque-based community ownership; family support group |
| **Denmark** — Aarhus model | "SSP" multi-agency approach: police, schools, social services, health; mentoring; exit programs; no criminalization of returnees where legally possible | Multi-agency local teams; mentoring; school and municipal involvement |
| **Nigeria** — Operation Safe Corridor (DDR for repentant Boko Haram) | Vocational training, psycho-spiritual counselling, community reintegration, follow-up | Reintegration plus Community Reintegration Committees; documented clearance certificates |
| **Colombia** — DDR with FARC (2016) | Individual reintegration plans; income support; transitional justice; truth-telling as social reintegration | Transitional justice link; individual plans; public truth mechanism |
| **Indonesia** — deradicalization programs | Government plus religious organizations; individual assessment; family and community | Family-centered programming; religious-organization delivery |

**Synthesis of design principles.** (1) Credible religious authority; (2) individual assessment and tailored plans; (3) family centrality; (4) multi-agency local delivery (police + social services + schools + health); (5) aftercare with real economic content; (6) community acceptance strategies and truth/reconciliation mechanisms where feasible; (7) clear legal status on release; (8) monitoring with exit criteria; and (9) independent evaluation with published results.

## 8.12 Critical Assessment: Are Pakistan's Deradicalization Programs Working?

**What can be established.** (a) Pakistan has *models* — the Sabawoon design is sound and internationally recognized; (b) the programs are *small* relative to need (hundreds, not thousands, of beneficiaries, against a recruitment pool of tens of thousands and a caseload of thousands of detainees); (c) the *evidence base is weak* — claims of 99 per cent success rates lack methodological support, and independent research documents reintegration failures and unverifiable claims; (d) *aftercare* is the principal weakness; (e) *geographic coverage* is concentrated in Swat/Malakand, missing the current epicentre (the southern belt and the NMDs), where programs have been initiated only intermittently; (f) *financing* has never been institutionalized in a budget line with accountability; and (g) the legal framework for detention and release of alleged militants creates perverse incentives (indefinite detention vs. premature release without genuine program completion), which deradicalization programs cannot fix from within.

**The verdict.** Pakistan's deradicalization infrastructure is *promising in design, marginal in scale, unverified in outcome, and misaligned with the current threat geography*. It is not, however, a failure of concept; it is a failure of political commitment and institutional investment. In a counter-insurgency that is demonstrably attrition-limited (Chapter 6), scaling deradicalization, rehabilitation and reentry is not a soft option; it is one of the few instruments that can reduce the enemy's replenishment rate.

**Priority recommendations (developed in Chapter 15).**
1. Enact a **provincial deradicalization and reintegration statute** establishing a KP Rehabilitation and Reintegration Authority with a legal mandate, defined admission criteria, judicial oversight of detention, published standards, independent evaluation, and a ring-fenced budget.
2. **Relocate and expand capacity to the current threat geography**: centres in Bannu/D.I. Khan and the NMDs (Bajaur, Khyber, North Waziristan), with mobile teams for the southern districts.
3. **Mandate independent evaluation**: a university-led, five-year longitudinal study with published recidivism, employment and psychosocial outcomes (with data-protection safeguards).
4. **Fix aftercare**: grants, apprenticeships, employer partnerships, psychological services, and clear release certification — the single most important determinant of durability.
5. **Employ the families**: expand a Sparlay-type program, with stipends tied to children's schooling and mothers' participation.
6. **Build the counter-narrative base locally**: fund KP-based Pashto-language media, mosque networks, and scholar partnerships; end the conflation of religious conservatism with extremism.
7. **Reform the legal pathway**: create a statutory juvenile-justice and rehabilitation track; end the practice of indefinite preventive detention of juveniles; and link deradicalization completion to judicial release mechanisms.
8. **Reopen civic space for PVE**: a transparent, rights-based NGO registration and NOC regime, and the withdrawal of the PTM proscription in favor of lawful political engagement (Chapter 12).


# CHAPTER 9 — GEOPOLITICAL AND CROSS-BORDER DIMENSIONS

## 9.1 The Afghanistan Factor: Taliban Government, Bilateral Relations and Security Spillover

**The structural transformation of 2021.** The Taliban's return to Kabul in August 2021 removed the principal external constraint on the TTP — a hostile Afghan government allied to the US-led coalition — and replaced it with an ideological kin government that Pakistan had supported for decades. The TTP's leadership relocated to Afghanistan (Kunar, Nangarhar, Khost, Paktika, Paktia and beyond); its fighters gained freedom of movement, training space, medical care, and access to newly abundant weaponry; and its families gained a sanctuary in which Pakistani pressure could not reach them directly. The International Crisis Group concluded in January 2026 that Pakistan was "the country worst affected by the fall of Kabul" — a striking inversion of Islamabad's expectations (ICG, cited in Wikipedia 2026d).

**Bilateral relationship, 2021–2025: from expectation to estrangement.** Pakistan's toolkit toward Kabul comprised: diplomatic engagement (repeated delegations, the "Afghan Inter-Ministerial Committee" and special envoys); border closures and trade restrictions (Torkham, Chaman); expulsions of Afghan refugees; and — ultimately — unilateral military action. Each instrument was tried; none produced compliance. The Afghan Taliban's position was consistent: it denies harbouring the TTP and characterizes cross-border attacks as Pakistan's internal problem; it refuses (as a matter of stated principle) to be seen acting against fellow jihadists under foreign pressure; and elements within it — particularly the Haqqani network, whose leaders dominate the Interior Ministry — maintain longstanding ties to the TTP and to Pakistani Taliban factions. Meanwhile, the TTP's own demands (prisoner releases; reversal of merger; compensation for casualties) make any Afghan-mediated deal hard to land.

**The escalation ladder to war (October 2025 – February 2026).** The sequence is documented: (i) *October 2025*: major cross-border clashes and Pakistani strikes inside Afghanistan, followed by a **Qatari/Turkish-mediated ceasefire (Doha, October 2025)**; (ii) *November 2025*: **Istanbul talks** produced agreement on a monitoring and verification mechanism — without a political settlement; (iii) *December 2025 – January 2026*: continued (if reduced) cross-border violence; Pakistan's air campaign inside Afghanistan resumed in late 2025 and continued through 2026 (reported strikes in Kabul and the eastern provinces); (iv) *6 February 2026*: the ISKP suicide bombing at an Islamabad imambargah (31 killed) — plus attacks in Bannu and Bajaur during early Ramadan — hardened Islamabad's position; (v) *21–22 February 2026*: cross-border clashes escalated; Pakistan launched airstrikes against alleged TTP/ISKP camps in Afghanistan; (vi) *26 February 2026*: **Operation Ghazab-lil-Haq** was announced, with the Defence Minister declaring "open war" (Wikipedia 2026a; Dawn 2026g; The Diplomat 2026a; Hindustan Times 2026).

**The war (February – October 2026).** Both sides deployed air power (Pakistan) and drones, artillery and border assaults (Afghanistan). Contested claims proliferated: Pakistan said it had killed 583 Afghan Taliban personnel, injured 795 and destroyed 242 checkposts by early March (later figures higher); Afghanistan claimed 55–110 Pakistani soldiers killed and dozens of posts captured; ISPR reported 274 Taliban/terrorists killed for 12 Pakistani troops in the first days (Dawn 2026g; Indian Express 2026; Hindustan Times 2026). Two Eid ceasefires — Eid al-Fitr (19–24 March, at Saudi, Qatari and Turkish request) and a TTP-announced pause over Eid al-Adha (late May) — briefly reduced hostilities; fighting resumed after each. On 24 September 2026, Pakistani strikes hit ten sites in Kandahar, Paktia and Khost; on 1 October 2026 strikes hit Kunar and Helmand, with UNAMA confirming at least 10 civilians killed (mostly children). Cumulatively, UNAMA recorded **764 civilian casualties (372 killed, 392 injured) in Afghanistan between 26 January and 31 March 2026 alone**, and **more than 1,100 civilian deaths and injuries by late September 2026** (UNAMA, cited in Khaama 2026; Dawn 2026g).

**Why it matters for KP.** The war has four direct effects on the province: (i) *force and attention diversion*: military resources shift to border and cross-border operations; (ii) *militant mobilization*: the TTP exploits the war to frame itself as an anti-state force in a national struggle, and announced its *Operation Khyber* spring offensive on 4 March 2026; (iii) *humanitarian flows*: displacement, return flows and border closures affect KP's border districts; and (iv) *escalation risk*: each cycle of strikes and retaliation hardens both governments' domestic political constraints against de-escalation.

**Assessment.** The Afghan sanctuary is the single most important *external* variable in KP's security equation, and the 2026 war has demonstrated that force alone cannot close it — Pakistan's airstrikes can punish and disrupt, but they cannot occupy Kunar or compel the Taliban's compliance. Sustained de-escalation would require a *verifiable* arrangement (monitoring, defined commitments on TTP movement/training, incentives such as trade and refugee cooperation), which the Istanbul mechanism attempted and the war destroyed. Rebuilding a credible channel — with regional guarantors — is a strategic necessity, not an act of goodwill.

## 9.2 The Durand Line: Historical Dispute, Fencing Project and Border Management

**The dispute.** The Durand Line (demarcated 1893–1896) has never been recognized by any Afghan government as a permanent international boundary. Afghanistan's position — that the line separated Pashtun and Baloch communities without their consent and is therefore void — has persisted through monarchic, republican, communist and Taliban governments. Pakistan's position — that the line is a settled international boundary, whatever its colonial origins — has been consistent since 1947. The dispute has practical consequences: Afghanistan's refusal to accept border posts and fencing as legitimate; periodic firing at construction and at posts; the non-regulation of cross-border movement in Afghan law; and the political costs of any Pakistani concession (which would be framed domestically as capitulation).

**Fencing and border management.** Pakistan began fencing the Durand Line in 2017 and reported substantial completion (of the KP/NMD-facing sectors and much of the Balochistan frontier) by 2021–2023, coupled with new terminals (Torkham, Ghulam Khan, Angoor Adda and others), border forts, and surveillance systems. Effects: reduced large-scale infiltration and a formalization of crossings; but also: increased friction with Kabul; disruption of traditional cross-border social and economic life (kin mobility, grazing, labour, and trade); and — crucially — a *diversion effect*: infiltration shifted to covert crossings, tunnels, and unfenced or barely monitored stretches, and firing over the fence became a recurring tactic.

**Closure as instrument.** Since 2021, both sides have repeatedly closed crossings (Torkham in early 2025 over a border-post dispute; Torkham and Ghulam Khan during the 2025 and 2026 hostilities). Closures damage both economies, particularly Afghanistan's (dependent on Pakistani transit) and KP's border districts (trade, transport, labour, and consumer prices).

**Assessment.** A fenced line without a *border regime* — lawful crossings, permits, trade facilities, dispute mechanisms and a hotline for incidents — is an incomplete solution: it hardens the border's security function while degrading its economic and social functions. The correct objective is not an impermeable seal (unattainable over 2,600 km of mountains) but a *managed border* that denies sanctuary transit while preserving the lawful movement on which border communities depend.

## 9.3 Afghan Refugees in KPK: Security Implications, Policy Dilemmas and Repatriation

**The scale.** Afghanistan's displacement crisis and four decades of conflict-related migration produced one of the world's largest protracted refugee situations. Pakistan hosts (by various estimates) 1.5–3.7 million Afghans, including UNHCR-registered Proof of Registration (PoR) holders (~1.3 million), Afghan Citizen Card holders, undocumented migrants, and new arrivals since 2021. **Khyber Pakhtunkhwa hosts the largest share — roughly one-third to one-half of the population of concern**, concentrated in Peshawar, Bannu, Nowshera, Swat, Mardan, Kohat, and camps (Nasir Bagh, Kacha Garhi, Jalozai closure, and others).

**The policy trajectory.** Phase one (2023–24): the Interior Ministry's **Illegal Foreigners Repatriation Plan (IFRP)** targeted undocumented Afghans from 1 November 2023; hundreds of thousands returned. Phase two (2025): **April 2025** deadlines led to 80,000+ expelled within weeks, with 1.3 million PoR holders (many with decades of residence) told to relocate out of Islamabad/Rawalpindi; UNHCR reported over 910,000 deported since September 2023. Phase three (2026): a **June 2026 review meeting** directed "expedited repatriation/deportation" including visa overstay cases; from **10 July 2026**, any Afghan found without a valid visa was to be arrested immediately; Bannu's camps (Bizan Khel, Ghoriwala, Mamand Khel) were vacated (525 families repatriated in one reported tranche); and KP prepared the "final phase" of repatriation for **~20,000 Afghans in Peshawar city**, with lists compiled by the Special Branch, District Security Branch and police, and Federal Constabulary support (Dawn 2026e; Tribune 2026b; Al Jazeera 2025; HRW 2026; NYT 2025).

**Evidence of abuses.** Human Rights Watch (April 2026) documented: arrests of Afghans while shopping, at school, or seeking day labour; confiscation of phones and cash; demands for bribes; detention and expulsion of persons at risk in Afghanistan (journalists, human rights defenders, former government officials); the forcible return of at least nine Afghan journalists including some with valid visas (Reporters Without Borders); and the November 2025 Surkhab camp operation in Balochistan in which more than 1,000 people were detained and homes and businesses bulldozed (HRW 2026).

**Security arguments and counter-arguments.** The security argument: Afghan nationals — including some linked to militant networks, and some in mixed-migration flows that criminals exploit — are present in enormous, poorly documented numbers; identity systems are incomplete; and camps have historically been used as cover (the October 2026 ATC conviction in Peshawar involved an abandoned house in a refugee camp in Mohmand used as a militant safe house). The counter-arguments are equally weighty: (i) the overwhelming majority of Afghans are not combatants, and mass expulsion is a collective measure that violates non-refoulement obligations for at-risk groups and generates profound resentment among the wider Afghan and Pashtun population; (ii) the policy is *substitutionary*: it addresses the population channel (Chapter 3) rather than the sanctuary channel; and (iii) it has been repeatedly shown to correlate with *rising*, not falling, violence in the periods of most intense expulsions.

**Assessment.** Pakistan has legitimate security, fiscal and demographic interests in a managed Afghan presence. The policy instruments available to serve those interests — registration, biometric documentation, work permits, encampment reform, and case-by-case enforcement against specific threats — are all more effective and lawful than mass arrest-and-expel campaigns. The current trajectory is producing three harms: humanitarian (refoulement); diplomatic (eroding Pakistan's standing and feeding Afghan irredentism); and security (creating grievance, disrupting information flows, and enabling extortion networks within the state apparatus).

## 9.4 Cross-Border TTP Sanctuaries and the Afghan Taliban's Relationship with the TTP

**The relationship's complexity.** The Afghan Taliban–TTP relationship is neither sponsorship nor estrangement; it is a set of competing and overlapping interests: (i) *ideological affinity* — shared Deobandi-jihadist lineage, personnel exchanges since the 1990s, and familial ties between borderland communities; (ii) *strategic dependence* — the TTP provided fighters for the Afghan Taliban's war and hosts fighters' families in Afghanistan; (iii) *divergence* — the Afghan Taliban govern a state whose interests now include (a) Pakistani trade, (b) not being attacked, and (c) keeping the TTP from importing Pakistan's war into Afghan politics; (iv) *fragmentation* — the Taliban's internal factions (Kandahar core; Haqqani network; military commissions) differ in their tolerance of the TTP, and some commanders function as intermediaries; and (v) *leverage* — the TTP is useful to Kabul as leverage in negotiations with Pakistan, and dangerous as an autonomous actor.

**Practical patterns.** Relocation and dispersal: repeated Pakistani requests and Afghan promises have produced some movement (relocations away from the border, shifts between provinces) without dismantling. Mediation attempts: the Afghan Taliban hosted and at times imposed ceasefires (including the November–December 2021 truce) and attempted to broker talks (2021–22). Enforcement: at no point has Kabul moved decisively against TTP infrastructure, and Afghanistan's position publicly treats the TTP as Pakistan's problem.

**Options for Pakistan.** The realistic set is: (a) *coercive pressure* — cross-border strikes (as in 2026) and economic restrictions — which disrupted but did not dismantle the sanctuary and produced escalation; (b) *conditional engagement* — trading on Afghanistan's dependencies (trade transit, currency, refugees, connectivity) for verifiable actions on specified armed groups; (c) *mediation* — using regional guarantors (China, Qatar, Türkiye, Saudi Arabia) to establish a monitoring mechanism with consequences; and (d) *decoupling* — recognizing that statelessness and fragmentation in Afghanistan mean that even a cooperative Kabul cannot perfectly control the TTP, and building internal defenses (border management, intelligence, deradicalization) accordingly. The study's assessment is that a combination of (b) and (d), with (c) as the process vehicle, is the only durable approach — and that (a) alone has demonstrated a poor cost-benefit record.

## 9.5 Role of Regional Powers: India, Iran, China, Russia and Central Asian States

**India.** Pakistan's persistent allegation that Indian intelligence networks support militant activity (principally in Balochistan, and occasionally claimed for KP) remains formally unverified; India denies it. Regardless of the factual dispute, the *strategic dynamic* is real: India–Pakistan tension (the 2019 and 2025 military exchanges, including Operation Sindoor in May 2025) shapes Pakistan's threat perception and force posture; the Taliban's relationship with India (diplomatic and humanitarian engagement post-2021) is read in Islamabad as encirclement; and any Pakistan–India crisis has historically coincided with heightened cross-border pressure in KP and Afghanistan. This study treats Indian involvement in KP's insurgency as *unproven*, while noting that the *perception* of it influences Pakistani policy and that Pakistan's own record of using non-state actors complicates its claims.

**Iran.** Iran's interests in Afghanistan (water rights, the Helmand basin; Shia and Persian-speaking populations; the Chabahar–India–Central Asia corridor) and its sectarian ties to Pakistan's Shia communities (including in Kurram and Hangu) make it a party to KP's security in ways that are rarely acknowledged. Allegations of Iranian-linked support to Shia militias in Kurram (including Zainebiyoun-linked fighters) and of Iranian involvement in Balochistan are contested; what is documented is Iran's mediation and its hosting of Afghan refugees (returned en masse during 2023–25). Iran's most significant structural impact is the sectarian equilibrium of the borderland: a reciprocal sponsorship dynamic in which each side's sponsorship is cited to justify the other's.

**China.** China's interests are threefold: CPEC security (including the safety of Chinese nationals — the March 2024 Shangla attack killed five Chinese engineers travelling to a Dasu project in KP, and attacks on Chinese nationals continued in 2024–26); counter-terrorism cooperation (China has supplied drones, surveillance technology and training support, and pressed Pakistan on TTP/ETIM threats); and mediation (China brokered Pakistan–Afghanistan talks in April 2026 and hosted a trilateral process). China also has economic interests in KP: the Rashakai SEZ (with 22 enterprises in progress by 2026, ~Rs 68 billion in planned investment, and ~3,000 expected jobs), the Karakoram Highway realignment, and mineral interest in the merged districts.

**Russia and Central Asia.** Russia's counter-terrorism concerns (ISKP; Central Asian fighters) have driven pragmatic engagement with the Taliban and with Pakistan; Central Asian states (Uzbekistan, Tajikistan, Kazakhstan) share the ISKP threat and trade interests in a stable Afghanistan (trans-Afghan connectivity: railways, power). Their interest in KP is indirect but real: instability in Pakistan's borderland constrains their trade routes and increases ISKP's reach westward.

## 9.6 The US Withdrawal from Afghanistan (2021): Impact on KPK Security

**Direct effects.** (i) *Sanctuary restored*: the withdrawal removed US ISR (intelligence, surveillance, reconnaissance) and strike capability that had degraded TTP/AQ leadership after 2001; Pakistan's own later operations operated without that enabler — a factor explicitly noted by analysts contrasting the Zarb-e-Azb environment with the current one (Dawn 2024a). (ii) *Weapons proliferation*: the Afghan National Security Forces' collapse released vast quantities of small arms, night-vision equipment, vehicles and munitions; Pakistan officially estimates USD 7 billion-plus of equipment was abandoned (Eurasia Review 2026a). (iii) *Refugee and migration pressures*: 2021–2022 saw new arrivals and, later, international resettlement pipelines that left thousands stranded in Pakistan when third-country admissions were suspended in 2025.
