(iv) *Diplomatic vacuum*: no external actor with leverage over the Taliban remains willing to invest in constraining the TTP on Pakistan's behalf, and the US–Pakistan relationship remains transactional (a dynamic visible in the 2026 US statement of support for Pakistan's "right to defend itself" while providing no material support).

**Second-order effects.** The perception of abandonment in Kabul's neighborhood (including Pakistan's sense that it paid the price for US policy) shaped Pakistan's more unilateral posture in 2025–26; and the lack of international funding for stabilization in the borderlands (contrasting with the AF-PAK era's development dollars) shifted the fiscal burden entirely to Pakistan's own strained budget.

## 9.7 Intelligence Cooperation and Failures: ISI, NDS/GDI, CIA Dynamics

**The triangle.** The ISI–CIA relationship (2001–2021) was essential to targeting al-Qaeda and produced the capture of numerous senior figures, but was chronically strained by divergent priorities (Pakistan's focus on India and on Afghanistan's post-settlement order; the US's demands regarding the Haqqani network and sanctuary). The ISI–NDS (Afghan National Directorate of Security) relationship pre-2021 was openly adversarial. Post-2021, the ISI deals with the Taliban's General Directorate of Intelligence (GDI) and military intelligence — counterparts with ideological ties to the adversary (TTP) that Pakistan wants them to act against. The institutional outcome is an intelligence relationship in which Pakistan lacks the leverage that a shared adversary provides.

**Documented failures and cooperation.** Failures: the TTP leadership's reconstitution post-2021; advance warnings missed for major attacks (including the Kohat Police Lines attack of September 2026, which struck a facility housing CTD and Special Branch offices — suggesting either a critical intelligence gap or a protection failure); and the ISKP's ability to strike inside Islamabad in February 2026. Cooperation: Pakistan's provision (and China's contribution) of intelligence to Kabul regarding ISKP; exchanges with Iran and China; and counter-financing cooperation via NACTA and the FATF context.

**Assessment.** In the absence of a cooperative Afghan counterpart, intelligence-led operations become the *entire* counter-terrorism strategy — with the self-limiting problems described in Chapter 6 (civilian harm erodes sources; attrition refills ranks). A sustainable approach requires either an intelligence-sharing channel with Kabul (which the 2025 Istanbul mechanism attempted) or a border-management and reconnaissance architecture comprehensive enough to compensate for its absence (which the fence-plus-drone model does not achieve).

## 9.8 CPEC Security and the Geopolitics of Economic Corridors

**CPEC in KP.** The China–Pakistan Economic Corridor's western and Hazara alignments pass through KP (Karakoram Highway realignment; the Rashakai SEZ; energy projects; the Swat Expressway's linkage). Estimated investment in KP under CPEC remains modest relative to on-paper commitments; Rashakai's progress (22 enterprises in progress; Rs 68 billion planned investment; the September 2026 inauguration of a mobile-phone manufacturing unit) illustrates both the promise and the slow pace.

**Security politics.** The security of Chinese nationals (and projects) has become a central domestic political issue and a diplomatic constraint: attacks on Chinese personnel (Dasu in 2021; Shangla in March 2024 killing five engineers; subsequent incidents) have prompted Beijing to demand stronger security guarantees, occasionally linking to demands for joint security arrangements — an unsettling prospect for Pakistani sovereignty. Chinese pressure also contributes to the political economy of operations (analysts have argued the timing of Azm-e-Istehkam reflected, in part, Chinese concerns) and to Pakistan's willingness to escalate against Kabul (Chinese mediation in 2026 notwithstanding).

**Assessment.** CPEC's security burden in KP is substantial: it requires protection of long linear infrastructure through contested terrain, generates grievance where land acquisition and labour practices are perceived as unfair, and creates high-value targets. The economic dividend (jobs, connectivity, energy) is real but smaller and slower than promised, and it cannot substitute for the fiscal settlement of the merged districts in producing a peace dividend. Moreover, the geopolitics of corridors means KP's security is now an element of Sino-Pakistani and Sino–Central Asian relations, further reducing Pakistan's freedom of action.

## 9.9 The Balochistan–KPK Security Nexus

**Connections.** The two western provinces' insurgencies are distinct (ethno-nationalist and separatist in Balochistan; jihadist and Pashtunist in KP), but they interlock in five ways: (i) *personnel and networks*: TTP factions operate across Balochistan (TTP claimed the Quetta and Ziarat attacks of July 2026, with the BLA claiming the Lasbela attack); the TTP's Baloch reach and BLA's Pashtun recruitment overlap in the border districts; (ii) *logistics*: arms and narcotics routes cross both provinces; (iii) *state response*: Azm-e-Istehkam's national framing, ISPR's aggregated casualty reporting, and the federal claim that "hostile agencies" sponsor both insurgencies; (iv) *politics*: the PTM's Balochistan bans and the Pashtun–Baloch solidarity framing in rights movements; and (v) *data and attention*: in Q3 2026, Balochistan (829 deaths) surpassed KP (556) as Pakistan's most violent region — a shift with national political consequences, including the July 2026 Quetta attacks and the Apex Committee response.

**Strategic implication.** KP and Balochistan jointly oblige Pakistan's military to fight on two fronts with different adversaries, distinct terrain, and separate political dynamics — a force-allocation reality that has historically produced discontinuous pressure in each province. Resource-constrained strategy should therefore be explicit about acceptable risk in each theatre rather than responding reactively to the latest attack, or the state will continue to oscillate between theatres.

## 9.10 The Role of International Organizations: UN, NATO, SCO, FATF

**United Nations.** UN instruments matter to KP through: (i) *Security Council sanctions* (1267 and 1988 regimes; the Analytical Support and Sanctions Monitoring Team's assessments, whose 37th report in February 2026 documented increased TTP attacks from Afghanistan); (ii) *UNAMA's* civilian-casualty documentation for the cross-border strikes; (iii) *UN human rights mechanisms* (the High Commissioner's and Special Rapporteur's statements; the Secretary-General's ceasefire appeals during the 2026 war); (iv) *UNHCR and IOM* for refugee and returnee management; and (v) *UN Women and UNODC* for PVE and narcotics programming. The UN's leverage is limited by the absence of enforcement mechanisms in the Afghanistan context and by the politics of the Security Council.

**NATO.** NATO's role ended with the withdrawal, but its legacy is material (weapons in circulation; the coalition support fund's fiscal dependence; the "Af-Pak" framework's collapse).

**SCO.** Pakistan's membership (and its hosting of the 2024 SCO summit, during which security was emphasized) provides a platform for counter-terrorism cooperation with China, Russia, and Central Asian states — including the Regional Anti-Terrorist Structure (RATS). SCO declarations routinely commit members to non-support for terrorism against each other — a commitment relevant to Afghan–Pakistan disputes.

**FATF.** Pakistan's exit from the FATF grey list (2022) was consequential for its international financial standing and for the seriousness with which it pursued counter-financing and madrasa financial oversight — indeed, FATF compliance pressure was the principal driver of madrasa regulation efforts, and its relaxation (following the 2024 statutory reversal) is a cautionary example of how external pressure can produce both progress and its reversal.

## 9.11 Geopolitical Scenarios and Their Implications for KPK Security (to 2035)

**Scenario A: Regional de-escalation and a verifiable border modus vivendi (low probability, high impact).** A Pakistan–Afghanistan agreement (brokered by China/Qatar/Türkiye/Saudi Arabia) with monitoring, prisoner exchanges, and reciprocal limits on cross-border activity; TTP pressure reduced but not eliminated; Pakistan's military returned to internal focus; fiscal space for the merged districts' settlement. Probability: low (15–20 per cent) absent a change in Taliban incentives; consequence for KP: the single largest improvement available.

**Scenario B: Managed confrontation (current trajectory).** Periodic cross-border strikes and ceasefire cycles; continued insurgency at 2025–26 levels; growing interdependence with China's security interests; gradual erosion of Pakistan's border-region economy and legitimacy. Probability: 45–55 per cent. Consequence: continued high casualties; a slow fiscal and institutional attrition.

**Scenario C: Escalatory collapse (interstate war intensifies).** Repeated mass-casualty events inside Pakistan (Islamabad/KP) trigger larger strikes; Afghanistan responds with cross-border attacks; border trade collapses; refugee flows reverse; TTP and ISKP both gain from the chaos; the TTP's operational reach expands to central Punjab and Karachi. Probability: 20–25 per cent. Consequence: KP becomes a full war economy with the highest civilian cost in a generation.

**Scenario D: Regional realignment (India–Pakistan crisis interlinked).** Any major India–Pakistan confrontation pulls Pakistani forces eastward, creates opportunity structures for both TTP and BLA, and produces a two-front crisis. Probability: variable (10–20 per cent for a major crisis). Consequence: severe; force allocation and political attention shift away from KP precisely when it is most fragile.

**Scenario E: Taliban internal instability.** Factional conflict or succession crisis in Kabul fragments control over TTP sanctuary areas, producing local deals and local chaos — potentially reducing some threats (command fragmentation) while increasing others (uncontrolled zones; ISKP opportunity). Probability: 25–35 per cent over a decade; consequence: unpredictable, possibly regionally serious.

**Cross-cutting analysis.** In four of the five scenarios, KP's security is determined in large part by decisions taken in Kabul, Islamabad, Beijing, and Doha rather than Peshawar. This is why the study treats "diplomatic and border strategy" as an integral part of the normalization roadmap, not an external footnote: a province cannot normalize its security while a neighbouring state hosts the force that attacks it.


# CHAPTER 10 — SOCIO-ECONOMIC DIMENSIONS OF SECURITY NORMALIZATION

## 10.1 Poverty and Inequality in KPK: Statistical Overview and Spatial Analysis

**The provincial picture.** In the FY2024-25 poverty estimates released by the Planning Ministry in 2026, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa's poverty rate rose from **28.7 per cent to 35.3 per cent** — the second-highest provincial rate after Balochistan (47 per cent) and far above Punjab (23.3 per cent) and the national average of 28.9 per cent (Economic Survey 2025-26). The increase is among the sharpest of any province: the causes officially cited include fiscal consolidation under the IMF programme, energy tariff adjustments, exchange-rate depreciation, inflation, disaster losses, and weak growth (Planning Ministry 2026; Indrastra 2026). The World Bank's provincial estimate for KP was lower (~29.5 per cent in 2025, per the draft KP Youth Policy), illustrating the sensitivity of poverty measurement — but the direction of change is unambiguous (KP Youth Policy 2026).

**Inequality.** The inequality index for KP rose from 24.8 to 29.4 — the largest proportional increase of any province (Punjab 28.4→32; Sindh 29.7→35.9; Balochistan 21→26.5). Nationally, the inequality reading was the highest in 27 years. In KP, inequality has a specific spatial form: the Hazara and Peshawar divisions concentrate industry, services, education and health; the southern districts and the merged districts are structurally poorer, with the NMDs showing the lowest human development performance in the province (SDPI 2025).

**Spatial analysis.** SDPI's district-level Human Development Index series (2024 report, covering 2019–2023 changes) found that KP's overall HDI fell from **0.650 to 0.623**, with only 2 of 29 tracked districts improving on score and 27 declining; health and living-standards sub-indices declined (education improved in 13 districts; health in 5; housing/living standards in 7). Reading the same series for *ranking*, districts including Swabi, North Waziristan, Abbottabad, Mardan, Mansehra, Peshawar, Malakand, D.I. Khan, Swat, Bajaur, Mohmand, Hangu, Kohat, Chitral and Nowshera improved their *relative* position — a reminder that rankings and scores can diverge when all districts decline (SDPI 2025).

**Interpretation.** The poverty and HDI data establish four facts relevant to security. First, KP's socio-economic position has *deteriorated* during the very period in which violence resurged — the causal arrows run in both directions (conflict destroys livelihoods; poverty recruits). Second, the deterioration is *policy-sensitive*: the macro-fiscal mix (tariffs, taxation, subsidy withdrawal) and disaster shocks, not conflict alone, drove it. Third, the geographic concentration of poverty maps onto the geography of violence (NMDs; southern KP), though the relationship is not mechanical (Swat's literacy and relative prosperity did not prevent its 2026 attack). Fourth, the *perception* of deprivation — relative inequality, visible elite wealth, and the failure of merger promises — matters as much as absolute measures.

## 10.2 Unemployment and the Youth Bulge: A Ticking Time Bomb?

**The labour market.** Pakistan's labour force reached 83.1 million with 5.9 million unemployed (7.1 per cent) in the FY2024-25 estimates — the highest unemployment in 21 years (Economic Survey 2025-26). KP's unemployment has run above the national average (8.8 per cent in 2020–21 in the Labour Force Survey series, one of the highest provincial rates); the *youth* dimension is more severe: **31 per cent of educated youth are jobless (PIDE, March 2025)**; women constitute 51 per cent of the unemployed; roughly **40 per cent of youth are not in employment, education or training (NEET)** per recent surveys cited in the KP Youth Policy process; and the ratio of aspirants to formal posts is extreme — **866,000 candidates applied for 16,454 teaching vacancies in mid-2025** (more than 52 applicants per seat).

**The youth bulge.** Approximately half of KP's population is under 25 (KP Government 2024). A cohort of this size entering the labour market with weak absorption capacity produces predictable outcomes: long unemployment spells; migration (internal and external — Gulf labour migration is a major safety valve); informality; and, at the margins, vulnerability to armed-group recruitment (which offers wages, status, and purpose). The KP Youth Policy 2026's own drafting acknowledges that "youth programmes cannot catch up with demand," and that graduates face "widespread unemployment and outdated curricula."

**Programs and their adequacy.** Provincial instruments include: the Youth Impact Challenge grants (expanded to Rs 2 billion); digital internship schemes; the KP Youth Employment Program (IT skills); the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa Rural Economic Transformation Project (KP-RETP); and the Climate Action Plan's youth and women components. These are worthwhile but quantitatively insufficient: no plausible increase in these programs can absorb a cohort of this scale. The binding constraint is *private-sector job creation*, which requires electricity reliability, credit, security, regulatory predictability and connectivity — i.e., the whole investment climate.

**Assessment: "ticking time bomb" is the wrong metaphor.** The evidence does not show that unemployed youth automatically radicalize; it shows that they are *available* — and that armed groups recruit from that availability at moments of mobilization (operations, funerals, sectarian incidents). The policy implication is not simply "create jobs" (a general development agenda) but *targeted* employment and engagement in the specific districts and cohorts where recruitment occurs, coupled with deradicalization aftercare (Chapter 8) and community programs (Chapter 12).

## 10.3 Education: Access, Quality and the Battle for Minds

**Access.** KP's literacy rate is 51 per cent (female 37 per cent; male 65 per cent), below the national 60 per cent (Economic Survey 2024-25). KP has one of the country's largest out-of-school populations; girls' enrolment lags, and in the merged districts the pipeline collapses early: approximately 37 per cent of girls attend primary school and only about 5 per cent reach secondary (KP Youth Policy 2026). Schools have been destroyed or closed by militants; hundreds were closed in Kurram, Bajaur, Khyber, Mohmand and North Waziristan for extended periods (EUAA 2026); and the 2025 floods damaged hundreds of schools in KP, disrupting education for thousands (UNICEF 2025).

**Quality.** Learning outcomes are weak nationally, and KP is no exception: high enrolment-to-learning gaps, teacher absenteeism, outdated curricula (particularly the absence of critical thinking, civic and digital skills), and inequitable teacher deployment. The university sector (Peshawar, Malakand, Swat, Kohat, Gomal, Hazara, and the NMDs' emerging campuses) produces graduates for a saturated public-sector market.

**The battle for minds.** Education is simultaneously the long-term prevention instrument and a contested space: the curriculum's treatment of religious diversity; the presence of sectarian content; the madrasa sector (Chapter 4); and the question of who teaches. The 2026 Swat attack's timing (during a "Pasoon" protest against militancy) and the 2012 shooting of Malala Yousafzai frame the stakes: girls' education is a front line, and the state's ability to protect it is a legitimacy test. The provincial "Education Emergency" declarations (2023–26) have not been matched with financing at the scale required (a commonly cited requirement: 4 per cent of GDP on education, versus ~2 per cent).

**Assessment.** No single intervention is more consequential for the 2035 security horizon than the education system — but only if it addresses quality (learning), girls' access (protection plus proximity), and school safety simultaneously. The merged districts require a *specific* program: school reconstruction, female teacher recruitment and safe accommodation, transport stipends, and community compacts (jirga- and cleric-endorsed) guaranteeing non-interference.

## 10.4 Healthcare Infrastructure and Its Security Implications

**Baseline.** KP's health system faces: workforce shortages (particularly female physicians and specialists in rural districts); uneven facility distribution (with tertiary care concentrated in Peshawar); drug shortages and out-of-pocket costs; and security threats to health workers (attacks on polio teams and their police escorts; the killing of health staff; and the withdrawal of services from insecure areas). The 2025 floods damaged 60 health facilities in KP (including 3 destroyed) and 68 Lady Health Worker houses (WHO 2025); the merged districts' indicators remain the weakest in the province (skilled birth attendance; road access to facilities) (ResearchGate/GRR 2025).

**Programs.** The Sehat Card Plus (a health insurance scheme covering provincial residents) was a flagship achievement — but its application to the merged districts was suspended at one point and became the subject of litigation (a 2022 PHC petition), illustrating how fiscal disputes translate into service withdrawal in the most vulnerable districts. Polio eradication remains a security-dependent program, and KP remains an endemic zone with recurring campaign disruptions.

**Security implications.** Three are direct: (i) attacks on vaccination teams and health workers represent attacks on state legitimacy in its most benign form; (ii) clinics and hospitals are among the few state presences in insecure districts, so their closure is a *governance* outcome, not merely a service one; (iii) conflict-related trauma and mental illness (see §10.7) burden a system with almost no psychiatric capacity — the WHO estimates that Pakistan has roughly 500 psychiatrists for 240 million people.

**Assessment.** Health is an under-used stabilizer. Mobile health units, maternal-child services, and mental-health outreach are low-cost, high-legitimacy instruments that can be delivered in the same districts where counter-insurgency seeks intelligence and cooperation. Their protection (via community escort arrangements and police protection where needed) should be treated as a security task.

## 10.5 Infrastructure Development: Roads, Electricity, Connectivity and Their Normalizing Effect

**Roads.** KP's road network expanded significantly in the last two decades: the Islamabad–Peshawar Motorway (M-1); the Hazara Motorway; the Swat Expressway; the Lowari Tunnel (connecting Chitral year-round); and various NMD roads. Roads moved markets, tourism and state presence; they also moved militants and smuggling, and their construction created both employment and grievance (land acquisition, contract capture). The NMDs' connectivity remains the weakest in the province, with projects stalled for lack of funds.

**Electricity and energy.** KP's electricity situation includes: chronic load-shedding in some areas; high losses (theft and non-payment — the PESCO problem); huge hydel potential (KP hosts a significant share of Pakistan's hydropower potential, with projects like Diamer-Bhasha downstream on the Indus and smaller run-of-river schemes); and community grievances about the province's energy exports without commensurate local benefit — a recurring political theme. Electricity reliability is the single most cited constraint in manufacturing surveys; in the merged districts, grid extension is incomplete.

**Digital connectivity.** Mobile coverage is broad but data quality is uneven; the merged districts had restricted mobile-data service for years (a security measure with enormous economic cost), gradually eased. Digital connectivity matters for: e-governance; the digital economy (freelancing — a genuine KP success story, with Peshawar and Swat producing substantial freelance IT exports); and the surveillance/security architecture.

**Assessment.** Infrastructure's normalizing effect is real but conditional: roads and electricity restore markets and state presence, but without accompanying security (protection from extortion and attack) and justice, they can be captured by militants or by elite networks. Empirical KP evidence — Swat's recovery, Bajaur's market revival — supports the proposition that *infrastructure plus restored security plus services* reduces support for militancy. Infrastructure alone does not.

## 10.6 The IDPs Crisis: Displacement, Return and Rehabilitation

**The scale over time.** Displacement in KP and the FATA since 2008 has been among the largest in the world: **over 6 million people displaced between 2003 and 2014** (EUAA, citing sources); 2–2.5 million in Swat/Malakand (2009); ~1 million from North Waziristan (2014); several hundred thousand from other operations; and recurring waves since — Kurram (November–December 2024: thousands in temporary shelters); **Bajaur (Operation Sarbakaf, mid-2025: ~100,000 displaced)**; and **Tirah Valley (Khyber district), where a second wave began in January 2026 and had reached 33,800 registered families by September 2026**, with a return window agreed for 10–15 October 2026 and disputed by displaced representatives under the banner of Tehreek Mutasireen Tirah (Countercurrents 2026; EUAA 2026; Paradigm Shift 2025).

**Return and rehabilitation.** Pakistan's return-and-rehabilitation architecture has included: TDP return programs (managed by the KP government with FDMA, army and UN support); cash grants for reconstruction (typically Rs 100,000–400,000 per family in various phases); food and NFI distributions; and RDP infrastructure. The documented weaknesses are consistent across episodes: compensation delays and under-coverage (the Tirah package — Rs 10 million for families of the killed, Rs 2.5 million for the injured — was reported delayed into 2026); inadequate verification and exclusion (IDP card fraud and political capture); poor sequencing in harsh seasons (the October return window vs Tirah's early winter); unexploded ordnance risk; and insufficient psychosocial support.

**The security IDP agenda.** Displacement is not merely humanitarian: it (a) removes people from their livelihoods and social protection networks (recruitment vulnerability); (b) generates grievances that armed groups exploit ("the state destroyed your house and promised compensation it never paid"); (c) reduces the population's *stake* in the state; and (d) creates information vacuums in emptied areas that militants exploit. Conversely, *successful return* — with real compensation, services and protection — is one of the most powerful normalization indicators available. The Tirah case in late 2026 is therefore a policy test case: if the return process fails the population (winter, delayed funds, insecurity), the counter-terrorism rationale for the operation will be undermined by its own aftermath.

## 10.7 Psychosocial Trauma and Mental Health in Conflict-Affected Communities

**The burden.** Two decades of exposure to suicide attacks, airstrikes, displacement, bereavement, torture and economic shock have produced a mental-health burden that is almost entirely undocumented and untreated. Available indicators: the national scarcity of psychiatric services (~500 psychiatrists nationwide, with very few in KP's public sector outside Peshawar); the documented trauma among children in Khyber, Darra Adam Khel and Swat (multiple studies; Save the Children and academic surveys); the mental-health consequences of the 2025 floods (WHO reported affected populations with elevated distress); and the specific pathology of *witnesses to atrocity* — including the APS generation.

**Programmatic response.** KP has a government mental-health policy and hospital psychiatric units in Peshawar (including at the Lady Reading Hospital); NGOs (including the Sabawoon program itself) provided counselling; international organizations have run psychosocial support programs after major displacements. But as a system, the response is: urban, clinic-based, underfunded, and disconnected from schools, primary care, and religious institutions where distress is first encountered.

**Why it matters for security.** Untreated trauma: (a) impairs child development and educational outcomes; (b) raises vulnerability to radicalization (revenge motivations and reduced impulse control); (c) degrades community resilience; and (d) is transmitted across generations. Psychosocial support is therefore a *security investment* with an unusually favourable cost-benefit ratio, particularly in schools (trauma-informed teaching), primary care (basic mental health integration), and mosques (pastoral counselling training).

## 10.8 Women's Security, Gender-Based Violence and Female Participation in Peacebuilding

**Security realities.** Women in KP face: high rates of domestic and honour-based violence (under-reported); restricted freedom of movement; limited access to justice (the *jirga* system's exclusion of women; legal pluralism; and the cultural premium on family reputation); economic exclusion (female labour force participation far below male rates; inheritance denial); and conflict-specific harms (widowhood; displacement; sexual violence in displacement settings; and the targeting of female students, teachers and health workers, including the attempted killing of Malala Yousafzai and attacks on girls' schools). Baseline data: female literacy 37 per cent vs male 65 per cent; women constitute 51 per cent of the unemployed; and the merged districts show the province's lowest female schooling (KP Youth Policy 2026; KP Government 2024).

**Policy instruments.** KP has a Women Empowerment Policy Framework (2017) and continuing programs (UN Women's gender-responsive justice work in KP; gender-inclusive service rules reform; women's police stations and desks in some districts; the KP Commission on the Status of Women). The National Security Policy (2022–26) articulated gender security ("full and meaningful participation of women in decision-making, law enforcement, justice, and peacekeeping") — a commitment whose implementation in KP remains limited.

**Women in peacebuilding.** Documented roles: PAIMAN's mothers' networks; UN Women's engagement of **228 Afghan and Pakistani women peace activists** in cross-border dialogue processes (2025); women's organizations (Aurat Foundation, Khwendo Kor, Aware Girls, women's wings of political parties); and female health workers and teachers whose daily work in insecure districts is itself a peacebuilding function. The constraints: cultural gate-keeping; security threats; under-funding; and shrinking civic space.

**Assessment.** The study's gender findings are three: (i) women's *security* deficits are a measurable drag on normalization (a province in which half the population cannot move, work, or seek justice freely has not normalized; the 5 per cent secondary-school completion rate for girls in the NMDs is a structural emergency); (ii) women are *effective* peace actors whose networks reach where police cannot; and (iii) the policy architecture treats gender as a cross-cutting accessory rather than a security-relevant capability — with a corresponding loss of effectiveness in both development and prevention programs.

## 10.9 Tourism as a Normalization Indicator: Swat, Chitral, Dir and the Tourism Revival

**The record.** Tourism in KP has become a genuine and measurable indicator of normalization: **12.4 million tourists visited KP in 2025** (KP Culture and Tourism Authority); over Eid-ul-Adha 2026, KP received **more than 1.26 million tourists — the highest recorded — including 416,794 in Swat** (the single highest provincial destination count), with Naran (187,954), Galiyat (148,828), and 394,682 visitors to merged-district destinations including South Waziristan (144,000) and Orakzai (92,112); tourism revenue over the holiday was estimated at **Rs 8–12 billion** (DGIPR 2026). Comparable holidays in 2025 recorded 932,000 visitors (Eid-ul-Adha) and 306,215 (Eid-ul-Fitr). The government attributes the growth to security improvement, tourism-police deployment and infrastructure.

**But: is it a leading or lagging indicator?** Tourism measures *perceived* security among travellers and depends on mobility, infrastructure and marketing: it can thrive in districts with acute rural insecurity elsewhere (Swat's 2026 attack killed 17 people weeks after record visitor numbers). The correct reading is that tourism revival demonstrates *recoverability* — the social and economic capacity of a district to rebound — which is itself a strategic asset: a district with hotels, jobs and visitors has a constituency and a fiscal stake in normality. The 2026 Swat attack (Kabal; 17 killed) is therefore not only a security setback but an economic attack on a recovery model; its aftermath — whether bookings collapse, whether security forces visibly protect the sector — will indicate the resilience of the recovery.

**Practical agenda.** Tourism protection (the Tourism Police; protective infrastructure; coordination with hotel associations); infrastructure resilience (roads, Gilgit-style check-in systems, weather risk); diversification beyond the top three destinations; and — critically — linking tourism development to merged-district employment (guides, transport, homestays, handicrafts), so that the peace dividend is visible where the conflict has been.

## 10.10 Agriculture, Livelihoods and Rural Economic Recovery

**The agricultural base.** KP's rural economy rests on: wheat, maize, rice (in the Peshawar valley), orchards (Swat, Hazara, Chitral — apples, peaches, citrus), livestock (a major income source, especially for the NMDs), and off-farm labour and remittances. The sector's constraints: smallholder fragmentation; water scarcity and irrigation inefficiency (with the Chashma Right Bank Canal proposed as a transformational project); market access constraints (roads, cold chains, credit); the collapse of horticulture exports at times of border closure and of transport disruption; and the catastrophic effects of floods (2022; 2025's Buner/Swat/Shangla destruction).

**Conflict effects.** Conflict and militancy damage agriculture through: displacement and abandoned land; extortion and "taxation"; the destruction of orchards as punishment (documented in Swat in the militant era); insecurity of transport; the loss of markets in the border belt; and the destruction of the livestock economy through operations and flooding.

**Programs.** Provincial and donor programs include KP-RETP (World Bank-supported), the AIP's agriculture components, livestock vaccination programs, and various value-chain initiatives (olive, honey, dry fruit in the NMDs). The evidence on post-operation agricultural recovery in Swat is broadly positive (orchards rehabilitated; markets restored), in the NMDs much weaker (capital destruction, unresolved land rights, weak marketing).

**Assessment.** Land and water rights regularization in the merged districts is the single most important agricultural policy task, and the most politically difficult: without documented rights, neither investment nor credit nor insurance can function, and disputes will continue to convert into conflict (Chapter 3). The second priority is *market access*: roads, cold storage, and border trade regimes that allow the NMDs' fruit and livestock to reach markets in Afghanistan, the Gulf and Karachi.

## 10.11 CPEC and Economic Zones in KPK: Economic Promise and Security Challenges

**Status.** The Rashakai Special Economic Zone (Nowshera) is KP's flagship CPEC industrial project: 1,000 acres; announced with expectations of 200,000 direct and indirect jobs and ~Rs 347 billion in investment; Century Steel as the first enterprise (with a planned investment raised from an initial $50 million to a reported $360 million); by September 2026, **22 enterprises in progress, ~Rs 68 billion in estimated investment, and ~3,000 expected jobs**, with a mobile-phone manufacturing unit inaugurated that month (cpecinfo 2021; Daily Ittehad 2026). Other KP elements: the Karakoram Highway realignment; hydel projects; and fibre-optic connectivity (a somewhat stalled CPEC component).

**The gap between promise and performance.** Rashakai's actual trajectory — from "200,000 jobs" to ~3,000 expected — illustrates CPEC's KP reality: infrastructure-heavy, employment-light, and slower than promised. Constraints include: security risk perceptions (which raise insurance and security costs); electricity pricing and connectivity; the federal-provincial division of industrial policy; land and utility provision delays; and the narrow export orientation of relocated Chinese firms (with currency and profit-repatriation constraints).

**Security challenges.** CPEC projects and personnel in KP face: attack risk (the 2024 Shangla attack killing five Chinese engineers was the most serious); the requirement for expensive protective deployments; labour-community tensions (wages, local employment quotas); and the broader geopolitical risk that any major incident forces project suspension.

**Assessment.** SEZs can contribute to normalization (jobs, skills, tax base, visible modernity), but their security requirements (guarded enclaves, protected convoys) can also *entrench* the geography of exception, producing "islands of development" disconnected from local economies. The policy target should therefore be: local employment quotas enforced, supplier linkages to local firms, and security arrangements that are visible but not socially corrosive.

## 10.12 The Development–Security Nexus: Theoretical and Empirical Analysis

**Three competing models.** (i) *Sequencing model* (security first): development cannot occur without security; therefore, establish security, then invest. This was the de facto Pakistani approach post-2009. (ii) *Development-first model*: poverty and injustice cause conflict; invest in development and security follows. This model's failure in the FATA of 2003–2019 is documented: development spending (often politically captured) without security and justice did not prevent the insurgency. (iii) *Co-production model (this study's position)*: security and development are mutually constituting — each enables and is enabled by the other — but they are *mediated by institutions*: specifically by (a) justice (the mechanism by which citizens decide the state is a better provider of order than armed actors), (b) fiscal institutionalization (predictable resources make services credible), and (c) local political accountability (which determines whether development is delivered or captured).

**Empirical evidence from KP.** Several natural comparisons support the co-production reading. *Swat post-2009*: security restoration plus investment plus tourism revival produced the most complete normalization in the province — but 2026's attack shows its vulnerability to external sanctuary dynamics. *The NMDs post-2018*: legal integration without *fiscal* integration produced neither security nor development. *Bajaur*: repeated operations since 2008 displaced populations with limited follow-through; the district remains violence-prone despite its market recovery. *Malakand's recovery vs the southern belt's deterioration*: where the state paired force with returning services and civil administration, violence fell; where it did not, it rose.

**The conflict-cost calculation.** Pakistan's cumulative economic losses from terrorism have been estimated at **USD 126.79 billion (2017–18 Economic Survey accounting)** to **USD 150–152 billion (parliamentary and official statements)** across the war-on-terror period, with some analysts estimating an annual GDP cost around 3 per cent; and the human cost is over 70,000 civilian/security deaths nationally (Economic Survey of Pakistan 2017-18; Arab News 2021; Stratagem 2015). For KP specifically, the losses are concentrated: destroyed industry and tourism (now recovering), displacement, the fiscal burden of security (Rs 170 billion annually on police alone, plus the military deployment costs), and — hardest to quantify — the foregone human capital of two generations. Conversely, the *dividend* of normalization is visible in the same data: tourism at 12.4 million visitors; Rashakai's investment; the resumption of markets. A defensible order-of-magnitude estimate — developed in Chapter 15 — places KP's annual conflict-related economic loss in the range of **Rs 400–800 billion** (security expenditures, foregone investment, displacement, and destroyed capital), against which the fiscal settlement of the merged districts (~Rs 192 billion per year promised) is not merely affordable but economically rational: it is a fraction of the cost of conflict.

**Policy conclusion.** Development is not a substitute for security, and security is not a substitute for development. The binding constraint in KP is *institutional*: the state must demonstrate, in the same districts where it fights, that it can protect, adjudicate, and deliver — simultaneously, durably, and with resources it has promised. Anything less will continue to produce what the data record: recovery followed by relapse.


# CHAPTER 11 — GOVERNANCE, POLICY FRAMEWORKS AND LEGAL ARCHITECTURE

## 11.1 The National Action Plan (NAP): Objectives, Implementation and Assessment

**Origins and content.** The NAP was adopted in December 2014 in the aftermath of the Army Public School (APS) attack (141+ killed, 132 of them children) — Pakistan's most consequential terrorism event, whose political effect was comparable to 9/11 for the United States or the 2005 London bombings for the UK. The original twenty points combined kinetic measures (military operations, counter-terrorism forces), legal measures (military courts through the 21st Amendment; proscription of armed groups; prosecution), regulatory measures (madrasa reform; choking terrorist financing; curbing hate speech and the use of media/internet), and political-administrative measures (reconciliation in Balochistan; FATA reforms; Karachi operation; provincial coordination through NACTA). The 2021 revision reduced the framework to fourteen points and added emphases on the cyber domain, criminal-justice reform, and merged-district reform.

**Implementation record (assessed).** *Strong:* kinetic operations (Zarb-e-Azb and successors); counter-financing (FATF-era compliance: asset freezes, Schedule IV designations, thousands of accounts blocked; Pakistan's 2022 exit from the FATF grey list); proscriptions (65 organizations listed, 4 under observation, and thousands of individuals under Schedule IV at latest public counts); cyber takedowns (over 1.3 million URLs blocked by 2023); police/CTD capacity expansion (with provincial budgets rising); and military courts' throughput (thousands convicted, though with fair-trial concerns). *Weak/partial:* madrasa reform (reversed in 2024); terrorism trials (2,273 pending as of November 2024; 657 death sentences awarded 2019–2022 with no executions during that period); merged-areas reform (fiscal failure, Chapter 5); reconciliation in Balochistan (rated unsatisfactory in successive reviews); and the soft/preventive agenda generally (deradicalization, counter-narrative, reintegration). *Negative:* restrictions on media and civil society adopted under NAP rubric (journalist harassment; NGO NOC regimes; the PTM ban).

**Assessment.** NAP's architecture was sound and its kinetic implementation real; its central deficiency was *asymmetry*: the measures that could be delivered by force and administrative order were implemented; those requiring political bargaining, fiscal commitment and institutional development were not. The ISPR's own public position — that the NAP "has not been fully implemented" — aligned with this study's reading (Pakistan Connect 2025; CSUN/CUNI dissertation evidence; TFT 2026).

## 11.2 National Internal Security Policy (NISP): Evolution and Effectiveness

**Evolution.** The NISP (first issued 2014, followed by the National Security Policy 2022–2026) sought to frame internal security as an integrated enterprise: counter-terrorism; counter-extremism; border management; cyber security; and the "whole-of-nation" approach conjoining economic, human and military security. The 2022 National Security Policy was notable for its explicit "comprehensive" framing — including a commitment to gender security and economic security as core national-security elements.

**Effectiveness.** The policies articulated the right ambitions but suffered from three defects: (i) *no binding implementation mechanism* — they lacked legal mandates, budgets, and accountability for line ministries/provinces; (ii) *no public monitoring* — performance reported episodically and selectively; and (iii) *no political consensus mechanism* — the policies were federal-executive documents, and they were overtaken from 2022 by the PTI–federal conflict, which made a national consensus on internal security impossible in the period when it was most needed.

**Assessment.** NISP's value is as an *inventory of intentions* against which KP's current arrangements can be measured (and found wanting): border management without a border regime; cyber security without data protection; counter-extremism without scale; and gender security without implementation.

## 11.3 The Provincial Security Architecture: Apex Committees, Security Briefings and Coordination Mechanisms

**Institutions.** The provincial security architecture comprises: (i) the **Provincial Apex Committee** (chaired by the Chief Minister, with the Corps Commander, Chief Secretary, IGP, and other stakeholders), the principal civil-military coordination forum, replicated at district level in some cases; (ii) the **Home and Tribal Affairs Department**, the administrative anchor; (iii) the **KP Police** and CTD (Chapter 7); (iv) the **provincial cabinet's security-relevant committees**; (v) ad hoc **jirga/peace-committee structures**; and (vi) **federal mechanisms** operating in the province (the National Security Committee; NACTA; the military's operational HQs).

**Performance.** The Apex Committee has been the site of both coordination and conflict: coordination in discrete episodes (operations, refugee repatriation planning, Kurram crisis management); conflict in the public disputes over Azm-e-Istehkam (2024) and Operation Sarbakaf (2025), where the provincial government's opposition was overridden by federal/military decisions; and in the post-October 2025 government of CM Sohail Afridi, whose public positions have included opposition to military operations and demands for withheld funds (Wikipedia 2026e; Dawn 2024a; Paradigm Shift 2025).

**Assessment.** The architecture has no *published doctrine* — no document defines who decides what, under what legal authority, with what oversight, and with what reporting obligations. Coordination therefore depends on personalities and on the balance of power between Peshawar and Rawalpindi — a fragile basis for a decade-long campaign. The recommendation (Chapter 15) is a *statutory provincial counter-terrorism coordination framework* with defined roles, published metrics, and legislative oversight.

## 11.4 The Role of the Provincial Assembly and Legislative Measures

**The record.** The KP Assembly has been an active legislator on security-adjacent matters: the **KP Police Act 2017**; the **Levies and Khasadar** legislation (2019); amendments to the local government law; the extension of provincial laws to the merged districts; the **KP Universities/Education** laws; and various criminal-justice measures. It has also functioned as a forum for opposition demands on security (the PML-N white paper of September 2026; JUI-F's positions on operations; the opposition's challenges to the October 2025 chief-ministerial transition).

**Limits.** Three constraints have limited legislative influence: (i) *policy primacy of the executive/military*: war and internal-security decisions are made federally, with the Assembly informed rather than consulted (the Azm-e-Istehkam episode); (ii) *capacity*: few members have security expertise; committee systems are weak; and there is no dedicated, resourced security committee with research support; and (iii) *politicization*: post-2022, provincial-national politics turn nearly every security question into a partisan dispute (operations; refugee policy; the PTM), making sustained oversight difficult.

**Assessment.** A stronger Assembly role is not merely a democratic nicety; it is a functional requirement: legislative authorization and oversight protect the police from political interference, give operations legitimacy, and force the publication of the metrics (casualties, compensation, convictions) that accountability requires.

## 11.5 Judicial System and Rule of Law in KPK: Challenges and Reforms

**Structure and performance.** KP's judiciary comprises the Peshawar High Court (with circuit benches), district and sessions courts, and special courts (ATCs; anti-corruption; accountability; family; consumer). Documented problems: massive pendency and delays (national ATC pendency of 2,273 cases as of November 2024 being the counter-terrorism subset); inadequate court infrastructure and security; insufficient judges (vacancy rates in district judiciary); poor investigation quality (the binding constraint on convictions); legal-aid scarcity; witness intimidation; and the *informal justice* parallel system — jirgas, *riwaj*, and *qisas/diyat* settlements — which handles the bulk of disputes in the merged districts and much of rural KP. The Peshawar High Court has been notably active on merged-district rights (the NFC shortfall litigation; the Sehat Card petitions; the October 2025 chief-ministerial transition case).

**Counter-terrorism adjudication.** ATCs try cases under the ATA; their conviction rates are low nationally; appeals face delays; and the *military court* alternative has absorbed a significant number of cases (May 2023 events; terrorism charges against civilians). The 21st Amendment's military courts were repeatedly extended; the 27th Amendment's constitutional restructuring has shifted the appellate landscape. At the same time, the constitutional right to fair trial (Article 10A, inserted in 2010) and the documented complaints about military-court procedures (no charge sheets, verdict documents withheld until after appeal deadlines — matters litigated in the PHC in 2025) raise a structural conflict between counter-terrorism's demand for speed/certainty and the constitution's demand for due process. The study's position: legitimate counter-terrorism requires *lawful* convictions, and the long-run legitimacy of the state's campaign depends on resolving this conflict through an effective civilian system (protected judges, witness protection, forensic capacity, and specialized procedures), not through avoidance.

## 11.6 Anti-Terrorism Legislation: Strengths, Weaknesses and Abuses

**The statute.** The Anti-Terrorism Act 1997 (as repeatedly amended — 2001, 2004, 2009, 2013, 2014, 2016, 2018, 2020s) defines terrorism broadly, establishes ATCs with special procedures (in-camera trials; restricted adjournments; admission of certain confessions; bail restrictions), creates the legal basis for proscription (Schedule I) and for "Fourth Schedule" restrictive measures, and provides for the CTDs. Supporting laws include PECA (2016, amended 2025), the Fair Trial Act 2013 (surveillance warrants), the Investigation for Fair Trial Act, and the KP Police Act.

**Strengths.** Swift procedures; specialized courts; robust proscription powers; asset-freezing and financing tools; and the legal wherewithal for CTD operations — the essential legal infrastructure of a counter-terrorism campaign. International benchmarking (FATF) relied on these powers, and Pakistan's exit from the grey list (2022) reflected the system's formal compliance.

**Weaknesses and abuses.** Five are documented. (i) *Definitional breadth*: the ATA's terrorism definition can capture ordinary crimes, protests and political expression (a pattern visible in May 2023 prosecutions and in cases against journalists and activists). (ii) *Procedural erosion*: in-camera hearings, confessions, and restricted appeals reduce defendant safeguards; the Supreme Court/Lahore High Court have struck down elements at times. (iii) *Selective application*: groups and individuals are treated unequally according to political context (the PTM case is the sharpest recent example; Kashmir-focused groups' historical differential treatment is the oldest). (iv) *Preventive detention practice*: detention without charge under security laws is widely documented (and contested) in KP, including of individuals later released without proceedings. (v) *Military courts' expansion*: the transfer of civilian cases to military courts raises due-process issues that have been litigated and criticized (with two Supreme Court judges resigning in protest over the 27th Amendment's judicial restructuring, warning that it subordinated the judiciary to the executive).

**Assessment.** The legal architecture is adequate for prosecution but has been used for *governance by exception* — treating security as a domain exempt from ordinary rights and oversight. This produces short-term operational freedom and long-term legitimacy costs that the counter-insurgency demonstrably cannot afford (Chapters 6, 12).

## 11.7 National Counter Terrorism Authority (NACTA): Mandate, Performance and Critique

**Mandate.** NACTA (NACTA Act 2013) is charged with coordinating national counter-terrorism policy: threat assessment; coordination of federal and provincial agencies; proscription/Schedule IV maintenance; counter-financing (the National Task Force with 27 member organizations); and counter-narrative/deradicalization coordination.

**Performance.** Achievements: the establishment of Counter-Financing of Terrorism (CFT) units in provincial CTDs; rationalization of proscription lists and freezing of assets (thousands of accounts); coordination of the National Narrative document; and a convening function. Deficiencies: chronic under-staffing (the agency's own 2018 report found approximately three-quarters of approved posts unfilled; subsequent years did not resolve the professional-staffing deficit); limited budget authority; the absence of trained analysts in CT, financing and strategic assessment; and a *structural* limit: NACTA coordinates agencies that do not report to it and provinces that have their own mandates. The appointment in 2026 of a serving Major-General (Faisal Naseer) as head was presented as an effort to give coordination "teeth" — an understandable response to the capacity critique, but one that also underscores the pattern of military leadership of nominally civilian coordination bodies.

**Critique and reform.** The credible reform agenda for NACTA is: (i) professionalize the secretariat with competitively recruited civilian analysts; (ii) give it statutory authority to require data from provinces and agencies, plus a public annual threat report; (iii) separate its coordination role from operational mandates; and (iv) place counter-narrative and deradicalization functions in a dedicated, funded directorate with evaluation capacity. Without these, NACTA will remain, in the assessment of successive Pakistani and international reviews, "a good idea, poorly developed and executed."

## 11.8 Local Government System and Security Governance at the District Level

**Legal framework.** KP's local government system (the KP Local Government Act 2013, replaced/amended in 2019 and after) provides for district, tehsil and village/neighbourhood councils with devolved functions (some services, minor development). Elections were held in 2021–22 (PTI leading; JUI-F strong in parts; independents prominent) with by-elections in 2023 and 2026.

**Security governance at the district level.** District security governance operates through the Deputy Commissioner (administration, relief, coordination), the District Police Officer (policing), the district intelligence coordination committee, and — where they exist — elected councils and peace committees. The district is the level at which: relief and compensation are distributed; curfews and Section 144 orders are imposed; peace jirgas are convened; and, in principle, local buy-in is built. Its weaknesses: resource dependence on the province; absence of real budgetary authority for councils; politicized DC–DPO relations; and, in the NMDs, the absence of functioning local government altogether.

**Assessment.** Local government is the missing tier in KP's security architecture. Comparative evidence (Chapter 13) shows that local legitimacy — not merely force — determines whether cleared areas stay cleared. Elected local bodies with security-relevant functions (dispute resolution, community policing oversight, relief coordination, and local development budgets) create the intermediaries and accountability that KP lacks. The repeated deferral of meaningful local government in KP (both settled districts and NMDs) is, in this study's assessment, one of the least-examined contributors to the province's recurrent insecurity.

## 11.9 Political Party Dynamics and Their Impact on Security Policy

**The party map.** KP's politics features: PTI (dominant in provincial government since 2018 and hegemonic in the merged districts; in sustained conflict with the federal government and the military establishment since 2022–2023); JUI-F (strong religious-political network, particularly in the southern districts and parts of the NMDs; ambivalent or opposed on operations; pivotal on madrasa policy); ANP (Pashtun nationalist; historically supportive of operations against the TTP in 2009, now critical of military-led policy); PML-N (rising in some districts; author of the 2026 security white paper against the provincial government); PPP (in government federally with PJDP allies; weaker in KP); Jamaat-e-Islami (urban vote; strong on social services); PTM (proscribed; significant popular resonance among Pashtun youth). 

**Effects on security policy.** Three dynamics matter. (i) *The PTI–establishment conflict* has made counter-terrorism cooperation between the province and the centre conditional and contested; the KP government's opposition to Azm-e-Istehkam and its demands for withheld funds are the practical expression. (ii) *Federal-provincial partisanship*: a PTI-governed province, a PML-N-led centre, and an army-led strategy produce a triangular blame game in which responsibility for failures is diffused (and intelligence-sharing degrades, per ICG 2026). (iii) *Electoral incentives on operations*: operations and displacement are locally unpopular; opposition to operations wins votes in the affected districts — an incentive structure that will persist until security policy delivers *protective* dividends that constituents can see (funded compensation, rebuilt homes, protected schools).

**Assessment.** Political competition, properly channelled, is not an obstacle to normalization but a condition of it — provided that (a) security policy has a legislative and accountability framework within which contestation can occur, and (b) the fiscal settlement removes zero-sum fights over resources. The status quo — where security policy is commanded federally but costed provincially, and where the province's democratically elected government is excluded from strategic decisions — converts security into a partisan battleground at the exact moment unity is required.

## 11.10 Federal–Provincial Coordination: Achievements and Tensions

**Achievements.** Formal coordination exists: the National Security Committee; NACTA; the Apex Committee mechanism; federal fiscal transfers (NFC); and inter-provincial police cooperation (though undocumented). Specific cooperative successes include: the 2014–2018 campaign coordination; counter-financing and FATF compliance; and refugee repatriation planning.

**Tensions.** Four are structural: (i) *fiscal*: the merged districts' shortfall; withheld dues; the provinces' 2026 agreement to forgo incremental FBR collection to the federation; and the general under-funding of provincial security mandates; (ii) *policy authority*: operations are decided federally; the province's elected government bears the political cost; (iii) *legal/administrative*: federal agencies' role in the NMDs' administration (SAFRON legacies; federal PSDP projects) versus provincial authority; and (iv) *political*: the PTI-federal conflict; the refugee-repatriation policy (federal decision; provincial implementation); and the dispute over the October 2025 chief-ministerial transition (PHC adjudication).

**Assessment.** Federal-provincial coordination in KP is not a technical problem; it is a *constitutional design* problem: the province is expected to deliver internal security (a provincial subject) in a conflict whose strategy is set federally and whose fiscal burden is only partially recognized. The remedy — a statutory fiscal and coordination compact with defined responsibilities, guaranteed transfers, and joint public reporting — is discussed in Chapter 15.

## 11.11 The Role of the National Security Committee and National Security Adviser

**The NSC.** The National Security Committee (chaired by the Prime Minister; comprising the service chiefs and senior ministers; supported by the National Security Division and an NSA/Adviser on National Security) is Pakistan's apex security policy forum. In KP's security, the NSC's role has included: endorsing Azm-e-Istehkam (2024); authorizing/adjusting the Afghan policy (including the resort to cross-border operations in 2026); and framing the counter-terrorism narrative ("Fitna al-Khawarij" terminology; the "hostile agencies" framing). The NSC publishes little; its decisions are conveyed through ISPR and press releases.

**Critique.** The NSC's opacity and its military-heavy composition (with the 27th Amendment's CDF reforms further centralizing military authority) limit both civilian and provincial input into decisions that determine KP's fate. Comparative practice suggests two reforms: (i) formal inclusion of provincial chief executives in decisions on operations within their provinces; and (ii) public summaries of decisions and frameworks — not operational detail, but decision-affecting frameworks. Absent both, federal–provincial estrangement is structural, not accidental.

## 11.12 Policy Coherence and Implementation Gaps: A Critical Assessment

**The implementation gap as the master variable.** Across every domain examined in this chapter, the pattern is the same: articulate policy; partial implementation; reversal or erosion. The causes are identifiable: (i) *no binding mechanism* (policies without statutes, budgets or reporting); (ii) *fiscal fragility* (programs dependent on discretionary allocations that evaporate under fiscal pressure — the merged districts being the outstanding example); (iii) *political discontinuity* (each government re-announces rather than continues); (iv) *institutional interest conflicts* (madrasa boards versus the state; federal agencies versus the province; cops versus CTD versus military); and (v) *absence of evaluation* (almost no policy in the KP security domain has been independently evaluated; programs survive on claims, not evidence).

**Three policy deficits that most urgently need repair.** (1) *The fiscal deficit in security policy*: KP fights a national counter-insurgency on provincial resources; the merged districts lack the resources promised at merger. (2) *The accountability deficit*: no reliable public data on operations, casualties, convictions, or program outcomes; no functioning parliamentary oversight of security. (3) *The legitimacy deficit*: policy instruments that criminalize political dissent (PTM; journalists) and collective punishment practices undermine the state's claim to be protecting the very populations it asks to help.

**Design principle for the repair.** The consistent lesson of this chapter is that KP's governance system is strong on *command* and weak on *legitimacy, accountability and fiscal predictability*. A normalization framework must therefore invert the current bias: fewer operational announcements; more statutory, funded, measurable commitments; and a public, annual normalization report by the provincial and federal governments jointly — with the KP Assembly and the public as its audience.


# CHAPTER 12 — ROLE OF CIVIL SOCIETY, MEDIA AND NON-STATE ACTORS

## 12.1 Civil Society Organizations Working on Peace and Security in KPK

**The landscape.** KP's peace-oriented civil society comprises five clusters: (i) *PVE and peace-education organizations* (PAIMAN Alumni Trust, PEAD Foundation, Khwendo Kor, Aware Girls, and a network of smaller district-level groups); (ii) *human rights and legal-aid organizations* (the Human Rights Commission of Pakistan's KP chapters; the Legal Aid Society's local partners; women's legal-aid groups); (iii) *media and research organizations* (journalists' safety networks; research groups producing district-level reporting); (iv) *victims' and survivors' groups* (including families of the APS attack; displaced persons' associations such as Tehreek Mutasireen Tirah; and martyrs' family associations); and (v) *community-based and faith-based organizations* (welfare societies, zakat committees, mosque-linked charities, and youth clubs).

**Contributions.** Documented contributions include: mothers' networks and family-based counselling against radicalization (PAIMAN); peace education in schools and madrasas; psychosocial support for conflict-affected children; practical support to displaced persons; victim documentation (including the documentation of drone-strike and operation casualties); monitoring closed schools and girls' education restrictions; advocacy for de-mining; and the provision of the *evidence base* that policy discussions consume (including the district-level human development series by SDPI and the incident monitoring of civil-society-linked researchers).

**Constraints.** Five are structural: (i) *funding insecurity* — dependence on short project grants, with periods of abrupt suspension (including the 2018–2020 USAID/development-funding disruptions and the subsequent contraction of international development financing); (ii) *legal-administrative friction* — NOC requirements, registration conditions, and complex reporting mandated for foreign-funded organizations; (iii) *smearing and intimidation* — organizations critical of military conduct or government policy face campaigns, and staff face threats from both militants and (in some documented cases) state actors; (iv) *physical risk* — attacks on NGO offices, and the killing or abduction of aid workers; and (v) *shrinking space* generally (see §12.6).

**Assessment.** Civil society's comparative advantage in KP is *access* — to women, children, households and displaced communities that neither police nor army can reach — and its comparative weakness is *scale and sustainability*: the sector is small, project-driven, and politically exposed. A normalization strategy that fails to protect and finance it loses its most credible preventers.

## 12.2 The Jirga System: Traditional Conflict Resolution and Its Modern Relevance

**What it is.** The jirga is a deliberative assembly for dispute resolution — from small local *marakas* to district and grand jirgas — grounded in Pashtun custom (*riwaj*/*tor*), with procedures that vary by locality, sect and district. Its strengths are recognized: speed, accessibility, legitimacy, cost, and context-sensitivity; its verdicts often include compensation (*swara*, *nazrana*), restitution and reconciliation, and its proceedings can repair relationships in ways that courts cannot. Its most consequential recent successes are in Kurram (the 2011 truce ending a multi-year conflict that killed ~2,000; the 14-point accord of January 2025; the May 2025 *Aman Teega*), in Swat's de-escalation episodes, in Tirah's return negotiations, and in hundreds of localized feuds.

**Its pathologies.** Documented critiques: (i) *exclusion* — women and youth are generally excluded from decision-making; (ii) *punitive norms* — verdicts have historically included practices that violate constitutional and human rights (forced marriage/compensation involving women; collective fines); (iii) *enforcement* — jirgas cannot execute decisions against armed actors and depend on state power they often distrust; (iv) *capture* — military, religious and political actors have used grand jirgas for legitimation, and some proceedings become instruments of political theatre; and (v) *legal pluralism* — where jirgas adjudicate criminal matters, they displace the statutory system and the rights it guarantees.

**Reform directions.** Comparative practice (Chapter 13) and Pakistani proposals converge on: formalizing jirgas as *mediators* rather than *adjudicators* (a role adjacent to the DRC model); mandatory inclusion of women's *nominated representatives* in civil disputes involving women; a prohibition on verdicts violating statutory rights; vetting of members; state support for implementation; and explicit disentanglement from terrorism prosecutions (no jirga amnesty for grave crimes). None of this is easy in the tribal belt, but the alternative — either criminalizing the jirga (destroying legitimacy) or ignoring it (entrenching illegality) — has failed.

## 12.3 Tribal Elders and Maliks: Declining Influence and Security Implications

**The collapse.** Between 2004 and 2014, militants systematically targeted tribal notables: hundreds of *maliks* and elders were assassinated (with jirga massacres in the NMDs that killed dozens at a time), driven out, or forced to surrender their positions; the state, meanwhile, progressively suspended allowances and patronage as the Political Agent system wound down and the merger abolished it. Independent accounts describe a *generational erasure* of the intermediary class, with consequences that are still working through the system: absent interlocutors, absent community negotiators, and absent bearers of local memory and customary process.

**Who replaced them?** Four substitutes have emerged: (i) elected politicians (MPAs/MNAs from the merged districts) whose authority is electoral but not fiscal; (ii) *peace committee* figures (often younger, sometimes armed, and dependent on state security patronage); (iii) *religious* authority (clerics, madrasa heads) whose authority in some districts exceeds that of the state; and (iv) the militants' own "spokesmen" and enforcers — a role that completes the rupture of the old order.

**Security implications.** The absence of legitimate intermediaries has three effects: (a) *intelligence degradation* (no trusted local interlocutors to talk to); (b) *communication failure* (the state cannot negotiate or de-escalate locally without intermediaries); and (c) *transition risk*: post-operation settlements and return processes depend on local brokers whose legitimacy is contested — as in Tirah's 2026 return negotiations and Kurram's repeated accords, where jirgas succeed temporarily but their enforcement collapses without sustained state and community investment.

**Assessment.** Rebuilding legitimate intermediary capacity — through elected local government, formalized jirgas, protected elder networks, and women's and youth inclusion — is a precondition for the sustainability of any security gains. This is a *capability-building* task, not a nostalgic one; the old malik system was itself a colonial artifact, and its restoration is neither possible nor desirable.

## 12.4 The Role of Media: Conflict Reporting, Peace Journalism and Propaganda

**The media ecology.** KP hosts a dense media landscape: national outlets (Dawn, The News, Express Tribune, Geo, ARY, private TV) with substantial Peshawar bureaus; a vibrant regional press; radio (including Pashto broadcasts with wide rural reach); and a large online ecosystem. Its reports are indispensable to the historical record that this study relies upon.

**Practices and pressures.** (i) *Courage*: KP journalists have documented operations, displacement, casualties, refugee policy, and militant violence at enormous personal risk — KP and the merged districts have been among the world's most dangerous places for journalists, with dozens killed (including the 2021 killing of Shaheena Shaheen in Khyber and the long roster of casualties documented by press-freedom organizations). (ii) *Pressure*: reporting on military conduct, disappearances, and militant taxation has been restricted by official pressure, access denial, legal threats (PECA and ATA charges against journalists), and physical risk; the PTM ban further limited coverage through political prohibitions on the movement's activities. (iii) *Conflict-insensitive practice*: some coverage amplifies sectarian, ethnic and conspiracy framings; "Afghan" and "terrorist" are often conflated; the reporting of casualty claims by militant media (Umar Media claims of attacks and killing counts, republished widely) amplifies the militants' information objectives; and the construction of a *national security narrative* in some state-aligned outlets reduces space for evidence-based reporting on operations' civilian harm. (iv) *Peace journalism deficits*: solutions-oriented, victim-centred, non-sectarian reporting exists (e.g., reporting on Swat's recovery; the documentation of peace jirgas) but is not systematically supported.

**Assessment.** Media in KP is simultaneously a security actor (framing threat and policy), a restraint on abuses, and a vulnerability. The policy implications: protect journalists (transparent investigation of attacks on media); decriminalize professional reporting (resolve PECA/ATA misuse); and support peace journalism with training, safety and financing — especially in Pashto and the merged districts where trust deficits are deepest.

## 12.5 Social Media: Radicalization Tool or Peacebuilding Platform?

**The dual character.** Social media in KP plays both roles simultaneously. *(1) Radicalization and mobilization:* TTP and ISKP propaganda distribution (Chapters 3, 8); sectarian incitement (Kurram's 2024 escalation included a documented role for sectarian social media content, as the January 2025 agreement's clause on "those fomenting on social media" attests); recruitment through group chats; and — in a different register — the circulation of graphic images of violence that magnify terror's psychological effects. *(2) Peacebuilding and accountability:* social media has been central to peace mobilization (the 2022–23 Pashtun long marches; the Swat and Waziristan protest movements against militancy; village-level mutual-aid networks during floods; and displaced-persons' advocacy such as the Tirah committees). It has also been the principal channel through which the human cost of the conflict is documented and contested — including the #SwatSaniha mobilization after the June 2025 attack.

**Policy tension.** Pakistan's regulatory posture — platform blocking; PECA prosecutions for online speech; content takedown (1.3 million URLs by 2023) — is broad and insufficiently targeted: it has produced documented prosecutions of journalists and activists for lawful expression while failing to eliminate extremist content, which migrates to new platforms and channels. The counter-narrative and PVE fields need online presence and credible local voices, not blanket restrictions.

**Assessment.** No serious normalization strategy can ignore the information domain. The minimal credible framework: (i) targeted, reviewed, appealable removal of *incitement to violence* (with published criteria and transparency reporting); (ii) protection of lawful speech (repeal or reform of PECA's broad speech offences); (iii) support for Pashto/Urdu counter-narrative content produced by local credible voices; and (iv) digital literacy in schools and madrasas.

## 12.6 The Pashtun Tahafuz Movement (PTM): Demands, Dynamics and Security Discourse

**History and demands.** The PTM grew out of the Mahsud Tahafuz Movement (May 2014), founded by students to demand de-mining and compensation after operations in Waziristan; renamed in 2018, it became the largest Pashtun civil mobilization in decades through rallies across Pakistan demanding: an end to extrajudicial killings and enforced disappearances; removal of landmines; freedom of movement (checkpoint abuses); the release of the detained; and accountability for the human costs of counter-insurgency — with a rejected demand for a truth and reconciliation framework. Its mobilization capacity was demonstrated in 2018–2019 and again in the 2021–2023 "long marches" after enforced disappearances (the disappearance and killing of Nazimuddin and others).

**The ban and its context.** The PTM was proscribed in October 2024 under ATA Section 11B (Chapter 4); a prior attempt at legal action (2019) and provincial bans on its leadership predated it. The state's case against the PTM rests on: provocative rhetoric against state institutions; slogans offensive to the military's identity; alleged incitement; and the risk of ethnic polarization in a war zone. The movement and its defenders emphasize its non-violence, its rights-based demands, and the absence of any documented commission of terrorism by its leadership.

**Analytical assessment.** The PTM is the most important non-violent Pashtun political movement since the Khudai Khidmatgar, and its treatment is the clearest current test of whether Pakistan can distinguish between *militancy* and *dissent*. The ban has (a) removed a non-violent channel for grievance articulation; (b) criminalized significant parts of the Pashtun political spectrum, particularly youth; (c) complicated counter-insurgency's local intelligence and cooperation networks (the movement's supporters include the families whose cooperation the state needs); and (d) been widely read as confirming the movement's core claim (that the state treats Pashtun grievance as security threat). For normalization, the pragmatic question is not whether to accept all the PTM's rhetoric but whether a democratic state can afford to outlaw a non-violent political movement in the middle of a counter-insurgency: the evidence from KP says it cannot.

## 12.7 Religious Leaders and Institutions: Constructive and Destructive Roles

**Constructive roles.** Religious institutions in KP perform welfare functions (madrasas educating poor children), social mediation, and moral authority. Anti-militant religious voices have been consequential: the Paigham-e-Pakistan declaration (2018); wafaq board statements; clerics who officiated at the funerals of slain police and condemned terrorist attacks; the anti-polio-vaccine reversal by many clerics after erring in some areas; and the counter-narrative content used in deradicalization (Chapter 8).

**Destructive roles.** A subset of institutions and leaders has supplied ideological justification, recruits and logistics to armed groups (Chapter 4); the 2019 registration framework and its 2024 reversal illustrate the sector's political leverage; and sectarian clerics have stoked inter-communal conflict (Kurram; Hangu; parts of Peshawar).

**The policy choice.** The state's relationship with religious authority in KP oscillates between instrumentalization (partners in the counter-narrative) and confrontation (regulation). Neither pure approach has worked. The available evidence suggests a *conditional-partnership* model: fund and credential moderate, anti-militancy institutions; enforce transparency (financing, foreign students, curricula standards) through consultative mechanisms (the Punjab registration model); and hold accountable, through law, the institutions that cross into incitement — while avoiding blanket suspicion of the sector, which produces only defensiveness and non-compliance.

## 12.8 International NGOs and Development Partners in KPK

**Presence.** KP has hosted one of Pakistan's largest development-partner footprints: UN agencies (UNDP, UNICEF, UNHCR, WFP, UN Women, IOM, UNODC); IFIs (World Bank, ADB, IFAD); bilateral donors (US and UK historical funding; EU; Germany; Japan; China); and international NGOs (Save the Children, IRC, ACTED, Islamic Relief, and others). Programs have covered: displacement response; livelihood restoration; education; health (polio, nutrition); water and sanitation; governance; and peacebuilding/PVE (with UN Women, USIP and others funding pilot work).
