**Contributions and limitations.** Contributions: humanitarian response at scale; technical assistance in justice and police reform; evidence and monitoring (including casualty documentation and human development data); financing for education and health; and PVE pilot programming. Limitations: (i) *short-termism and projectization* — donor cycles rarely align with a decade-long normalization requirement; (ii) *absorption and access* constraints (security, government NOC requirements); (iii) *coordination gaps* with provincial priorities and between donors; (iv) *political sensitivities* around monitoring casualties and civil-society support; and (v) the *financing shift*: the global decline of development assistance (and the diversion of funds to other crises) has reduced INGO presence in KP's conflict districts precisely as needs grew.

**Assessment.** Development partners cannot substitute for the state's role in security and justice; but they hold a comparative advantage in *education, health, livelihood and protection programs* — the soft infrastructure of normalization — and in *independent evaluation*, which KP's security sector almost entirely lacks. Their most valuable contribution would be a coordinated, decade-horizon program focused on the merged districts, conditioned on the government's fiscal delivery.

## 12.9 Youth Activism and Civic Engagement for Peace

**Forms.** Documented youth activism includes: the PTM's youth base and its marches; Swat's protest movements (against militancy and against military operations in different periods — including the Pasoon network's organizing, which produced the rally during which the 2 August 2026 Kabal attack occurred); student unions (long banned and only recently partially restored) and campus civic groups; sports and arts movements (cricket in the tribal districts; the recently established literary festivals); the digital freelancers who have made Peshawar and Swat hubs of online work; and village-level volunteer networks during disasters (the flood responses of 2022 and 2025).

**Constraints.** Youth civic engagement faces: high unemployment; the collapse of student representative institutions; the risks of protest under security laws; distrust of parties; and the lack of funded platforms. Militancy competes for the same cohort with offers of salary, status and purpose.

**Assessment.** Youth activism is both the primary recruitment pool and the primary peace constituency: the same cohort that militancy targets is the one that fills peace marches, volleyball courts, and freelance training programs. Policy should therefore treat youth engagement as a *security program*: sports and cultural facilities (a cheap, high-visibility intervention); funded internship/training pipelines; protected student organizations; and support for youth-led civil society.

## 12.10 Women's Organizations and Gender-Inclusive Security

**Organizations.** KP's women's movement includes: large national organizations with provincial programming (Aurat Foundation, more than four decades active); local providers (Khwendo Kor); women-led PVE groups (Aware Girls under Gulalai Ismail, which faced state pressure and whose leadership relocated); the KP Commission on the Status of Women; women's wings of political parties; and informal networks of female teachers, health workers and madrasa graduates.

**Roles in security.** Women's organizations have: delivered psychosocial support and family counselling; run peace education and interfaith programs; documented violence against women and girls; advocated on girls' education and school closures; supported displaced women; and engaged in cross-border dialogue (UN Women's 228-participant Afghan–Pakistani women peacebuilder network). The National Security Policy's gender commitment provides a formal basis; implementation is minimal.

**Assessment.** The evidence from Pakistan and internationally indicates that women's participation strengthens both prevention programs and community-level mediation (particularly in family and youth radicalization cases). In KP, women's participation is constrained by security, convention and funding — yet the female cohort (37 per cent literacy; five per cent secondary completion in the NMDs) is simultaneously the province's most under-utilized peacebuilding asset. The specific recommendations: fund women-led PVE organizations with long-horizon grants; create safe mechanisms (women's councils, female jirga auxiliaries) to participate in dispute resolution; expand female police recruitment and women's desks (Chapter 7); and protect female students and teachers with targeted security measures (routes, escort, secure campuses).

## 12.11 Private Sector and the Business Community's Role in Normalization

**The business voice.** KP's business community — chambers of commerce (Peshawar, Swat, D.I. Khan, Mardan), sector associations (marble, hotels, transporters, mining, pharmacy), and the freelancer/digital economy (a growing export sector) — has consistently articulated the costs of insecurity: extortion, KFR, transport disruption, insurance and security costs, and loss of tourism and investment. Business associations have supported relief (flood and displacement response), engaged in peace advocacy, and occasionally brokered locally.

**Business as a security actor.** Three roles are documented: (a) *employers* — the primary mechanism of reintegration (Chapter 8), though few firms hire ex-combatants, citing risk; (b) *advocates* — chambers have pressed for infrastructure (roads, electricity, border trade reopening) and for the release of held-up funds; (c) *targets and funders* — extortion and protection payments make businesses both victims and, involuntarily, financiers of armed groups (a dilemma most firms cannot resolve individually and which requires collective state action).

**Assessment.** The private sector's stake in normalization is large but unstructured. The most valuable policy moves are: (i) *public-private security compacts* (joint vetting of threats; coordinated protection of corridors; support for tourism routes with insured security); (ii) *employment incentives* for hiring at-risk youth and rehabilitees (subsidies, certification, assurances); and (iii) *transparency against extortion* (a confidential reporting channel with police protection, breaking the silence that allows extortion networks to operate).

## 12.12 Building a Culture of Peace: Long-Term Social Transformation

**What is meant by "culture of peace" in KP.** Not sentimentality, but a set of institutionalized practices and public attitudes: rejection of violence as a political instrument; acceptance of sectarian and ethnic pluralism; education that teaches critical thinking and civic values; commemoration of all victims; and the availability of peaceful channels (courts, elections, unions, media) for the grievances that currently find violent outlets.

**What would build it.** Seven tested components: (1) *curriculum and teachers* — civic education, critical thinking, anti-sectarian content, teacher training; (2) *memorialization and truth* — public recognition of all victims, including of state operations, with the APS memorialization being a precedent for national unity around victimhood; (3) *the arts and sports* — the most scalable counter-recruitment instruments (music, Pashto literary revival, cricket and football leagues in violence-affected districts); (4) *media pluralism* — peace journalism, local language content, journalists' protection; (5) *religious and tribal institutions* — constructive roles formalized and funded; (6) *youth and women's participation* — as above; and (7) *visible justice* — nothing builds a culture of peace more than seeing perpetrators convicted and victims compensated (Chapter 7).

**The counterfactual risk.** The alternative to a culture of peace is a culture of endurance: a society in which violence is understood as normal, revenge as honourable, and the state as an unreliable protector. KP has elements of both cultures today; the balance between them over the next decade will determine the durability of whatever security gains are achieved. That balance is not determined by military operations; it is determined by schools, courts, compensation payments, media, and the honesty of public accounting for what has happened.


# CHAPTER 13 — COMPARATIVE ANALYSIS AND INTERNATIONAL LESSONS

## 13.1 Northern Ireland Peace Process: Power-Sharing, Decommissioning and Lessons for KPK

**The settlement.** The Good Friday/Belfast Agreement (1998) ended three decades of conflict (the "Troubles": ~3,500 deaths) through a three-stranded architecture: power-sharing in a devolved assembly; North–South and East–West institutions; and mutual concessions on prisoners, decommissioning and policing. Violence did not end in 1998 — dissident republicans and loyalists persisted, and the killings of the 2000s (including the 1998 Omagh bombing that killed 29) and the 2011 death of a police officer demonstrated the residual threat — but the political process held. Subsequent agreements (St Andrews 2006; the 2005 IRA decommissioning) consolidated it.

**Key mechanisms.** (i) *Inclusion of non-violent politics*: Sinn Féin and the loyalist parties were brought into constitutional politics; the state did not defeat the IRA militarily, nor did it demand ideological surrender — it demanded the end of violence and acceptance of the political process. (ii) *Police reform as a peace dividend*: the Patten Commission (1999) produced the most far-reaching police reform in the developed world — renamed service (PSNI), new recruitment quotas, human-rights-based training, an independent ombudsman and Policing Board. This is arguably the single most transferable element to KPK: the recognition that a security force seen as an instrument of one community cannot deliver normalization. (iii) *Prisoner releases and victim-centred legacy bodies*: early releases were traded for violence-ending; but legacy issues (collusion inquiries, victims' pensions, inquests) remain disputed, illustrating how *unresolved truth* can persist for decades. (iv) *Economic dividend and reconstruction*: investment (including EU peace programmes) helped normalize. (iv) *Third-party facilitation*: US, British and Irish governments as guarantors, plus independent monitors.

**Counterpoints.** Northern Ireland's conditions differ from KP's in crucial ways: the conflict was intra-provincial but within a single developed state; there was no cross-border sanctuary (though arms smuggling existed) and the "Kinahan" organized-crime overlap came later; and the British state's fiscal capacity dwarfed Pakistan's provincial budgets. Its principal relevance is therefore *not* the grand settlement but the three operational doctrines: police reform, inclusive political process, and sequenced prisoner/justice measures with monitoring.

## 13.2 Colombia's Peace Agreement with FARC: DDR Programs and Transitional Justice

**The settlement.** The 2016 Havana agreement between the Colombian government and the FARC ended a 52-year conflict (over 220,000 deaths). It comprised: (i) *rural reform* (land restitution, development); (ii) *political participation* (the FARC's transformation into a political party with guaranteed seats); (iii) *drugs policy* (crop substitution, voluntary eradication); (iv) *transitional justice* (the Special Jurisdiction for Peace, JEP; the Truth Commission; reparations); and (v) *DDR* — with around 13,000 combatants demobilized into reintegration zones, individual reintegration plans, income support, and monitoring.

**Outcomes and lessons.** (i) *DDR can work at scale, but its survival depends on security guarantees*: hundreds of former combatants were assassinated after demobilization (over 400 documented in the years following), and dissident factions (with coca-related interests) re-armed; the lesson for KP is precise: reintegration without protection (from former enemies, criminal groups and rogue state agents) fails. (ii) *Politics as the DDR's endpoint*: the FARC's conversion into a political party was contested domestically but provided a non-violent outlet for membership. (iii) *Partial implementation is corrosive*: delays in land reform and security guarantees drive dissidence and erode the public's faith in the agreement. (iv) *Justice with participation*: the JEP's mix of restorative and retributive sanctions is a model for contexts where blanket amnesty is politically impossible (as in KP, where victims' families would reject impunity) — but it requires enormous institutional investment and years of patience.

## 13.3 Sri Lanka's Post-LTTE Normalization: Military Victory and Political Reconciliation

**The case.** Sri Lanka's military defeat of the Liberation Tigers of Tamil Eelam (LTTE) in 2009 ended a 26-year civil war and eliminated the world's most effective insurgency's territorial control. The government's approach to post-war normalization combined: massive military presence in the north/east; infrastructure and economic development (especially in Colombo and the east); a Lessons Learnt and Reconciliation Commission (2011); and minimal political devolution. Reconciliation stalled; militarization persisted; the recommendations of accountability mechanisms were largely unimplemented; the 2019 Easter Sunday bombings (retaliatory-Islamic State-linked) and the 2022 economic collapse showed how the absence of a political settlement leaves structural vulnerabilities.

**Lessons for KP.** (i) *Military victory is not normalization*: Sri Lanka defeated the LTTE but did not end Tamil grievances; the absence of devolution and truth-telling produced a long-term legitimacy deficit that proved internationally costly. (ii) *Development without political settlement buys quiet for a while*. (iii) *The counterfactual of a "hard security" model* is that it can suppress insurgency but cannot produce durable peace — a warning equally applicable to any strategy of pure coercion in KP. Transposed to the province: even a successful campaign against the TTP would not automatically normalize Swat, the NMDs, or Kurram.

## 13.4 Iraqi Kurdistan's Security Transition: Peshmerga, Governance and Economic Development

**The case.** The Kurdistan Region of Iraq (KRI) built a functioning autonomous administration after 1991 (Safe Haven; no-fly zone), with its own Peshmerga forces, two-party system (KDP/PUK), and — from 2003 — substantial oil revenues. It has been more stable than central Iraq but faces: internal party-based security fragmentation (duplicated Peshmerga structures under KDP and PUK control); fiscal dependence on Baghdad transfers and oil; the 2017 independence referendum's disastrous aftermath (loss of Kirkuk; economic crisis); the ISIL war (2014–2017), in which Peshmerga fought; and long-standing governance deficits (corruption, delayed elections, patronage).

**Lessons for KP.** (i) *Autonomy/devolution can stabilize if it is fiscally real*: the KRI's stability rests on revenue-sharing (which it fought over) — an echo of the merged districts' fiscal shortfall. (ii) *Security-sector fragmentation along party lines entrenches instability*: the KDP–PUK duplication is a warning for any provincial force built on patronage — and for the NMD policing model (Chapter 7). (iii) *Internal political unity is a security precondition*: the KRI's biggest vulnerabilities were internal (2017 referendum; the 2013–15 budget standoff), not military — an analogy to the federal–provincial estrangement over KP's security (Chapter 11). (iv) *Oil/dependable rent economies are not a substitute for institutionalized statebuilding.*

## 13.5 Afghanistan's Ongoing Challenges: Lessons in What Not to Do

**The record, 2001–2026.** Afghanistan's post-2001 experience offers negative lessons unmatched in the contemporary record: (i) *DDR without a political settlement*: the Afghanistan Peace and Reintegration Programme (APRP) and earlier DDR efforts demobilized tens of thousands of combatants superficially while warlord networks — and their militias — retained power, ensuring that demobilization did not reduce armed politics (some DDR beneficiaries rejoined the Taliban). (ii) *Counter-insurgency dependent on foreign forces*: the ANSF collapsed within weeks of the allied withdrawal (August 2021), demonstrating that security forces built on external funding, airlift and logistics collapse when the patron leaves. (iii) *Corruption and parallel governance*: the Kabul government's legitimacy deficit — warlord capture, electoral fraud, and predatory local administration — handed the Taliban their strongest recruiting argument ("we deliver justice, they sell it"). (iv) *Sanctuary myopia*: the alliance's toleration of Taliban sanctuaries in Pakistan (2005–2021) and Pakistan's toleration of TTP sanctuaries in Afghanistan (2021–2026) show both how sanctuaries extend conflicts and how each side's short-term calculus lengthens the war. (v) *The peace process's design failure*: the US–Taliban Doha agreement (2020) excluded the Afghan government and traded withdrawal for promises rather than verification, producing the collapse of the government before any negotiated settlement.

**Lessons for KP.** The Afghan record is the study's principal caution against five fallacies: that security forces can be *rented* rather than built; that *reintegration* without politics and economics works; that *corruption* is a side-issue in counter-insurgency; that *sanctuaries* can be tolerated with impunity; and that *peace processes* designed to satisfy external actors will hold.

## 13.6 Mozambique and Sierra Leone: African Models of Post-Conflict Recovery

**Mozambique (1992 Rome General Peace Accords).** The accords between Frelimo and Renamo ended a 16-year civil war; UN-supported DDR demobilized ~90,000 combatants; multiparty elections (1994) brought Renamo into politics; and sustained growth followed. The success factors: a genuine mutual hurting stalemate; effective third-party mediation (the Sant'Egidio community, with UN and Italian). The cautionary suffix: Renamo's return to armed conflict (2012–2016) after protracted exclusion and electoral disputes in 2013–16, and the Cabo Delgado insurgency (from 2017) that revealed how a resource-rich periphery with weak governance can become a new conflict frontier. The KP relevance: demobilization success does not immunize against future insurgency if political exclusion and peripheral governance persist.

**Sierra Leone (1999 Lomé → 2002 end).** The rebel RUF's terror campaign (amputations, child soldiering) ended through a combination of British military intervention (2000), UN peacekeeping, DDR of ~70,000 combatants, a Truth and Reconciliation Commission, and a Special Court (which tried Charles Taylor and others) — a case demonstrating that hybrid justice (truth commission + court) with sustained external involvement can consolidate peace. The KP applicability is limited by scale and geopolitics but the *model* — local truth-telling plus selective judicial accountability — is relevant to Kurram, Swat and the NMDs, where impunity is pervasive and grievance accumulates.

## 13.7 The Philippines' Mindanao Peace Process: Autonomous Governance and Security

**The case.** The Mindanao conflict between the Philippine state and Moro insurgencies produced two settlements: with the Moro National Liberation Front (1996) and the far more consequential 2014 Comprehensive Agreement on the Bangsamoro with the Moro Islamic Liberation Front (MILF), institutionalized through the Bangsamoro Organic Law (2018) and the establishment of the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region (BARMM, 2019). Key mechanisms: (i) *asymmetric autonomy* with a regional parliamentary government, fiscal arrangements (block grants, tax powers) and specified competences; (ii) *phased decommissioning* of the MILF's 40,000-strong force — with donors providing transition payments — implemented in stages that continued after 2019; (iii) *third-party monitoring* (the International Monitoring Team, IMT, with Malaysian, Indonesian, Japanese and other participation; the Independent Decommissioning Body; the Third-Party Monitoring Team reporting publicly); (iv) *militia normalization* (dealing with private armed groups); and (v) *economic development* (roads, social services).

**Outcomes and lessons.** Progress has been real (violence declined among the main groups; BARMM has governed since 2019) but partial: residual jihadist groups (BIFF, IS-linked factions) exploited the transition; clan politics and electoral violence persist; the 2022 elections tested the transition; and the normalization annexes (decommissioning, disbanding private armies, policing) lagged implementation. **KP relevance** — the closest structural analogy available: (i) *autonomy with real fiscal transfers* is the analogue of the merged-district settlement: promised transfers that are not delivered undermine the political class the settlement creates; (ii) *third-party monitoring with public reporting* — an approach entirely absent from KP's merger implementation — creates accountability and confidence; (iii) *phased, funded decommissioning of armed groups* is the analogue of a future TTP disengagement process, should one ever be possible; and (iv) the residual-jihadist problem (BIFF/ISKP-analogue) persists in every post-settlement environment, requiring sustained counter-terrorism even after a political settlement.

## 13.8 Chechnya/Dagestan: Russia's Counter-Insurgency Model (Critical Assessment)

**The model.** Russia's North Caucasus campaigns (two wars in Chechnya: 1994–96, 1999–2009; continuous operations in Dagestan and Ingushetia) produced a form of stabilization that combined: overwhelming force (massive artillery and air campaigns; "cleansing" operations — documented mass abuses); "Chechenization"/"Kadyrovization" — transferring security to a local strongman, Ramzan Kadyrov, with a large militia/police apparatus funded from Moscow; extreme coercion plus selective social subsidy; and the suppression of all independent political activity, media and civil society. The measured outcome: open insurgency in Chechnya was largely defeated; extremist violence shifted to Dagestan/Ingushetia in the late 2000s and declined substantially by the 2010s — while producing: thousands killed and displaced; mass human-rights abuses (documented by Memorial and others, including "disappearances," torture, and persecution of LGBT individuals); a repressive, personality-based regional regime; and export of violence (the 2013 Boston Marathon bombing by Chechen-linked brothers; ISKP recruitment from the Caucasus).

**Critical assessment and relevance.** The Chechen case demonstrates that *militarily effective* counter-insurgency is achievable through extreme coercion — and that its costs include the destruction of the rule of law, the corruption of the state, exported violence, and the impossibility of measuring "success" against anything but the absence of armed self-reporting. It is not a transferable model for Pakistan for three reasons: (i) Pakistan is not an authoritarian federation able to impose a Kadyrov-type equilibrium without dismantling its own constitutional order (though the 27th Amendment's centralization moves in that direction); (ii) the Chechen analogue would require a monolithic local enforcer with consolidated power — which no single faction in KP possesses; (iii) the model leaves the recruitment narrative intact and merely suppresses its expression, producing periodic recurrence. Its *one* legitimate lesson: local security forces with local legitimacy (however manufactured) outperform foreign ones — but only if "local legitimacy" is built through consent, not terror.

**Dagestan's specifics** are arguably more relevant to KP: a region with a mixed ethnic composition, a Salafi-jihadist insurgency operating from rural and mountainous sanctuaries, an ethnic and governance mix, and an insurgency that was ultimately suppressed by a combination of special operations, amnesties, and co-opted religious authorities — with recurring resurgences whenever the co-optation or coercion lapsed.

## 13.9 Comparative Framework: Key Variables for Successful Security Normalization

| Variable | Evidence from cases | KP position (2026) |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Political settlement** | NI, Colombia, Mindanao, Mozambique (1992) succeeded where settlements created non-violent outlets; Sri Lanka, Chechnya & Afghanistan failed without them | Absent — no settlement; TTP excluded from politics; PTM banned |
| **2. DDR/reintegration** | Colombia & Mindanao: effective with financing, security guarantees, political integration; Afghanistan: failed without politics; Cabo Delgado: reconstruction ≠ reconciliation | Nascent/marginal (Chapters 8) |
| **3. Police & justice reform** | NI: the single most consequential reform; Colombia: rural justice gaps drove dissidence | KP Act 2017 progressive; implementation partial; NMD policing unresolved |
| **4. Economic dividend & reconstruction** | NI/Mindanao: consequential; Sierra Leone: partial; Mozambique (Cabo Delgado): insufficient | Swat recovered; NMDs' fiscal shortfall catastrophic |
| **5. Sanctuary management** | Afghanistan/Pakistan: sanctuary prolongs; Colombia: neighbours cooperated | Acute (Afghanistan); unresolved |
| **6. Inclusion & local legitimacy** | Mindanao autonomy, NI inclusion, KRI devolution vs Sri Lanka/Chechnya exclusion | NMD local government absent; PTM/PTM-aligned youth excluded |
| **7. Transitional justice/victims** | Sierra Leone (TRC+SC); Colombia (JEP); NI (legacy unresolved → 25+ years of dispute) | Absent; impunity widespread; no victims' process |
| **8. Third-party guarantees & monitoring** | Mindanao (IMT/TPMT); NI (guarantors); Mozambique (Sant'Egidio) | Episodic (Qatar/Türkiye/China mediation) without institution |
| **9. Time horizon** | All successful cases: 10–25+ years of sustained attention | Policy horizon is election-cycle; no long-horizon framework |
| **10. State coherence** | KRI fragmentation, Afghan corruption: internal incoherence lethal; NI required London–Dublin–Belfast alignment | Federal–provincial estrangement is acute |

**Synthesis.** Across cases, no variable is sufficient alone, and the *sequencing varies* — but the recurring pattern is: a *political framework* (settlement, autonomy, or inclusion) + *security-sector reform* + *economic dividend* + *legitimacy mechanisms* (justice/truth) delivered over a decade or more, with third-party monitoring. Where any element is missing, normalization stalls even when violence declines.

## 13.10 Transferable Lessons and Contextual Adaptations for KPK

**Lessons that transfer directly.**
1. **Police reform is the load-bearing wall** (Northern Ireland). KP cannot normalize while its police are simultaneously victims, occupiers and ineffective — the Patten-style agenda (rights-based training, community accountability, representative recruitment, independent oversight, professional investigations) applies with modifications to the KP Police Act 2017 framework and the NMD policing question.
2. **Reintegration must be protected and financed** (Colombia, Mindanao): ex-combatants who are killed, excluded or impoverished become the next insurgency. KP's deradicalization programs need security guarantees, employment, and community acceptance.
3. **Third-party monitoring creates accountability** (Mindanao): an independent monitoring body with public reporting — for the merged districts' fiscal commitments and for any security arrangement with Afghanistan — would address the implementation-gap pattern that defines KP's policy history.
4. **Fiscal delivery is the political class's lifeblood** (Mindanao/BARMM, KRI): promised transfers not delivered discredit the settlement's local champions; KP's Rs 568 billion shortfall is precisely this failure.
5. **Justice must be visible, but need not be retributive-only** (Sierra Leone, Colombia): hybrid approaches (local truth processes; selective accountability; reparations) can substitute where full trials are impossible — relevant to Kurram and to state-abuse victims.
6. **The security sector cannot be a rented service** (Afghanistan): the police and courts must be built as indigenous institutions with public legitimacy, not as donor-funded expeditionary capacity.

**Lessons requiring adaptation (not transfer).**
7. **Peace agreements with insurgents** (NI, Mindanao): KP's TTP is not a political movement with negotiable demands comparable to the IRA or MILF; any negotiation must deal with a coalition whose factions split (JuA 2026) and whose sponsorship base is external — so the *negotiation design* must be decentralized, conditional, verified, and staged (cessation → confidence-building → disengagement → reintegration), and must not repeat the failed pattern of unverified deals (2004–2008; 2021–22).
8. **Autonomy as a solution**: KP's challenge is not secession but integration; the Mindanao analogy is *fiscal devolution and local self-government*, not sovereignty.
9. **International intervention**: no external force will provide security in KP; the "third party" roles are monitoring, mediation, financing and evaluation — and those are available.

**The synthesis for policy.** KP's optimal pathway is a *hybrid* of the Northern Ireland (police/justice/inclusion), Mindanao (fiscal autonomy and monitoring), Colombia (reintegration and transitional justice), and Sierra Leone (truth plus selective accountability) models, adapted to the conditions of an entrenched, sanctuary-supported insurgency within a nuclear-armed federal state. Chapter 15 translates this into an implementable roadmap; Chapter 14 establishes its strategic context.


# CHAPTER 14 — SCENARIOS, PROJECTIONS AND RISK ASSESSMENT

## Methodological Note on Scenario Construction

The scenario method used here is *driver-based* rather than forecasting: rather than extrapolating trend lines, the analysis identifies the four variables with the greatest demonstrated causal weight on KP's security trajectory (established in Chapters 3–11) — (i) the Afghanistan security channel; (ii) the federal–provincial security compact; (iii) the merged districts' fiscal settlement and governance build-out; and (iv) the police protection and capability trajectory — and constructs alternative futures from their interactions. Probabilities are *subjective assessments* derived from historical base rates (KP's record of relapse; the failure rate of ceasefires; the repeat of operation cycles) and from current trend evidence (2026 data). They are offered to discipline thinking, not to claim precision.

## 14.1 Scenario 1: Optimistic Trajectory — Sustained Normalization and Peace Consolidation

**Probability: 15–20 per cent over 2026–2035 (under current leadership and resource conditions).**

**Narrative.** In this scenario, a conjunction of three favourable developments occurs within 24–36 months. First, the Pakistan–Afghanistan channel stabilizes: a mediated arrangement (Chinese/Qatari/Turkish/Saudi facilitation) establishes a monitoring mechanism with reciprocal commitments — TTP relocations away from the border, verified curbs on cross-border movement, prisoner exchanges, and trade/mobility normalization (Chapters 9, 15). The TTP's sanctuary dividend erodes gradually, and its operational tempo declines as leadership losses (already significant: 921 militants killed in Q3 2026 alone) accumulate without replacement from safe rear areas. Second, the fiscal settlement of the merged districts is delivered: following the Prime Minister's June 2026 assurance, the NFC revision process (or a presidential order under Article 160) creates a statutory, ring-fenced transfer of the promised 3 per cent (or an equivalent quantified package), and the 2028 expiry of the original commitment is pre-empted by a successor framework. The merged districts immediately see payrolls funded, ~6,000 promised police posts created, courts staffed, and road/health/school projects resumed. Third, the federal–provincial compact is repaired through a statutory coordination framework and a security-financing arrangement that eschews political blame games; the police receive protection equipment, counter-drone and counter-IED capability, and the casualty rate declines from the 2026 peak.

**Outcome indicators by 2030.** KP violence-linked fatalities fall below 500/year (vs 2,331 in 2025); police killed fall to double digits annually; attacks on the NMDs decline 60 per cent-plus; tourism exceeds 20 million provincial visitors; Rashakai and the NMDs' mineral sector generate visible employment; the merged districts' fiscal absorption reaches 80 per cent of allocated funds; and a functioning district judiciary is reported in all seven NMDs.

**Requisites (each necessary).** A verifiable Afghanistan arrangement; fiscal settlement legislated; leadership continuity across at least two electoral cycles; sustained external (donor) financing for reintegration and education; internal TTP fragmentation exploited; and no major India–Pakistan crisis.

**Assessment.** This scenario's low probability is a function of the number of independent conditions, not of their impossibility. Notably, its *most* contingent element is the fiscal settlement — the one item requiring no adversary's consent. That asymmetry is the strongest argument in the study for prioritizing it.

## 14.2 Scenario 2: Status Quo — Fragile Equilibrium with Periodic Violence

**Probability: 50–60 per cent — the central case.**

**Narrative.** Hostilities with Afghanistan oscillate between episodes of relative calm (ceasefires, mediation cycles) and strikes-and-retaliation cycles; the TTP retains its sanctuary and enough capacity to sustain 300–600 attacks per month across KP, with periodic spectacular attacks (a Kohat- or Swat-scale event every 6–18 months, an Islamabad-scale event every 1–3 years). The police continue to absorb losses (100–150 killed annually) with incremental equipment and technology improvements; IBOs continue to kill several hundred militants annually; the merged districts' fiscal shortfall is partially addressed (piecemeal releases; political bargaining) but not structurally resolved; local government remains stalled; and economic recovery proceeds unevenly (tourism resilient; investment cautious).

**Outcome indicators.** Annual KP violence-linked fatalities steady in the 1,400–2,500 band; deaths among security forces 300–500 annually; attacks on the police 600–900 annually; displacement episodes of 20,000–100,000 people recurring in the frontier districts; education and health indicators stagnant; radicalization pipelines partially replaced by ongoing recruitment.

**Why this is the central case.** It requires no change in any party's behaviour — it is the continuation of observable trends (2024–2026), smoothed. It is "fragile" because the equilibrium can tip (Scenario 3) on any of several shocks, and it is "equilibrium" because none of the core drivers is self-correcting: the sanctuary persists, the fiscal shortfall persists, and the police protection deficit persists.

## 14.3 Scenario 3: Deterioration — Resurgence of Militancy and State-Erosion Risk

**Probability: 25–30 per cent by 2035 (with sub-scenarios below).**

**Narrative components (each independently plausible).**
(a) *Interstate escalation trap*: repeated mass-casualty attacks inside Pakistan trigger larger strikes on Afghanistan; Kabul retaliates with sustained border fire and drone attacks; Torkham and Ghulam Khan close for months; cross-border trade collapses; border populations (already displaced 33,800 families in Tirah alone) radicalize further; and the TTP's Operation Khyber framing gains traction (Chapters 3, 9).
(b) *Urban mass-casualty terrorism*: an ISKP or TTP attack on a soft urban target in Peshawar or Islamabad (a mosque, hospital, market, or school) produces political panic, curfews, and possibly a nationwide crackdown that compounds alienation.
(c) *Police collapse dynamic*: if police casualties continue at 2026 rates (+93.7 per cent H1/2025→H1/2026), recruitment, retention and morale degrade to the point where static posts are abandoned and rural policing collapses in parts of the south — handing some districts to TTP's parallel governance (taxation, courts), a threshold-crossing event that would make the NMDs resemble pre-2009 Swat.
(d) *Escalation in sectarian conflict*: a Kurram-scale or larger incident (a major attack on a Shia procession, or a Hazara massacre) ignites sustained sectarian warfare; the state's capacity is diverted; and both Iran-linked and ISKP-linked escalatory pressures intensify.
(e) *Two-front stress*: an India–Pakistan crisis (e.g., a repeat of 2019 or 2025-style exchanges) draws forces eastward, creating space for TTP/BLA operations in the west.

**Outcome indicators.** Annual KP fatalities exceed 3,500; the TTP holds "no-go" pockets by night in ≥3 districts; parallel taxation is routinized in ≥5 NMD districts; police recruitment shortfalls exceed 20 per cent; provincial revenue-strapped government fails to pay salaries on time; provincial elections (or their postponement) become a legitimacy crisis.

**Assessment.** The constituent risks are not hypothetical; several have already occurred in partial form (the 2026 war; the Kohat attack; Tirah displacement; Kurram's siege-like closure). What distinguishes Scenario 3 from the status quo is *threshold crossing*: the transition from episodic violence to durable loss of state control in parts of the province — which would require not a new set of causes but the removal of the current counterweights (police presence, tribal resistance, intelligence penetration, international attention).

## 14.4 Key Variables and Tipping Points

| Variable | Direction that improves security | Tipping point (negative) |
|---|---|---|
| **Afghanistan channel** | Mediated, verifiable arrangement; TTP relocation pressures | Sustained border war; sanctuary consolidation; TTP leadership freed from restraint |
| **Federal–provincial compact** | Statutory coordination; security financing; unified narrative | Continued estrangement; provincial refusal to support operations; federal exclusion of Peshawar |
| **Merged districts' fiscal settlement** | 3% floor legislated (or equivalent); transfers on schedule; local government funded | Expiry in 2028 without successor; further withholding; visible elite capture |
| **Police protection & capability** | Protected mobility, counter-IED, counter-drone; casualty decline; tenure stability | Casualty rates above H1 2026 (122/6 months); mass resignations; collapse of static posts |
| **Justice & prison system** | Convictions in visible cases; witness protection; ATC throughput | High-profile acquittals/escapes; military-court overuse eroding legitimacy |
| **PTM and civic space** | Legal political space; release of activists; protest normalization | Further proscriptions; mass arrests of young Pashtun activists |
| **Sectarian flashpoints (Kurram)** | Enforced peace agreement; reopened roads; disarmament | Mass-casualty sectarian attack; sustained road closure |
| **Economy & employment** | Growth, exports (tourism, IT, minerals), youth employment | Fiscal crisis; rupee shock; loss of remittances (Gulf) |
| **External finance** | Donor support for reintegration/education | Global aid contraction; CPEC pause after a major attack |
| **Elite cohesion** | Cross-party consensus on security framework | Contested election/postponement; imposition of emergency governance |

[[FIG:fig5_scenarios.png|Figure 5. Subjective probability assessments for the three KP security scenarios to 2035 (Chapter 14). These are analytic judgements, not forecasts; Chapter 9's five geopolitical scenarios describe the external environment within which these outcomes would unfold.]]

## 14.5 Risk Assessment Matrix: Political, Security, Economic, Social and External Risks

| Risk | Likelihood (to 2035) | Impact | Composite | Mitigation lever |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mass-casualty urban terror attack | High | High | Critical | Intelligence fusion; protection of soft targets; deradicalization |
| Sustained border war with Afghanistan | Medium-High | Very High | Critical | Mediation; verification mechanism; border regime |
| Police force attrition/collapse in south KP | Medium | Very High | Critical | Protection package; federal financing; recruitment in situ |
| Sectarian mass-casualty escalation (Kurram/Parachinar) | Medium | Very High | Critical | Enforced accords; road security; disarmament; justice |
| Fiscal failure of the merger (post-2028) | Medium-High | High | High | Statutory fund; presidential order; donor conditionality |
| ISKP expansion in KP | Medium | High | High | Targeted CT; border control; sectarian de-escalation |
| PTM-related unrest/martyr narratives | Medium-High | Medium | High | Lift proscription; lawful political space; dialogue |
| Climate disaster (mega-flood/GLOF) | High (recurring) | High | High | Preparedness; resilient infrastructure; relief accountability |
| CPEC/Chinese national attack (major) | Medium | High | High | Protection; local employment; political de-risking |
| Two-front crisis (India) | Medium | Very High | High | Strategic restraint; crisis management |
| Youth radicalization acceleration | High (structural) | Medium-High | High | Education; employment; PVE at scale |
| Economy/fiscal shock (IMF-linked) | Medium-High | Medium | Medium-High | Fiscal stability; provincial revenue reform |

## 14.6 Early Warning Indicators for Security Deterioration

A functional early-warning system (currently absent) would track, quarterly: (1) attack frequency and fatality trends by district, disaggregated by actor and target type; (2) police casualties and attacks, and *thwarted* attacks, by district; (3) TTP taxation/"checkpoint" reports by district; (4) drone-attack attempts and counter-drone successes; (5) compensation-payment arrears and displaced-return rates; (6) school closures and girl-enrolment by district; (7) conviction rates and pending cases in ATCs; (8) TTP/ISKP propaganda volume and recruitment narratives (media monitoring); (9) cross-border trade volumes and border-post closure days; (10) local-government budget flows (or their absence); (11) police recruitment/retention rates; and (12) sectoral indicators of state presence (health facilities functional; courts sitting; teachers present). Thresholds on several indicators (e.g., a district where TTP taxation is reported for two consecutive quarters; three consecutive months of rising police attacks; failure to pay compensation within 180 days) would trip escalation of the response — from development acceleration to security surge.

## 14.7 The Role of Climate Change and Environmental Degradation in Future Security

**The risk profile.** KP faces: accelerated glacial melt (Pakistan has 7,000+ glaciers, mostly in KP and Gilgit-Baltistan); increased monsoon intensity (the NDMA forecast 22–26 per cent above-normal rainfall for 2026); GLOF risk in Chitral, Swat, Dir and Gilgit-Baltistan's border areas; flash flooding (the 2025 disaster: 504 KP deaths, 1.57 million affected, ~100,000 displaced, 437 schools affected, 60 health facilities damaged); landslides; and prolonged drought cycles in the southern districts. These are *security-relevant* through five channels: (i) displacement (recruitment, trafficking, and lost livelihoods); (ii) the destruction of state infrastructure precisely in contested districts; (iii) relief distribution becoming a site of capture and conflict (flood relief in KP has been politically contested); (iv) water conflict (canals, entitlements, and the trans-boundary Kabul River); and (v) economic contraction (agriculture losses driving rural poverty, which is already increasing).

**Scenario relevance.** Climate shocks in Scenario 2 are recurrent stress; in Scenario 3 they are accelerants. Policy must integrate climate resilience into the security framework: flood-resilient roads and schools (double-purpose: state presence and disaster protection), upstream GLOF early warning, and relief distribution through transparent, community-audited mechanisms (avoiding the patronage capture that feeds grievance).

## 14.8 Demographic Trends and Their Security Implications (Youth Bulge, Urbanization)

**Trends.** KP's population (~40 million) is growing at roughly 2 per cent-plus annually, with about half under 25. Urbanization is accelerating: Peshawar's metropolitan area, Mardan, Swat, Abbottabad and D.I. Khan are growing, driven by rural-to-urban migration, displacement, and conflict. The combined effect by 2035: a labour force expanding by ~400,000–600,000 people per year against formal job creation that in recent years has been a fraction of that figure.

**Security implications.** (i) *Urbanization of violence*: as cities grow, so does the target set for terrorism (markets, transport, mosques, government offices) — the pattern already visible in Peshawar, and threatened in the Kohat/Swat episodes. (ii) *Informal settlements*: rapid urbanization produces unplanned peripheral settlements (the natural habitat for anonymity, criminality and radicalization networks). (iii) *The youth bulge*: a cohort with weak job prospects and strong connectivity will generate recruitment pressure and mobilization potential for both militant and protest movements — the PTM's peak mobilization (2018–2020) was demographically driven. (iv) *Elderly/veteran problem*: decades of conflict produce a large cohort of injured, traumatized and disarmed men needing reintegration.

**Policy implication.** Any normalization plan that does not include an explicit *urban and youth strategy* (housing, transport, skills, sports/culture, and urban policing) will be overwhelmed by demography. Conversely, urbanization can be an advantage: services are cheaper to deliver in dense settlements, and city governance — if empowered — can be a laboratory for accountable security provision.

## 14.9 Technology, AI and the Future of Security Management in KPK

**Applications now arriving.** (i) *Surveillance*: the Peshawar Safe City system (711 AI cameras, facial recognition; D.I. Khan, Bannu, Lakki Marwat in progress) plus mobile surveillance vehicles, drones and anti-drone systems. (ii) *Biometrics and identity*: NADRA-linked verification, SIM registration, border-crossing biometrics. (iii) *Military technologies*: anti-drone electronic warfare; night-vision and precision optics; ISR drones. (iv) *Data analytics*: police data systems; the provincial "Digital Transformation Policy 2030." (v) *Militant technologies*: quadcopter IED delivery (341 attacks foiled in H1 2026), commercial drones, encrypted communications, online propaganda.

**Emerging risks and opportunities.**
*Opportunities*: predictive policing (with safeguards); automated threat alerts at checkpoints; rapid forensic identification; counter-drone systems; digital payment tracking (anti-financing); and e-governance improving service delivery and reducing corruption contact points.
*Risks*: (i) *rights and oversight* — facial recognition without data protection (there is no Pakistani data-protection law of general application) becomes a tool of political surveillance; the Safe City Authority, if executive-controlled, would compound the 27th Amendment's centralization; (ii) *technological overconfidence* — cameras cannot solve a rural insurgency in which the state lacks presence, intelligence and legitimacy; (iii) *the adversary adapts* — drone/dark-web/AI-enabled propaganda will evolve faster than procurement cycles; and (iv) *digital exclusion* — merged-district connectivity gaps mean the digital dividend accrues unevenly, deepening the core-periphery gap.

**Policy direction.** (a) Legislate data protection and surveillance oversight *before* expanding the Safe City network; (b) treat counter-drone capability as a frontline police protection requirement (the Karak attack pattern); (c) invest in counter-propaganda AI tools only within a rights framework with published rules; and (d) prioritize *investigative* technology (forensics, digital evidence chain of custody) over mass surveillance — the former is what converts detentions into convictions, the latter is what produces grievance.

## 14.10 Strategic Foresight: KPK Security in 2035

Three 2035 snapshots, corresponding to the scenarios above:

**2035-A (Normalization, ~18 per cent).** KP is a middle-income provincial economy with functioning district courts in the NMDs, a professionalized police force with an NMD wing, a legislated merged-districts fund delivering on schedule, 20 million annual tourists, a minerals sector with community benefit-sharing, and a border regime with Afghanistan managed through monitoring agreements. Violence is at criminal levels (200–400 fatalities/year), and the FATA merger is regarded regionally as a success model. This future requires the fiscal settlement and a border détente — nothing else.

**2035-B (Fragile Equilibrium, ~55 per cent).** KP has absorbed another decade of violence: cumulative 200,000+ displaced, tens of thousands dead, a police force hollowed by casualties, an economy that grows slowly while the merged districts remain the poorest in Pakistan, periodic border wars with Afghanistan, and a political system in which security remains the dominant issue. Tourism continues; the province holds; but its institutions remain below the level required to break with the past.

**2035-C (Deterioration, ~27 per cent).** The eastward shift of the insurgency continues to the settled districts; the TTP exercises parallel governance in some NMD pockets; sectarian conflict has erupted at scale in at least one district; the state's response has produced further centralization and repression; a PTI-era political crisis has hardened Pashtun alienation; and Pakistan ranks among the world's most terrorism-affected countries for a decade running. This future's seeds are visible in 2026's data — a 93.7 per cent increase in police fatalities, a Rs 568 billion fiscal betrayal, and an interstate war with no exit.

**Foresight conclusion.** The difference between these futures is not primarily the number of militants Pakistan kills; it is the delivery of three state commitments that are entirely within Pakistan's control: money for the merged districts; protection for the police; and a lawful political channel for Pashtun grievance. The external variable — the Afghan sanctuary — can be mitigated or worsened by diplomacy, but it cannot be waited out. **KP's 2035 hinges on whether Peshawar and Islamabad treat the province as a constitutional partner or as a war zone with a budget.**


# CHAPTER 15 — A PHASED ROADMAP FOR NORMALIZATION: IMPLEMENTATION, MONITORING AND FINANCING

## 15.1 Purpose, Design Principles and the Logic of Sequencing

**Purpose.** This chapter converts the study's findings into an implementable agenda. Its objective is not to end all violence — no plan can — but to move the province from Scenario 2 (fragile equilibrium) toward Scenario 1 (sustained normalization) and to make a transition to Scenario 3 (deterioration) less likely. The roadmap is deliberately long-horizon: 15 years, in three phases (0–2, 2–5, 5–15 years), because the evidence in Chapters 13–14 shows that normalization is generational, not annual.

**Design principles.**
1. **Security and legitimacy together.** Operations that kill militants without building the institutions that replace them (courts, police, local government) reproduce the cycle documented in Chapter 6. Every security measure should have an accompanying legitimacy measure.
2. **The state's own commitments are the binding constraint.** The clearest lesson of Chapters 5, 11 and 14: the largest obstacles to normalization are not TTP's fighting power but the failures of fiscal delivery, police protection and lawful political space — all of which the state controls.
3. **Do no harm to the fiscal settlement.** The merged districts' Rs 568 billion shortfall is not a technicality; it is the material foundation of the state's claim to have annexed the tribal areas into the nation. It must be fixed before anything else is credible.
4. **Subsidiarity and local legitimacy.** Every function that can be performed at district or tehsil level should be, with resources attached. Normalization delivered from Peshawar and Islamabad has a lower ceiling.
5. **Evidence, transparency and independent verification.** The province's security statistic is contested; the roadmap therefore includes a monitoring architecture with published data — the most conspicuous gap in the current system (Chapter 11).
6. **Reversibility and no-amnesty boundaries.** Reintegration and negotiation (where possible) should not expand to impunity for mass-casualty atrocities; the state's credibility with victims depends on this line.
7. **Identify, don't demonize, communities.** Pashtuns are the principal victims of the insurgency; policy must target networks and behaviours, not identity (Chapter 4).
8. **Reconcile the scenarios.** Chapter 9's five geopolitical scenarios (de-escalation, managed confrontation, escalatory collapse, India–Pakistan crisis, Taliban instability) describe the *external* environment; Chapter 14's three province-level scenarios describe the *internal* outcomes. They are nested, not competing: the roadmap must be robust across the four non-catastrophic geopolitical cases and hedging against the fifth (see §15.11).

**The three deficits as the roadmap's anchor.** Chapter 11 established that KP's policy implementation fails through three deficits: **fiscal** (the resources never arrive), **accountability** (no one monitors or answers for outcomes), and **legitimacy** (the population does not own the settlement). Each phase below is scored against all three, and §15.9 makes them the core of the monitoring framework.

## 15.2 What "Normalized" Means: A Measurable End-State

Normalization is defined here by eight measurable conditions, not by the absence of attacks:

| # | Condition | 2035 target |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Violence at criminal, not insurgent, levels | ≤ 400 conflict fatalities/year province-wide |
| 2 | State monopoly of violence restored in the NMDs | No district with sustained TTP taxation or parallel courts for 4 consecutive quarters |
| 3 | Police able to protect themselves and the public | ≤ 50 police killed per year; ≥ 80% of attacks thwarted or survived without loss |
| 4 | Justice delivered where the conflict was fought | ≥ 75% of ATC cases concluded within 24 months; functioning district courts in all seven NMDs |
| 5 | Fiscal settlement delivered | 3% (or legislated equivalent) transferred in full, with independent audit, every year |
| 6 | Local government functioning | Elected councils in all NMDs with own-source revenue and budget authority |
| 7 | Economy recovering inclusively | Merged-district poverty within 10 points of the provincial average; ≥ 15% growth in non-farm employment |
| 8 | Legitimate political space | No proscription of non-violent parties; protest and media functioning without criminalisation |

## 15.3 Phase I (0–2 Years): Stabilisation, Fiscal Rescue and the Protection Pivot

### A. The merged-district fiscal rescue (immediate, highest priority)
1. **Legislate the transfer.** Secure the promised 3 per cent NFC share (or a quantified equivalent) through: (a) conclusion of the current NFC award; failing that (b) a presidential order under Article 160; failing that (c) a statutory federal grant with a minimum floor — so that the commitment survives an award cycle. *Responsible:* Federal Ministry of Finance, NFC, Prime Minister's Office; *with:* KP Finance Department; *by:* budget cycle 2027–28 at latest, before the 2028 expiry of the original commitment. *Verification:* independent annual audit published by the Auditor-General with a standing committee review (Chapter 5 design requirements).
2. **Clear all arrears within 12 months.** Pay the outstanding Rs 568 billion on a published schedule with quarterly milestones; publish district-wise releases monthly (currently unavailable).
3. **Protect the money from capture.** Ring-fence annual development program allocations for the NMDs; require community monitoring committees in each district; publish beneficiary lists for compensation payments (Tirah, Sarbakaf, Kurram and operation-affected); and refer documented diversion to the NAB/ACE with public reporting.
4. **Pay compensation and rebuild destroyed property.** Complete Tirah compensation, rebuild or compensate the destroyed homes, mosques and schools of the 2024–2026 operations and airstrikes; treat this as a *security expenditure*, not welfare.
5. **Reopen and fund the service pipeline.** Restore suspended education and health projects; rehire teachers/personnel in the merged districts; restart the halted road connections (including the Torkham–Jalalabad corridor's trade infrastructure) under protection.

### B. The police protection pivot (immediate)
6. **Emergency protection package.** Protected mobility (mine-protected vehicles), counter-IED equipment and training, counter-drone systems for static posts, ballistic protection and helmets, secure communications, and drone/surveillance support to district police — deployed first to Kohat, Lakki Marwat, Bannu, D.I. Khan, Tank, Kurram, Khyber and North/South Waziristan (where casualty rates are highest). *Responsible:* KP Police, Interior/Home, with federal supplementary grant.
7. **Insurance, compensation and family support.** Complete the insurance coverage promised for police (the Inspector-General's costed plan) — a retention prerequisite; raise the martyrdom package to parity with other forces; and institutionalize family support (education, pensions, job quotas).
8. **Attack attribution and learning.** Mandatory post-incident reviews after every police fatality in an attack, published in aggregate quarterly with actionable findings (a measure the current system does not have).
9. **Halt the free fall in police strength.** Expedited recruitment (including 6,000+ new posts from the July 2026 package), with provincial recruitment quotas for the merged districts; competitive pay adjustments; and rehabilitation posts for wounded personnel.

### C. The border and cross-border channel (immediate)
10. **Consolidate the mediated ceasefire into a verification mechanism.** Build on the Qatar/Türkiye/China facilitation of 2025–2026 to secure a defined set of commitments — no cross-border attacks, no sanctuary support, prisoner exchanges, and trade corridors kept open — with an independent monitoring presence (UN, OIC, or a China-facilitated joint commission) and a quarterly published status report. *Responsible:* Foreign Office (MOFA), with Security Divisions; *realistic aim:* manage, not solve.
11. **Border regime.** Complete fencing/fortification, formalize the Torkham/Chaman/Angoor Ada trade and mobility regime (documented crossers, trade facilitation, and reciprocal arrangements for families), and fund border-district police and Levies in step with any restriction regime (otherwise restriction becomes abuse — Chapter 5's border-management findings).
12. **Refugee policy with dignity.** Complete the Afghan-refugee registration/repatriation processes lawfully: individual, documented, appealable procedures; no mass refoulement; and a joint UNHCR–government verification of any voluntary-return program — because abuse here feeds the insurgency's narrative (Chapters 9, 11).

### D. Civic space and legitimacy (immediate, low cost, high return)
13. **Lift the PTM proscription and release detained activists** lacking terrorism charges; open a lawful channel for Pashtun political demands (see Ch. 4, 12 analysis). *This single measure costs no money and removes the strongest single driver of ethnic-grievance mobilization.*
14. **End indefinite detention without charge.** Publish the number of detainees held under security laws; move all cases to charging or release within statutory periods; and create a statutory juvenile track (Chapter 8 recommendation).
15. **Restore the fourth estate.** Resolve the criminalisation of journalistic expression; implement a mechanism for investigating attacks on journalists; and permit independent casualty and displacement reporting — contested facts are better than suppressed ones (Chapter 11's accountability deficit).

### E. Intelligence and operations (immediate)
16. **Intelligence-led targeting with discrimination.** Continue IBOs and high-value operations with tightened collateral-damage safeguards, documented post-strike assessments, rapid compensation where civilian harm occurred, and no operation without a designated post-operation stabilization component (Chapters 6, 9). The operational record shows that kinetic success without stabilization produces recurrence.
17. **Counter-financing.** Strike TTP's revenue (extortion, taxation, mining, smuggling) with financial-intelligence-led action coordinated between the CTD, FIA, Customs and provincial mining authorities; publish aggregate seizure data (de-risking the state from its biggest counter-TTP lever).
18. **Tackle the drone threat as a top-tier program.** 341 foiled drone attacks in H1 2026 is a warning, not a comfort: build a provincial counter-drone unit with an airspace-management protocol; equip static posts; and criminalise drone-based IED delivery with dedicated prosecutions.

## 15.4 Phase II (2–5 Years): Institutionalisation

### A. Security-sector reform (Chapters 7's ten recommendations, sequenced)
19. **Establish the merged-district police framework.** Resolve the Levies/Khasadar integration entirely; statutory police for all seven districts; tribal constabulary terms, pay and pensions harmonised; district recruitment ramped up; and the Local Police Act or equivalent tested in consultation with jirgas (Chapter 5 design requirement).
20. **Professionalise the CTD.** Separate investigation from operation; an independent prosecution liaison unit; analytical cadre with specialist training; and transparent case data (arrest → charge → conviction) published annually — the province's whole counter-terrorism credibility depends on evidence that terrorism cases end in convictions.
21. **Reform police accountability.** Strengthen the KP Public Safety Commissions; institute independent investigation of serious police misconduct (lethal force, custodial abuse); and adopt body cameras/supervisory review at scale as evidence, not surveillance.
22. **Invest in investigations over enforcement.** Forensics (DNA, ballistics, digital exploitation), crime-scene management, prison-security upgrades (the Kohat/Bannu attack pattern is a prison-and-police-lines vulnerability), and witness-protection legislation with a functioning protection unit.
23. **Build the provincial intelligence architecture within law.** A unified provincial threat-fusion centre with police, CTD, Levies, NACTA and intelligence representation; data-protection rules and a parliamentary oversight committee (Chapter 7; Ch. 14.9's rights warning).

### B. Justice and rule of law (the legitimacy core)
24. **Judicial delivery plan for the NMDs.** Appoint and fund judges, prosecutors, staff and court infrastructure; mobile courts and video links where insecurity demands; and mandatory ATC case-conclusion targets with published quarterly data.
25. **Reparations and victims' mechanism.** A statutory provincial scheme covering: compensation (already mandated but inconsistently paid), medical and psychosocial support (500 psychiatrists nationally is a crisis — fund provincial psychiatric expansion and community-based psychosocial services), reconstruction, and *status* (a documented register of victims and a memorialization policy that includes all victims of the conflict, including those harmed by state forces). This is the concrete form of the PTM's truth-and-reconciliation demand, domesticated into the legal system.
26. **Kurram and sectarian peace enforcement.** Turn the 2025 *Aman Teega* and later accords into enforced instruments: disarmament with verifiable milestones, guaranteed road reopening, prosecution of those violating accords, a compensated, resettled peace, and a standing third-party (provincial + PMC) oversight mechanism — the Mindanao/monitoring model applied locally (Chapter 13).
27. **Jirga formalisation.** Legislate the jirga's role as mediator in civil disputes with rights safeguards (women's representation, prohibition of verdicts violating statute, no jurisdiction over terrorism cases) and fund district dispute-resolution centres with referral protocols to courts (Chapter 12.2).

### C. Deradicalization and reintegration at scale (Chapter 8's eight recommendations, sequenced)
28. **Statute and a Rehabilitation and Reintegration Authority.** Legal framework for voluntary and court-ordered programmes; a statutory juvenile track; and a funded authority (federal-provincial) with published standards.
29. **Relocate and expand programmes.** Move to where radicalization is occurring — Bannu, D.I. Khan, the NMDs — with family-based rehabilitation (the Sparlay-type model), vocational training, apprenticeships and aftercare grants, plus employment placement partnerships with chambers of commerce (Chapter 12.11).
30. **Independent evaluation.** A five-year, independently conducted evaluation of Sabawoon, Mishal and all provincial programmes, with pretraining/post-release measurement, recidivism tracking and *published* results — the absence of independent evaluation after 17 years is unacceptable and fixable (Chapter 8).
31. **Counter-propaganda with local credibility.** Fund Pashto-language counter-narrative content produced by Pashtun religious scholars, teachers, women's groups and former combatants; distribute via radio, social media and madrasa networks; do not outsource to distant agencies.
32. **Madrasa reform, second attempt.** Restore a consultative registration framework (statutory, transparent, non-punitive) after the October 2024 reversal; condition financing on transparency; fund modern-subject teaching; and separate the registration function from security surveillance to rebuild trust (Chapters 8, 11).
33. **Reintegration security guarantees.** Protect ex-detainees/rehabilitees from retaliation (police liaison, protection for those threatened, anonymous reporting) — the Colombian lesson: unprotectyed reintegration produces dissidence (Chapter 13.2).

### D. Governance and local government (the missing tier)
34. **Hold local elections in the merged districts.** Extend statutory local government systems to the NMDs with devolved budgets and own-source revenue; ring-fence a share of the merged-district fund for district councils (this is the single missing institution capable of absorbing the fiscal infusion into public goods rather than elite capture).
35. **Federal–provincial security compact.** A statutory framework clarifying roles (federal financing of military operations; provincial policing primacy in civilian security; joint command for emergency responses), ending the "provinces are not consulted" dynamic and the blame Games that follow incidents (Chapter 11).
36. **Institutionalise the Apex Committee model.** Regularise provincial security coordination with published, non-classified summaries; include elected representatives and, where appropriate, civil-society observers in a defined consultative role.
37. **Anti-corruption as security policy.** Strengthen KPPRA, Ehtesab Commission powers, and asset-recovery in the NMDs' contracts (minerals, land, CPEC) — because corruption is the state's own insurgency oxygen (Chapters 4, 13.5).

### E. Economy and services (the co-production agenda, Chapter 10)
38. **Deliver the development–security package.** Prioritize, in the seven NMDs and south KP: secondary schools (girls' enrolment is the single most correlated indicator of long-run stability), technical/vocational institutes, health facilities with staffed capacity (not buildings alone), irrigation and road connectivity, and electricity/gas access. Fund through the merged-district scheme with quarterly public delivery dashboards.
39. **Jobs: the private-sector channel.** Rashakai and the other SEZs; mineral benefit-sharing; tourism infrastructure (the 12–20 million visitor economy is the fastest-growing employment engine); IT/freelancing hubs (Peshawar, Swat, Abbottabad) with connectivity and payment infrastructure; and the agricultural value chains of the southern districts.
40. **Land, water and property rights.** Resolve disputed land in the merged districts (a long-standing conflict driver); formalize tenure where possible; and settle upstream water entitlements transparently.
41. **Climate-adaptive reconstruction.** Rebuild 2025 flood damage with resilience standards; GLOF early warning; and a standing disaster-response mechanism integrated with police/Levies.

### F. Education and human capital (the generational bet)
42. **Emergency education program for the merged districts.** The NMDs' ~37 per cent primary and ~5 per cent secondary female enrolment are a security statistic: fund stipends, girls' schools (including secure campus transport), female teachers, and adult literacy; measure and publish district-level enrolment quarterly.
43. **Curriculum and teacher reform.** Civics, critical thinking, anti-sectarian and anti-extremism content; teacher training and certification; textbook review; and protection of education (no military use of schools; attacks on schools prosecuted).
44. **Youth pipeline.** Scale apprenticeships, internships and entrepreneurship financing; restore student unions and campus civic space; and fund sports and cultural infrastructure in the worst-affected districts — cheap, visible, and effective.

## 15.5 Phase III (5–15 Years): Consolidation and Transformation

**Security consolidation.** (45) Transition from population-centric IBO cycles to *community security contracts*: local security plans co-designed with councils, jirgas and police, with published targets; (46) institutionalize the Afghan border regime (monitoring, trade, mobility, and dispute resolution) so that border friction does not automatically escalate — building the "managed confrontation" scenario (Ch. 9-B) into an institutionalized relationship; (47) if and when a TTP disengagement process becomes possible, apply the Chapter 13 criteria — decentralized, conditional, verified, staged, with monitored decommissioning and explicit no-impunity boundaries, and with third-party monitoring.

**Justice and reconciliation.** (48) A provincial *truth and documentation* body — not a South Africa–style amnesty commission, but a legally established process to document the conflict's human cost (all sides), record testimony, identify the missing, and publish a public record; paired with (49) selective accountability for mass-casualty atrocities; (50) a permanent victims' support institution with reparations as a statutory entitlement.

**Political consolidation.** (51) Guarantee lawful political space for Pashtun parties and youth movements; (52) build cross-party consensus on the security framework (a provincial security charter) so that policy survives elections; (53) institutionalize federal–provincial coordination (joint annual security and development review, published).

**Economic transformation.** (54) Consolidate the merged-district fiscal guarantee beyond the current NFC cycle; (55) build a provincial resilience economy: minerals with local value addition, energy (hydropower), tourism, IT exports, agriculture; (56) close the NMD human-development gap to within 10 points of the provincial average; (57) maintain climate resilience as a standing program.

**Regional normalization.** (58) Trade and people-to-people ties with Afghanistan (economics as the peace dividend); (59) regional frameworks (SCO, OIC) for counter-terrorism cooperation and refugee burden-sharing; (60) a settled, non-militarized border regime ratified on both sides — the only durable answer to a porous, contested frontier.

## 15.6 Sequencing Logic and Critical Dependencies

| Phase | Must complete before dependent steps | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Paying arrears & legislating the fund | Phase II local government; education expansion; economic programs | Without money, devolution and services are fictional and the political class is discredited |
| Police protection package | Phase II police reform & CTD professionalisation | Recruitment and reform fail if officers keep dying without kit |
| Ceasefire → verification mechanism | Phase I border regime; Phase III regional normalization | A ceasefire without monitoring is a pause, not a settlement (the 2004–2008 lesson) |
| Lifting the PTM ban & releasing detainees | Phase II political consensus; Phase III reconciliation | Alienation is the recruitment channel; criminalising it is self-defeating |
| ATC throughput & witness protection | Phase II/III accountability, truth process | Impunity reinforces both militancy and grievance |
| Local government elections (NMDs) | Phase III community security contracts; NMD development | Without elected local bodies there is no interface for either services or security |
| Madrasa re-registration framework | Phase III counter-narrative consolidation | The 2024 reversal must be reversed again to close the theological pipeline |

**The critical path:** **fiscal rescue → police protection → justice delivery → local government → reintegration at scale → political inclusion → regional normalization.** Parallel action is desirable, but the first three are prerequisites for credibility; a roadmap that begins with reconciliation while officers lack helmets will not be believed.

## 15.7 Institutional Responsibilities Matrix

| Function | Federal | Provincial | District/Local | Security forces | Partners |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Merged-district fund (3%) | Finance/NFC/PMO | KP Finance | — | — | Auditor-General, donors |
| Police protection & pay | Interior (supplementary grant) | Home Dept, Police | — | KP Police | Donors (equipment) |
| CTD & intelligence | Interior/NACTA | Home, CTD | — | CTD, Levies, ISI/IB liaison | — |
| Border regime | MOFA, Interior | Border districts | Levies/police | Army/FC, police | UN, China/Qatar facilitation |
| Refugee policy | SAFRON/Interior | KP | — | Police/FC | UNHCR, IOM |
| Deradicalization/R&R | NACTA/Interior | Home, CTD | DRCs | Police | UN, NGOs, chambers |
| Local government (NMDs) | Law/Elections | Local Govt Dept | Councils | — | UNDP, donors |
| Education in NMDs | Federal schemes | Education Dept | DEOs, schools | Police (school protection) | UNICEF, WFP, donors |
| Justice & ATCs | Law/Justice, Supreme Court | Prosecution, PHC | District courts | Prisons/police | INL, UNODC |
| M&E and data | Planning/NACTA | Planning & Dev | District stats | — | SDPI, academia, donors |

## 15.8 Financing the Roadmap: Costs, the Conflict-Loss Baseline and the Fiscal Arithmetic

**The baseline.** Chapter 10 estimated KP's annual conflict-related economic loss at **Rs 400–800 billion** (1.5–3 per cent of provincial economic output under different assumptions); Pakistan's cumulative war-on-terror losses since 2001 are internationally cited at **USD 126.79 billion to 152 billion** (with individual years estimated over Rs 1 trillion nationally). The merged-district shortfall alone is **Rs 568 billion** — roughly 0.7–1.4 years of the entire provincial conflict loss, or (to put it differently) the state loses in 8–17 months of conflict what it has failed to transfer to the merged districts in a full award cycle.

**The arithmetic of the fix.** NMD obligations: Rs 568 billion arrears + annual 3 per cent allocation (on a rising provincial share). Police protection package: a one-time Rs 50–80 billion (protected vehicles, counter-IED, counter-drone, communications) with Rs 30–50 billion annually in recurrent costs (pay, insurance, equipment replacement). Deradicalization and reintegration at scale: Rs 10–20 billion annually (facilities, staff, stipends, aftercare, independent evaluation). Education emergency in the NMDs: Rs 40–60 billion annually to approach provincial enrolment ratios. Local government and devolved budgets: funded from the merged-district allocation. Judicial expansion: Rs 5–10 billion annually. **Total incremental requirement: on the order of Rs 150–250 billion per year at maturity, in addition to the Rs 568 billion arrears** — i.e., at the lower bound, *less than half* of the estimated annual conflict loss. The investment case is thus straightforward: the province and the federation already pay 2–4 times the roadmap's incremental cost in lost output, displacement, security expenditure, and destroyed capital.

**Revenue sources.** (1) The NFC share and provincial taxes (with strengthened anti-corruption in collection); (2) reallocation within the federal security budget (Pakistan's defence/security spending is large; the merged-district shortfall is small relative to it); (3) donor and IFI financing for education, health, reintegration and monitoring (conditioned on delivery); (4) CPEC-related provincial revenue as it materializes; (5) a dedicated merged-district development window with multi-year budgeting (the absence of which is exactly what produces lapsed allocations — the Rs 132bn of Rs 700bn pattern); and (6) diaspora/Pakistani philanthropic funding for education, channeled through transparent institutions.

**Financing principles.** Ring-fence and protect allocations from lapsed budgets; publish district-wise releases monthly; condition donor tranches on verified delivery; finance *recurrent* costs (teachers, police, judges) as well as buildings — the classic Pakistani failure is projects without staff; and treat compensation payments as automatic entitlements, not discretionary patronage.

## 15.9 Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning Framework

**Design.** The M&E framework operationalises the three deficits (Chapter 11) into measurable commitments, with independent verification:

*Fiscal deficit indicators:* percentage of awarded NMD funds released within the fiscal year; arrears balance; percentage of development budget spent (with district-wise publication); compensation payments made within 180 days (target: 90 per cent); audit report timeliness and referral of findings.

*Accountability indicators:* police attacks, police killed, and *cases progressed* (arrest → charge → conviction) for attacks on the state; ATC cases concluded within 24 months (target ≥75 per cent); independent police-misconduct investigations completed; prison-security incidents; counter-terror financing seizures; operation collateral-damage reviews published.

*Legitimacy indicators:* NMD local-government elections held; PTM proscription status; number of security detainees held without charge; journalists/activists prosecuted for expression; public-opinion surveys (trust in police, courts, provincial government) conducted independently and published; victim-satisfaction with compensation and rehabilitation services; displaced return rates and property-restoration rates.

*Security-consolidation indicators:* conflict fatalities by district and actor (trend); TTP taxation/parallel-justice reports; attacks on schools/girls' education; border-closure days and cross-border trade volumes; thwarted drone attacks; attacks on polio teams and minority sites.
