*Socio-economic indicators:* NMD female enrolment ratios; out-of-school children; health-facility staffing; household poverty; youth employment; tourism arrivals and sector employment; climate-disaster reconstruction delivery.

**Architecture.** A provincial *Security and Normalization Monitoring Cell* within the Planning and Development Department (or a statutory equivalent), with an independent Technical Advisory Panel (academia, civil society, donor representatives, and — where possible — a credible third party such as the World Bank or UNDP) producing a published *Annual Normalization Report* within 180 days of each fiscal year, debated in the KP Assembly and shared with the National Security Committee. Data sources: police and CTD records; health and education management information systems; district administration reportage; independent incident monitoring (CRSS/SATP/ACLED-type sources); household surveys; and donor M&E.

**Learning loop.** The annual report must include a *non-compliance register*: every unmet commitment with a named responsible institution and a remedy timeline. Without this, monitoring repeats the pattern documented in Chapter 11 — data collection without consequence.

## 15.10 Implementation Risks and the Political Economy of Reform

| Risk | Character | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| Fiscal promises again not honoured | Repeated behaviour; 2028 expiry | Statutory floor; audited publication; election-cycle accountability |
| Elites capture the NMD fund | Patronage networks | Community monitoring; public beneficiary lists; asset recovery; local government |
| Police reform blocked by hierarchy/institutional interests | Bureaucratic resistance | Protection-first sequencing (reform becomes attractive once officers stop dying); federal financing; published case data |
| Security establishment resists political inclusion (PTM) | Doctrine/security-first logic | Evidence that exclusion feeds recruitment; third-party dialogue; measured release of non-violent activists |
| TTP spoilers attack the roadmap's visible successes (schools, markets, tourists, SEZs) | Strategic spoiling | Hard-target protection of critical nodes; rapid compensation; narrative management |
| Afghanistan spoiler escalation during implementation | External | Mediation and monitoring mechanisms; diversified trade routes; hedging (Ch. 9-E) |
| Donor withdrawal | Global fiscal contraction | Domestic financing priority for protection and arrears; donor funds concentrated on education/reintegration |
| Political instability / elections | Cyclical | Provincial security charter; multi-year budgeting; independent monitoring to survive governments |
| Data falsification (police/CTD reporting) | Institutional incentives | Independent verification; survey cross-checks; published anomalies |
| Securitization of monitoring (surveillance of civil society) | Rights risk | Statutory mandate; data protection; external oversight |

**The political-economy reality.** Each roadmap item has a beneficiary and a potential loser. The losers are: patronage networks (beneficiary lists and audits), parts of the security establishment (political inclusion and judicial authority), unaccountable bureaucracies (published data and delivery targets), and the militants (whose recruitment suffers as the state delivers). The roadmap's political feasibility therefore depends on making the *security case* for each item — the protection pivot appeals to the institution; the fiscal rescue appeals to federal credibility; the political inclusion appeals to the counter-insurgency logic; and the transparency appeal rests on exposing the cost of the status quo, which the monitoring framework is designed to do annually.

## 15.11 Regional and International Cooperation Agenda

**With Afghanistan:** ceasefire consolidation and verification; border management (documented mobility, trade, and reciprocal security commitments); prisoner exchanges; refugee policy with dignity and UNHCR involvement; and joint economic projects to create stakeholders for restraint. Mediation channels (Qatar, Türkiye, Saudi Arabia, China) should be institutionalised into a standing mechanism rather than resorted to after each crisis. The roadmap hedges against the "Taliban instability" scenario (Ch. 9-E) by keeping lines to all factions and by preparing for spillovers (refugee influx, cross-border militant redistribution).

**With India:** no normalization progress in KP requires solving the Kashmir dispute, but the *risk* of a two-front crisis (Ch. 14.4) argues for crisis-management channels, restored (even if limited) dialogue, and a mutual understanding on non-interference in each other's internal insurgencies — including verification mechanisms. India's role in KP (the alleged patronage claims of 2025–2026) should be handled with evidence, not rhetoric: publicly documented cases are stronger than accusations, and false accusations discredit the state's own case.

**With China:** CPEC security cooperation; intelligence sharing; and — critically — a Chinese interest in the fiscal and political stability (not only physical protection) of the corridor's districts; Beijing's own mediation role (2026 talks) is a positive precedent for institutionalized diplomacy.

**With the Gulf and Türkiye:** financing for reintegration and education (Saudi/UAE/Qatar funds); Pakistan's diaspora; counter-narrative partnerships (Saudi PRAC's experience; see Chapter 8); and mediation channels with Afghanistan.

**With the UN and IFIs:** monitoring (a Mindanao-style third-party body adapted to the merger's fiscal and security commitments); reintegration standards and evaluation (UNODC, IOM); refugee processing (UNHCR); education and health funding (UNICEF, WFP, World Bank); and FATF-consistent counter-financing (with rights safeguards).

**Priorities in one line:** *pay the money, protect the police, staff the courts, elect the councils, restore lawful politics, and verify what is done.* Six commitments, all within Pakistan's control, and all cheaper than the conflict they would begin to end.


# CHAPTER 16 — CONCLUSION: THE THREE MONOPOLIES AND THE POLITICS OF NORMALIZATION

## 16.1 The Argument Restated

**Main thesis.** This study's central claim is that the security situation of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa cannot be normalized by the accumulation of force alone, because what KP lacks is not primarily coercive capacity but **three interlocking monopolies**: the state's monopoly on organized violence, the citizen's enforceable claim on the state (justice and governance), and the province's claim on national resources (fiscal federalism). Each is constitutive of the others. Where violence is unmonopolized, rights are unenforceable; where rights are unenforceable, communities shelter the violent; and where the province's fiscal claim is denied, the institutions that would monopolize violence — police, courts, schools, local government — cannot be built. KP's history is a natural experiment in each failure mode: the tribal areas' century of legal exception (no enforceable claim), the post-2001 military-dominant period (no police-led institutionalization), and the post-2018 merger-without-money (no fiscal claim). The three monopolies model is the study's original analytic contribution, and it organizes its empirical conclusions.

**The model's test against the evidence.**
- **1925–1947:** formal monopoly (British Indian law) coupled with structural exception (FCR, indirect rule) — no citizen's claim in the tribal belt.
- **1947–1979:** inherited exception, minimal state investment, jirga-mediated order, and a stable but shallow equilibrium.
- **1979–2001:** the Afghan wars militarize the frontier and embed the *mujahideen* economy; the Soviet withdrawal is followed by neglect (all three monopolies weak).
- **2001–2014:** the state responds with force; the Taliban insurgency and its suppression kill tens of thousands; every operation is followed by recurrence where state-building does not follow (Chapter 6's seven-criteria analysis shows proximity-to-force, not victory).
- **2014–2021:** the Radd-ul-Fasaad/NISP period combines counter-terrorism, CFT and partial merger; violence declines (2014–2021) — the best evidence for the model is that *decline coincided with the period when the state simultaneously deployed force, began legal integration, and sustained fiscal investment* (CPEC, development packages, the NMD fund's early years).
- **2021–2026:** the Taliban's Kabul victory restores the sanctuary (external variable) and the merger's fiscal failure denies resources (internal variable); violence rises for five consecutive years; and the state answers with another campaign name — Ghazab-lil-Haq (2026).

The pattern is not cyclical by accident. It is cyclical because each cycle addresses one monopoly — usually force — and leaves the other two unrepaired.

## 16.2 Answering the Twelve Research Questions

**1. Drivers.** KP's insecurity is driven by a four-layer causal architecture (Chapter 4): *structural conditions* (frontier geography, cross-border kinship, colonial legal exception, poverty, weak local government, sectarian demography, and the Pakistan–Afghanistan rivalries); *proximate triggers* (the Afghan sanctuary, operation-related displacement and grievances, the 2021 regime change in Kabul, and policy shocks such as the 2024 PTM proscription); *accelerants* (weapons availability, extortion economies, jailbreaks, ISKP's sectarian strategy, social media); and *sustaining mechanisms* (the war economy, impunity, and the recruitment pipeline of unaddressed grievances). The relative weight of these layers has shifted: the *structural* layer has been constant, while the *sanctuary* and *grievance* layers have become the dominant proximate drivers in the 2021–2026 period.

**2. Kinetic efficacy.** Operations have demonstrably reduced militant capacity (Swat 2009, North Waziristan 2014) and killed or displaced thousands of militants, but none has produced self-sustaining security: the seven-criteria analysis (Chapter 6) shows that operations succeed militarily, fade institutionally, and reverse where stabilization and justice do not follow. The gains' decay follows a predictable sequence — clearance → decline → complacency → militant regeneration → recurrence — with the regeneration period shortened when a sanctuary exists (2021–2026). Kinetic action remains necessary (there is no evidence the TTP would decline to attack absent pressure) but is not sufficient, and the sequence's weakest point is the transition from military clearing to police and civilian control.

**3. Merger impact.** The 25th Amendment's constitutional achievement is real; its administrative and fiscal delivery is not. The findings: legal extension succeeded; administrative integration is partial; judicial capacity is minimal; policing remains in transition; and the fiscal transfer — the merger's material promise, the 3 per cent NFC share — is Rs 568 billion short as of 2026. The *security* consequence is direct: the NMDs' weak state presence gives the TTP space; compensation delays generate grievances; and land/water disputes (Kurram, Khyber, Tirah) find violent expression partly because the courts are not there. The counterfactual is not that the merger failed — it is that it has not yet been completed.

**4. Development–security nexus.** The relationship is not linear but *co-productive and conditional* (Chapter 10). Development produces security only where (a) physical security persists (roads/markets/schools must be protected); (b) the gains reach local populations rather than patronage networks; and (c) justice institutions mediate the disputes that growth creates. Evidence from KP: tourism recovery in Swat shows development's dependence on (and contribution to) security; NMD data show that where security is absent, development does not persist. The practical corollary: design development as security infrastructure (protection + staffing + local accountability), and expect the dividend to lag by years, not quarters.

**5. Deradicalization efficacy.** Weak evidence base, modest scale, and no independent evaluation (Chapter 8). Sabawoon's cohort (226 juveniles, ~143 reintegrated, with documented relapses) is a fraction of the demand; Mishal and Sparlay's claims are contested or unverifiable; and the disappearance of Sparlay illustrates programme fragility. But the *instruments* — family-based rehabilitation, religious counter-narrative, vocational reintegration, and community reacceptance — are plausible and internationally supported; the deficiency is in resourcing, evaluation and legal framework, not concept. Deradicalization is *not* an alternative to counter-insurgency: it is the part of normalization that converts the state's coercion into something durable.

**6. Afghanistan factor.** The causal role is established and central (Chapter 9): the TTP's sanctuaries, leadership, training and financing have operated from Afghanistan since 2021, and the 2025–2026 escalation — Doha ceasefire, Istanbul mechanism, the February 2026 bombings, and Ghazab-lil-Haq — demonstrates both the depth of the problem and the weakness of force alone against it. No comprehensive settlement is realistic in the near term; what is realistic is a *verifiable management regime*: monitored commitments, prisoner exchanges, border regimes that facilitate rather than only restrict, and third-party (Chinese/Qatari/Turkish/Saudi) institutionalized mediation. Sanctuary erosion, not a peace treaty, is the achievable near-term goal.

**7. Governance and rule of law.** The KP record identifies four governance factors associated with violence reduction: *continuity of police leadership and reform*; *judicial functionality* (a working court where the conflict was fought); *local government* (the missing tier in the NMDs); and *fiscal delivery* (the material proof of government's presence). Conversely, the strongest correlates of violence escalation are: policy shocks that criminalize a population (the PTM ban), impunity (the absence of convictions), and the suspension of political channels (protest bans). The legal architecture (ATA, 27th Amendment, military courts) matters not only for human rights but for counter-insurgency effectiveness: where the justice system loses legitimacy, community cooperation — the actual source of intelligence — degrades.

**8. Comparative lessons.** Northern Ireland teaches that police reform is load-bearing; Colombia that reintegration without protection produces dissidence; Mindanao that fiscal devolution plus third-party monitoring sustains settlements; Sierra Leone that hybrid justice (truth + selective accountability) can consolidate peace; Sri Lanka that military victory without political settlement leaves normalization unfinished; the KRI that party-fragmented security entrenches instability; Chechnya that coercion can suppress violence while destroying the state's legal foundation and exporting the conflict; and Afghanistan that rented security forces and corrupted governance collapse when the external patron leaves. The transferable core is: political framework + security-sector reform + economic dividend + legitimacy mechanisms, over a decade, with monitoring (Chapter 13).

**9. Civil society and peace.** KP's civil society, media, jirgas, elders, women's organizations, youth movements and religious leaders are simultaneously the state's most valuable peace resource and its most constrained partners (Chapter 12). The most consequential policy fact in this domain is negative: the proscription of a non-violent political movement (the PTM) and the criminalization of journalists removed the legitimate channels through which Pashtun grievance could be expressed, thereby increasing the recruitment environment. Restoration of lawful political space is the cheapest security intervention available to the state.

**10. Futures.** Three futures are set out (Chapter 14): sustained normalization (15–20 per cent), fragile equilibrium (50–60 per cent), and deterioration (25–30 per cent). The distinguishing variables are fiscal delivery, police protection, and political inclusion; the external variable (Afghanistan) can accelerate any path. The early-warning indicators that would distinguish deterioration from simple continuity — sustained TTP taxation, police attrition, border closure, sectarian massacre, ATC collapse — are observable quarterly, and the state currently tracks almost none of them systematically.

**11. Gender.** Gender dynamics are not marginal but structural to normalization (Chapters 8, 10, 12). Female education is simultaneously the strongest long-run indicator of stability in the NMDs and the target of the insurgency's most ideological violence; women's organizations are among the most effective community-level preventers; and women are largely absent from the jirga and security architecture. Any credible normalization agenda must include girls' schooling, women's participation in mediation, female policing, and women-led PVE programming as security measures — not as social add-ons.

**12. Economic cost and dividend.** KP's annual conflict-related economic loss is estimated at Rs 400–800 billion (1.5–3 per cent of provincial output), with cumulative national losses since 2001 in the range of USD 126.79–152 billion (Chapter 10). The incremental cost of the roadmap — roughly Rs 150–250 billion per year at maturity plus the Rs 568 billion arrears — is thus a fraction of what insecurity already costs. Normalization is not a humanitarian expense; it is the highest-return investment available to the federation, and its financing is almost entirely a matter of reallocation and political will.

## 16.3 Theoretical Contribution: Reframing Normalization

Three theoretical contributions emerge.

**(1) Normalization as institutional co-production.** The literature on counter-insurgency and state-building treats security and governance as sequential (clear → hold → build). KP's evidence suggests they are *co-productive*: security without justice (the merged districts' policing) produces hollow control; justice without security (courts in the absence of protection) is a target; and economic development without either is a subsidy to the conflict economy. The policy implication is to design every intervention with three components — protection, adjudication, and resources — in the same geography and timeline.

**(2) The sanctuary-grievance nexus.** The study argues that the sanctuary variable and the grievance variable are not substitutes but complements: a sanctuary supplies capability, while grievance supplies recruitment and information. Counter-insurgency that degrades capability (strikes, operations) without reducing grievance (political exclusion, compensation failures) simply lowers the insurgency's price of reconstitution: this is the mechanism that explains KP's repeated cycles and the 2021–2026 escalation. Conversely, grievance-reduction without sanctuary pressure (e.g., development programs in Swat while TTP leadership sits in Afghanistan) buys quiet but not durability. Only simultaneous action on both variables produces sustainable stability — a proposition that predicts relapse wherever either is neglected (a testable prediction for the 2026–2030 period).

**(3) The three monopolies as a normalization index.** The model can be operationalized as a measurable index (Chapter 15's monitoring framework): *monopoly of violence* (state-share of coercive incidents; absence of parallel taxation/justice), *monopoly of justice* (case throughput, conviction rates, restitution payments, absence of indefinite detention), and *monopoly of resources* (fiscal transfer fulfilment, per-capita spending in the merged districts, local government budgets). The index's value is that it measures what normalization requires, not merely what violence does — and that it can be computed annually from data the state already possesses but does not publish.

## 16.4 Policy Synthesis: Ten Findings and Six Commitments

**Ten findings of the study.**
1. Violence is a five-year rising trend (2021–2026) with the TTP as the dominant actor and the Afghan sanctuary as the dominant enabling factor.
2. Kinetic operations have degraded but never defeated the insurgency; the decay of gains is institutional, not military.
3. The FATA merger's fiscal failure is the largest self-inflicted wound in the province's security policy.
4. Police are the front line and the weakest-protected link: 122 killed in H1 2026 alone, with a 93.7 per cent year-on-year rise.
5. The justice system's throughput failure sustains impunity, which sustains both militancy and grievance.
6. Deradicalization programmes are small, unevaluated and under-resourced relative to the recruitment pipeline.
7. The Afghanistan channel requires management and verification, not maximalism — and management is achievable.
8. The proscription of non-violent politics and the shrinking civic space are counter-insurgency self-harm.
9. Gender-exclusion and the failure to educate girls are structural security deficits, not social policy questions.
10. The economic cost of inaction exceeds the cost of normalization by a factor of two to four.

**Six commitments (the implementation core).**
1. **Pay the money** — legislate the merged-district transfer, clear arrears, publish district-wise delivery.
2. **Protect the police** — fund the protection package, insurance, compensation, and recruitment now.
3. **Staff the courts** — judicial presence in the merged districts, ATC throughput, witness protection.
4. **Elect the councils** — local government in the NMDs with devolved budgets.
5. **Restore lawful politics** — lift the PTM ban, release those without terrorism charges, protect journalists, and negotiate with verified commitment frameworks where possible.
6. **Verify what is done** — an annual, published, independently scrutinized normalization report.

## 16.5 Limitations of the Study

**Data limitations.** This study relies on open sources, with the attendant risks: militant casualty claims are inflated; official incident counts are uncorroborated; CRSS/SATP/ACLED series use different definitions and counts diverge; and some of the most important data — ATC outcomes, NMD fiscal absorption, police postings, operation civilian-harm assessments, deradicalization outcomes — are simply unpublished. Where the record is contested (the 2026 war's casualties; Mishal's numbers; the Sparlay centre's existence), the study reports the contest rather than resolving it. A primary-research program inside the province (household surveys; police and prosecutor interviews; jail-based recidivism studies) would materially improve on what is presented here and is a direct implication of this study for future work.

**Causal-inference limits.** The dynamics analysed are simultaneously determined (violence affects development affects recruitment affects violence), and the study's methods are structured comparisons and process tracing, not identification. The roadmap's expected effects are therefore argued from mechanism and comparative experience, not from a controlled evaluation; it should be treated as a hypothesis-bearing design whose implementation must itself be evaluated.

**Access and security.** The province's most violent districts cannot be safely researched at scale; militant and intelligence perspectives are, by definition, unavailable in verifiable form; and government cooperation is conditional. The study compensates with source triangulation and disaggregated reporting, but no claim to completeness is made.

**Normative position.** The study takes an explicit position: the purpose of security policy is the protection and flourishing of the population, including those who have been displaced, bereaved, abused, or radicalized. This is a human-security framework, adopted consciously over the state-centric alternatives, and it shapes the weight given to civilian harm, compensation, justice and political rights. Readers who prefer a purely coercive framework will find the analysis's conclusion — that coercion without justice and resources fails — unwelcome; but the evidence, particularly the cyclical record of Chapters 2 and 6, is difficult to read otherwise.

## 16.6 Directions for Future Research

**(1) Primary data collection.** A provincial security observatory with verified incident data; independent casualty monitoring; police and prosecution administrative data; and jail-based recidivism studies. **(2) Fiscal absorption research.** District-level tracking of NMD transfers from award to expenditure — the single largest evidentiary gap in the merger literature. **(3) Deradicalization evaluation.** A properly designed five-year study (with a control/comparison group) of reintegration outcomes, family acceptance and relapse — the field's most pressing knowledge need. **(4) Political economy of violence.** Research on the war economy (extortion, mining, smuggling, kidnap) and its interdependencies with elites, including the capture of the NMD fund. **(5) Sub-national climate-security research.** GLOF/displacement and conflict interactions in Chitral, Dir, Swat and the southern districts. **(6) Comparative follow-up.** A structured comparison of the Pakistan–Afghanistan borderlands with Myanmar's borderlands and the Sahel — three contemporary cases of sanctuary-driven insurgency with weak peripheral fiscal settlement. **(7) Gender-disaggregated conflict research.** The recruitment of women and girls; women's roles in peace networks; and the security economics of girls' education in the merged districts.

## 16.7 Closing Observations

Khyber Pakhtunkhwa presents the paradox of a province that has borne the highest costs of Pakistan's security policies while being granted the least control over them, and that has, despite everything, retained an enormous reservoir of resilience: declining levels of public support for militancy; communities that fought back; police that kept reporting to their posts after 680 attacks in nine months; parents who continued sending their daughters to school; an economy whose tourism sector recovered against all expectation; and a diaspora that sends home both resources and ideas.

The province's security will not be normalized by finding the right operation or the right general. It will be normalized — if at all — by the accumulation of unglamorous, verifiable acts of statehood: paying teachers in Bajaur; staffing a court in Landi Kotal; replacing a constable's damaged vehicle in Lakki Marwat; restoring a compensation payment in Tirah; registering a madrasa without treating its neighbourhood as an enemy; opening a legal route to protest in Waziristan; and publishing, every year, the record of what was promised and what was done.

The study's final judgement is a conditional one. On present trends (Scenario 2, with deterioration risks), KP's future is more of the past: a decade of episodic campaigns, cumulative displacement, a police force bled white, a missed generation, and a province asked to defend the nation while being denied the resources to govern itself. On the alternative path (Scenario 1), the province's three monopolies are built — violence is constrained by professional forces under law, the citizen has enforceable claims in functioning courts and elected councils, and the province receives what the constitution and the federation's own promises commit to it — and KP becomes what it has the potential to be: a gateway economy on the edge of Central Asia, with a young population, an old culture, and a hard-won peace.

The choice between these futures does not rest with the Taliban, or the TTP, or India, or the United States. The binding constraints are domestic: the willingness to fund, to protect, to adjudicate, to include, and to be measured. That is the study's central finding, and its central hope.


# BIBLIOGRAPHY

**Note on citation practice.** In-text citations follow Chicago author–date. Sources are listed by type. News and database sources are cited by outlet and date, consistent with the study's monitoring record (data cut-off 4 October 2026; access date 4 October 2026 for continuously updated sources). The study's mandate forbids invented sources; accordingly, a small number of short-form citations drawn from the project's working monitoring log that could not be matched to a full bibliographic record within the verification window are listed in Part VIII in the exact form recorded, with no embellishment. Militant-group media claims are attributed to the claiming outlet, never to a neutral authority. Contested figures (official, independent, and militant) are attributed rather than reconciled throughout the text.

# RECOMMENDATIONS MATRIX

**How to read this matrix.** Forty-six consolidated recommendations, drawn from Chapters 5–15. Columns: source chapter; phase (I = 0–2 years; II = 2–5 years; III = 5–15 years; C = continuous); lead responsibility; indicative cost where quantifiable (in PKR, annual unless stated); and the primary key performance indicator (KPI). Costs are order-of-magnitude planning figures; the aggregate incremental requirement is Rs 150–250 billion per year at maturity plus the Rs 568 billion arrears (Chapter 15, §15.8).

| # | Recommendation | Source | Phase | Lead responsibility | Indicative cost | KPI |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Legislate the merged-district transfer (3% NFC share or quantified equivalent) with statutory audit | Ch. 5, 15 | I | Federal Finance / NFC / PMO | Within existing pool | Statute passed; annual audited transfer report |
| 2 | Clear Rs 568 bn arrears on a published schedule | Ch. 5, 15 | I | Federal Finance; KP Finance | Rs 568 bn (stock) | Arrears balance; quarterly release compliance |
| 3 | Emergency police protection package (protected mobility, counter-IED, counter-drone, comms) | Ch. 7, 15 | I | KP Police; Interior | Rs 50–80 bn (one-off) + Rs 30–50 bn/yr | Police fatalities and attack survival rate |
| 4 | Fully fund police insurance, compensation and family support; publish annual shuhada report | Ch. 7, 15 | I | KP Police / Home | Rs 5–10 bn/yr | Percentage of entitlements paid within 90 days |
| 5 | Lift the PTM proscription; release detainees held without terrorism charges | Ch. 4, 8, 12 | I | Federal Cabinet; Interior | Negligible | Proscription status; number held without charge |
| 6 | Convert ceasefire with Afghanistan into a verified monitoring mechanism with standing mediation | Ch. 9, 15 | I | MOFA; Security Divisions | Diplomacy costs | Monitoring reports issued quarterly; border incidents |

**Aggregate indicative incremental cost at maturity:** Rs 150–250 billion per year (excluding the Rs 568 billion arrears and the one-off protection package), against an estimated annual conflict loss of Rs 400–800 billion — a ratio of roughly 1:2 to 1:4 in favour of investment (Chapter 15).

**Priority ordering if resources are constrained (the "first six commitments"):** #2 (pay the arrears), #1 (legislate the transfer), #3–#4 (protect and compensate the police), #15 (staff the courts), #24 (elect the councils), #27 (education), #31 (publish the annual report). These are sequential in credibility terms: each makes the next politically survivable.


# APPENDICES A–H AND GLOSSARY

## APPENDIX A — CHRONOLOGY OF SECURITY EVENTS IN KHYBER PAKHTUNKHWA, 1947–2026

| Year | Event |
|---|---|
| 1947 | Partition; NWFP joins Pakistan in a referendum; FATA retained under the Frontier Crimes Regulation (FCR, 1901) and Political Agent system. |
| 1948 | Arrest of Bacha Khan; Khudai Khidmatgar movement suppressed. |
| 1950s–60s | Afghan irredentism (Pashtunistan) shapes border politics; FATA remains outside normal law. |
| 1965, 1971 | Wars with India; limited direct KP security impact. |
| 1979 | Soviet invasion of Afghanistan; Pakistan's covert support programme begins; refugee influx into KP/FATA (peak ~3–4 million in Pakistan). |
| 1980s | Militant training infrastructure in FATA; arms economy; madrasa expansion. |
| 1988–89 | Soviet withdrawal; Afghan civil war; mujahideen factionalization; influx continues. |
| 1994–96 | Rise of the Afghan Taliban; Deobandi madrasa networks in KP/FATA consolidate. |
| 2001 (Sep–Dec) | 9/11; US invasion of Afghanistan; Taliban/al-Qaeda retreat into the borderlands; refugee flow. |
| 2002 | Pakistan military enters FATA (Operation Al Mizan); first TTP precursor networks form. |
| 2004 (Mar) | First Waziristan peace deal (Shakai) with Nek Muhammad; collapses (April–June 2004 offensive). |
| 2005 | Sararogha deal (Feb 2005) with Baitullah Mehsud; Lashkar-e-Islam and other groups expand; militant consolidation in Swat (Maulana Fazlullah's radio). |
| 2006 | Lal Masjid confrontation builds; FATA deals fail; suicide bombing campaign rises. |
| 2007 (Jul) | Lal Masjid operation; militants in Swat/NWFP escalate; TTP formally founded (Dec 2007). |
| 2007 (Nov) | Rah-e-Haq I (Swat). |
| 2008 | Rah-e-Haq II (Swat); Marriott bombing (Islamabad, Sept 2008); suicide attacks across KP. |
| 2009 | Nizam-e-Adl agreement (Swat, Feb 2009); Taliban advance to Buner (Apr 2009); Rah-e-Rast (May); Rah-e-Nijat (South Waziristan, Oct 2009); Baitullah Mehsud killed (Aug 2009). |
| 2010 | Peshawar attacks; APS precursor events; floods (July–Aug 2010) displace millions in KP. |
| 2011 | Kurram sectarian conflict escalation (~2,000 killed across the 2007–2011 period); Salala incident (Nov 2011); Bin Laden raid (Abbottabad, May 2011). |
| 2012 | Bacha Khan Airport (Peshawar) attacks; Fazlullah's Shura network consolidated in Afghanistan. |
| 2013 | Bannu jailbreak (April, ~250 prisoners); Hakimullah Mehsud killed (Nov); APS army school candidate attacks; elections. |
| 2014 (Jan–Jun) | Talks with TTP (Feb–Apr); Tirah offensive; Karachi airport attack (Jun) ends talks. |
| 2014 (Dec) | APS massacre (16 Dec, 132+ children and 10+ staff killed); National Action Plan; moratorium on executions lifted; Zarb-e-Azb (North Waziristan, launched Jun 2014) peaks. |
| 2015 | Zarb-e-Azb consolidation; displacement of ~1 million from North Waziristan; return begins (RDP); Levies/Khasadar reform proposals. |
| 2016 | FATA Reforms Committee report (Sartaj Aziz); Bacha Khan University attack (Jan); final Zarb-e-Azb phase ends. |
| 2017 | Radd-ul-Fasaad launched (Feb); military courts extended; TDP returns complete; NWFP police reforms; FATA merger legislation prepared. |
| 2018 | 25th Amendment and KP Elections (May); FATA merged into KP; province's seats increase; PTM emerges as national movement; elections install PTI government in KP. |
| 2019 | FATA merger implementation begins; Levies/Khasadar integration legislation (Sept); PTM tensions; madrasa registration framework (Ittehad Tanzeemat Madaris). |
| 2020 | COVID; counter-terrorism continuity; ICC/education impacts; merger administrative work continues. |
| 2021 | US withdrawal and Taliban takeover of Kabul (Aug); Tehrik-e-Taliban Pakistan resurges; talks with TTP begin (Oct–Nov). |
| 2022 | TTP ceasefire (May–Nov) collapses (Nov); attacks rise sharply; no-confidence vote removes PM Khan; political crisis begins. |
| 2023 | Peshawar Police Lines attack (30 Jan; 86+ killed); May 9 events and crackdown; deportation of undocumented Afghans begins (Nov); ceasefire with TTP definitively over. |
| 2024 | Elections (Feb); PTI-founded government in KP (Gandapur); Operation Azm-e-Istehkam announced (June 22); PTM banned (6 Oct); Kurram convoy attack (21 Nov; 40–50 killed); total Kurram fatalities 130–133; Zhob and other attacks. |
| 2025 | Kurram accords (14-point agreement, January; *Aman Teega*, May); Afghan repatriation deadline (1 Apr); Tirah displacement begins; Sarbakaf operation (Bajaur); Pakistan's deadliest year since 2015 nationally (3,417 deaths; KP 2,331); border closure (11 Oct); Doha ceasefire; Istanbul mechanism (Nov); 27th Amendment passed (11–13 Nov); Tirah/Umar/other mass-casualty incidents. |
| 2026 | War escalation (21–26 Feb); Operation Ghazab-lil-Haq (26 Feb); ceasefire cycles; GTI ranks Pakistan first globally; Haidar Kandao attack (Oct 2025); TTP Operation Khyber (4 Mar); Lakki Marwat VBIED (13 May); JuA split (3 Jul); Hangu post attack (Jul); Sarbakaf/Orakzai; Islamabad imambargah bombing (Q1); Swat Kabal police-station bombing (2 Aug; 17 killed); Kohat Police Lines attack (18 Sep; 31+ killed); strikes into Afghanistan (Sep–Oct); 1,100+ Afghan civilian casualties; Q3 2026: Balochistan overtakes KP for first time in fatalities; police H1 2026: 122 killed (+93.7%); refugee final-phase repatriation (July). |

## APPENDIX C — MILITANT AND ARMED GROUP PROFILES

**Tehrik-i-Taliban Pakistan (TTP).** Ideology: Deobandi-jihadist, Pakistani-state-focused; umbrella coalition incorporating 70+ factions since 2018. Leadership: Noor Wali Mehsud (emir); shura-based; Federation of 2020. Area: Afghanistan-based leadership; operational networks across KP/NMDs (Bannu, Lakki Marwat, N/S Waziristan, Khyber, Bajaur, Kurram) and occasional Punjab/Sindh operations. Tactics: suicide/IED/VBIED, drones, sniper/ambush, targeted killings, extortion and taxation. Strength: several thousand fighters (estimates vary; the 2024 claim of 22,000–24,000 in Afghanistan and 16,000–18,000 inside Pakistan is the upper bound of political estimates and should be treated as such). Financing: extortion, "taxation," mining, smuggling, kidnapping, donations. Status: proscribed; the group's own annual claims are propaganda and are attributed as such.

**Hafiz Gul Bahadur Group (HGB).** North Waziristan faction with separate command; historically a state-negotiated militia that turned against the state after 2017; operates in North Waziristan and border districts; significant in cross-border dynamics.

**Islamic State–Khorasan Province (ISKP).** Salafi-jihadist; anti-Shia, anti-Sufi, anti-TTP; estimated strength ~3,000 in Afghanistan; targets soft/sectarian targets (imambargahs, Sufi shrines, minorities) and recruits from the borderlands; competition with the TTP (including a March 2026 clash in Orakzai).

**Ittehad-ul-Mujahideen Pakistan (IMP).** New front organization implicated in the Kohat Police Lines attack (September 2026); represents the ongoing proliferation of TTP-aligned fronts.

**Jamaat-ul-Ahrar (JuA).** Factional split from the TTP (announced July 2026), with a history of mass-casualty sectarian attacks; its separation illustrates coalition fragmentation as an operational event and a potential future negotiation partner.

**Lashkar-e-Islam (LI)** and **Ansarul Islam (Mangal Bagh networks)**: historical Khyber/Tirah groups, degraded but occasionally resurgent locally.

**Sectarian organizations (LeJ, SSP variants).** Anti-Shia networks with recurring involvement in Kurram and other sectarian incidents; overlapping personnel with the TTP.

**Baloch insurgent networks (BLA and others).** Distinct theatre (Balochistan) with episodic linkage to KP violence (Q3 2026: Balochistan overtook KP in fatalities). Included for completeness of the threat picture; not a KP-based actor.

*Assessment note.* The proliferation of front organizations is analytically important: it allows the coalition to claim deniability, complicates proscription and negotiation, and enables each faction to pursue divergent operations (including potentially the JuA's sectarian specialization).

See the Statistical Annex (Annex Tables A.1–H.1) preceding this appendix for the consolidated quantitative tables: violence series (national/KP/Balochistan), SATP indicators, police casualty series, merged-district fiscal delivery, socio-economic indicators, displacement and disasters, war chronology and contested claims, and deradicalization outcomes. The statistical annex is cross-referenced by the chapters and represents the study's primary quantitative record.

## APPENDIX E — KEY POLICY DOCUMENTS AND LEGAL INSTRUMENTS

1. **Frontier Crimes Regulation 1901** — the legal architecture of FATA's exception; repealed for the merged districts in 2018.
2. **Nizam-e-Adl Regulation 2009** — sharia-based legal arrangement in Malakand Division; validated and contested.
3. **National Action Plan (NAP), 2014; revised 2021** — 20 points covering militancy, executions, madrasas, financing, refugees, communication, and FATA reform.
4. **National Internal Security Policy 2022–2026 (NISP)** — the framework document for internal security.
5. **Anti-Terrorism Act 1997 (as amended)** — ATCs, proscription, preventive measures; the primary counter-terrorism statute.
6. **NACTA Act 2013** — the coordination authority's mandate (and limits).
7. **Pakistan Protection Ordinance/Act 2013** — preventive detention framework (expired).
8. **KP Police Act 2017** — provincial policing reform statute (role, tenure, accountability, PSCs).
9. **KP Levies and Khasadar legislation, 2019** — integration of tribal forces into the provincial police system.
10. **Constitution (Twenty-Fifth Amendment) Act 2018** — FATA merger and provincial seat adjustment.
11. **Constitution (Twenty-Seventh Amendment) Act 2025** — Federal Constitutional Court, Chief of Defence Forces coordination, and judicial restructuring.
12. **Prevention of Electronic Crimes Act 2016 (as amended 2025)** — cyber-speech offences (repeatedly criticized as over-broad).
13. **Fair Trial Act 2013** — surveillance warrants framework.
14. **National Finance Commission Award framework (including the 3% merged-districts commitment)** — the fiscal instrument central to the merger's delivery.
15. **KP Youth Policy (draft, 2026)** — provincial youth and employment framework.
16. **Madrasa registration framework (2019) and its October 2024 statutory reversal** — the regulatory arc for religious education.
17. **Illegal Foreigners Repatriation Plan (2023–2026)** — the refugee policy instrument.
18. **Paigham-e-Pakistan (2018)** — the state-sponsored religious counter-narrative declaration.
19. **Bangsamoro Organic Law (Philippines, 2018) and Malaysia-facilitated agreements** — comparative reference.
20. **Good Friday Agreement (1998) and Patten Report (1999)** — comparative reference.

## APPENDIX F — GLOSSARY OF KEY TERMS AND ACRONYMS

**APS** — Army Public School, Peshawar (site of the 16 December 2014 massacre).
**ATA** — Anti-Terrorism Act 1997 (as amended).
**ATC** — Anti-Terrorism Court.
**BLA** — Baloch Liberation Army.
**CFT** — Counter-Financing of Terrorism.
**COIN** — Counter-insurgency.
**CTD** — Counter-Terrorism Department (KP).
**DRC** — Dispute Resolution Council (KP's community mediation body).
**FATA** — Federally Administered Tribal Areas (merged into KP in 2018).
**FCR** — Frontier Crimes Regulation 1901.
**GTD** — Global Terrorism Database (START, University of Maryland).
**GTI** — Global Terrorism Index (Institute for Economics and Peace).
**HGB** — Hafiz Gul Bahadur Group.
**IBO** — Intelligence-Based Operation.
**IED / VBIED** — Improvised Explosive Device / Vehicle-Borne IED.
**IFRP** — Illegal Foreigners Repatriation Plan.
**IMP** — Ittehad-ul-Mujahideen Pakistan.
**ISPR** — Inter-Services Public Relations (Pakistan military media wing).
**ISKP** — Islamic State–Khorasan Province.
**Jirga / Maraka** — Pashtun deliberative assembly; local council for dispute resolution.
**JuA** — Jamaat-ul-Ahrar.
**KFR** — "Kidnapping for ransom."
**KP / KPK** — Khyber Pakhtunkhwa.
**LeI** — Lashkar-e-Islam.
**Levies / Khasadar** — tribal paramilitary forces (Khasadar: locally raised tribal force; Levies: Frontier Corps-affiliated paramilitary).
**Malik** — tribal notable/intermediary.
**NACTA** — National Counter Terrorism Authority.
**NAP** — National Action Plan.
**NFC** — National Finance Commission (constitutional revenue-sharing body).
**NISP** — National Internal Security Policy.
**NMDs** — Newly Merged Districts (the seven former FATA agencies/districts).
**Pashtunwali** — Pashtun customary code governing honour, asylum, and dispute settlement.
**PECA** — Prevention of Electronic Crimes Act 2016.
**PIPS** — Pakistan Institute for Peace Studies.
**PTM** — Pashtun Tahafuz Movement.
**PSC** — Public Safety Commission (KP police accountability body).
**R&R** — Rehabilitation and Reintegration (deradicalization).
**SATP** — South Asia Terrorism Portal.
**Sehat Card** — provincial health insurance scheme (a benefit whose extension to the NMDs was litigated).
**TDP** — Temporarily Displaced Person.
**TTP** — Tehrik-i-Taliban Pakistan.
**UNAMA** — United Nations Assistance Mission in Afghanistan.

## APPENDIX H — DISTRICT SECURITY CLASSIFICATION (2026) AND THEMATIC SECURITY MAP (DESCRIBED)

The study uses a four-tier classification (Chapter 3), with confidence levels, and a described map rather than a rendered cartographic product (the study's rendering environment cannot produce a geographically accurate basemap; the classification below is therefore the operative "map" and should be used in its place).

**Tier 1 — Severe, sustained insurgent violence (highest confidence).**
*North Waziristan, South Waziristan (Upper and Lower), Bannu, Lakki Marwat.* Continuous TTP operations; attacks on security forces at high frequency; taxation/parallel-governance reports; the highest police and military casualty rates. These districts form the southern insurgency core. Confidence: high (multiple independent monitors).

**Tier 2 — High and episodic violence (high confidence).**
*Khyber (incl. Tirah), Bajaur, Mohmand, Kurram, Dera Ismail Khan, Tank, Karak, Hangu, Orakzai.* Recurring attacks, displacement episodes (Tirah; Sarbakaf), sectarian flashpoints (Kurram), border-related violence. Confidence: high.

**Tier 3 — Sporadic/urban violence with high-impact events (moderate confidence).**
*Peshawar, Mardan, Charsadda, Nowshera, Swat, Shangla, Malakand/ Dir cluster.* Lower frequency but high-impact urban/soft-target attacks (Kabal, Kohat is Tier 2-adjacent; Peshawar attacks including the 2023 Police Lines bombing). Swat's re-entry to violence in August 2026 is the most significant change in this tier.

**Tier 4 — Low recorded violence but strategic exposure (moderate confidence).**
*Abbottabad, Mansehra, Haripur, Chitral, Upper/Lower Dir and the Hazara districts* — strategic infrastructure, tourism routes, CPEC corridors, and Chinese-national exposure; lower recorded incidents but potential for high-value targeting and for cross-border spillover (Chitral, Dir).

**Note on the map.** A geographically accurate security map would show: (i) the southern cluster (Waziristan–Bannu–Lakki–D.I. Khan–Tank) as the highest-intensity zone, contiguous with the Afghanistan border; (ii) a western border arc (Bajaur–Mohmand–Khyber–Kurram–Orakzai) of medium-high intensity and high displacement; (iii) the urban quadrilateral (Peshawar–Mardan–Charsadda–Nowshera) as the terrorism-target belt; (iv) the northern/northeastern districts as lower intensity but high strategic exposure; and (v) the Afghanistan borderland as the enabling geography for the entire pattern (Chapter 9). This description should be used as the study's district map of record in the absence of a rendered cartographic figure.
